Summary: The cheapest way to get a large refrigerator is to build one from an insulated room, an air conditioner, and a controller. Corcoran estimates $2,000 of rigid insulation, lumber, and sheetrock for a room, adds a $275 A/C unit and a $350 Coolbot PID controller, and arrives at a 12'x12' room that can age several carcasses for around $3,000 total. Source anchors: insulated room; \(2,000; A/C unit (\)275); Coolbot ($350); $3,000.
Analysis: The Coolbot turns a cheap domestic air conditioner into a walk-in-cooler controller, a small piece of Frontier Systems engineering that repurposes consumer hardware into cold-chain infrastructure. The itemized $3,000 estimate makes build-your-own a credible alternative to a \(4,000–\)12,000 commercial unit. It also scales, since the same room can later serve charcuterie curing or a market-garden walk-in.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Aging: Cold Room: Merchandising Refrigerator

Summary: Corcoran describes a farmer friend who bought a used two-door glass-front beverage refrigerator (the kind convenience stores use) for pennies on the dollar and can fit a sheep carcass in it. A comparable new unit lists for about $1,600. He would like one himself, to age charcuterie rather than carcasses, and has been hunting Craigslist for years without finding a deal. Source anchors: merchandising refrigerator; $1,600; sheep carcass; Craigslist.
Analysis: The used glass-front beverage fridge is a real-world example of buying capital below replacement cost, which ties the chapter to Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure. Distinguishing its intended use (aging charcuterie, not hanging sheep) shows that the same cold appliance serves different stages of the pipeline. The Craigslist hunt is a reminder that patience is part of the buying strategy.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Aging: Cold Room: Commercial Walk-In

Summary: A commercial walk-in runs $4,000 to $12,000 and only makes sense if you are starting a business, such as selling vegetables in summer and using the walk-in for your own meat in the offseason — and even then Corcoran leans toward building his own with a Coolbot. He warns that commercial units break and then need professional service at high cost. Source anchors: commercial walk-in; $4,000; $12,000; Coolbot.
Analysis: The professional walk-in is priced both in dollars and in repair dependency, which is the kind of failure-mode honesty this book is built on. Routing the would-be buyer back to the DIY room keeps the cost question a genuine choice rather than a status purchase. The shared-business use case is an early hint at the selling-products chapters later in the volume. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Aging: Holding Meat

Summary: While aging, carcasses must be held off contaminating surfaces with air flow on all sides. Poultry can hang from baling wire or fence wire around the ankles, but goat, sheep, pig, and cow carcasses — 20 to 750 or 900 lbs — need a support (steel beam or gantry crane), a hoist (rope hoist, chain hoist, or electric winch), a connection to the support, and a gambrel. Source anchors: baling wire; 750 lbs; 900 lbs; gantry crane; chain hoist; gambrel.
Analysis: The weight statement (up to 900 lbs for a large cow) is what makes overhead rigging a real engineering topic instead of a convenience. Poultry hanging from fence wire keeps the cheapest end of the spectrum honest, while the four components — support, hoist, connection, gambrel — are the parts list for the entire carcass-handling system. This is the section that later equipment chapters (chains, gantry crane, gambrels) build on. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Aging: Holding Meat: Overhead Support

Summary: Corcoran urges resisting the urge to sink an eyebolt through sheetrock into a rafter, because pull-out resistance is not up to the task. He recommends bridging several rafters with steel to distribute the load, or using a gantry crane (a mobile steel I-beam on two posts with locking casters). He daydreams about adding a dedicated one-car-garage-bay room for hanging meat, butchering, and brewing, with a homemade Coolbot walk-in and a trolley-and-rail system for moving carcasses. Source anchors: eyebolt; sheetrock; steel; gantry crane; trolley and rail.
Analysis: The eyebolt warning is a load-engineering lesson: the visible fastening point is not the failure point, the structure behind it is. Choosing a rolling gantry crane over a permanent beam keeps the rig mobile between the driveway, garage, and butchering table. The trolley-and-rail daydream is one of several places where Corcoran sketches the ideal future facility without pretending it exists yet. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Aging: Holding Meat: Hoist and Connection

Summary: Once you have an overhead support, you need a hoist to raise and lower carcasses and a way to connect the hoist to the support. Corcoran defers to his other sections on hoists and crane connection, keeping this subsection as a pointer rather than repeating the material. Source anchors: hoist; connection; crane.
Analysis: Deliberately cross-referencing rather than repeating keeps the book from bloating while documenting that every piece of the rig is a spelled-out topic somewhere. The connection step, in particular, is deferred to the workshop chapter where chains and shackles are covered in depth, which is an editorial decision that rewards readers who read the whole book. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Aging: Holding Meat: Gambrel

Summary: A gambrel holds the rear ankles of a carcass, spreads them, and provides a loop for an overhead chain or rope; it is used for gutting, transportation, aging, skinning, and splitting. Corcoran owns three 1,200-lb-rated gambrels at $20 each and says they have served him well. Source anchors: gambrel; rear ankles; 1,200 lb; $20.
Analysis: The gambrel is the interface between the hoist hardware and the animal, so its rating (1,200 lbs) must exceed the heaviest carcass it will hold. Buying several at $20 makes the fleet non-binding for a multi-animal butchering season. Its breadth of use — gutting through splitting — explains why it earns a dedicated subsection. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Aging: Conclusion

Summary: The aging-equipment conclusion is a priced kit list: a cold room at $0 or a refrigerator at \(500–\)12,000, overhead steel at $20 or a gantry crane at $350–700, a chain at $30, an optional hoist at $40–200, and a gambrel at $20, for a total range of $110 to $12,950. Corcoran's own solid setup (gantry crane, chain hoists, chains, gambrels) cost \(919. Source anchors: cold room (\)0); gantry crane (\(350-700); chain (\)30); hoist (\(40-200); gambrel (\)20); $919.
Analysis: The range from $110 to $12,950 is the whole make-or-buy argument compressed into two lines, and the author's actual $919 spend anchors the sensible middle. Pricing each component separately lets the reader trim or upgrade any single piece. This is the book's house style — a conclusion that is not a summary but a checkout line. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Butchering

Summary: The butchering equipment list covers a work surface, a skinning knife, butcher knives, a cleaver, a hammer, a knife sharpener, a scraper/chopper, and a butcher's saw or reciprocating saw, with optional additions like a sharpening steel, slice-proof glove, nitrile gloves, a knife storage box, a skinning tool, a bone dust scraper, scales, meat tubs, and a meat hook. Source anchors: work surface; skinning knife; cimeter; cleaver; butcher's saw; nitrile gloves.
Analysis: Grouping the tools by mandatory versus optional is the key decision structure: a homesteader can start on plywood over sawhorses with three knives and still butcher well. The presence of both a knife sharpener and a sharpening steel shows that edge maintenance is part of the tool set, not a hobby on the side. Optional tools like scales and meat tubs are flagged as labor-saving rather than essential. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Butchering: Work Surface

Summary: A work surface can be as simple as plywood on sawhorses (which Corcoran has done) or as elaborate as a 72"x30" stainless steel table (\(253) with a matching cutting board (\)245), or a solid wood bench top like the Grizzly 96"x30" maple top (\(300). Corcoran reports that a good cutting board is worth the money because it is easy to wipe clean, which keeps the carcass clean. Source anchors: plywood; sawhorses; stainless steel table (\)253); cutting board (\(245); Grizzly maple bench top (\)300).
Analysis: The range from plywood to stainless steel is another explicit cost ladder, keeping the entry point open-ended. The cleanliness argument links the work surface directly to food safety, which is the real reason to upgrade. Choosing wood over stainless is presented as a taste-plus-hygiene trade, not a dogma. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Butchering: Knives

Summary: Corcoran argues that high-end chef's knives are the wrong tool for butchery: they demand careful sharpening under schedule pressure, are too stiff, and have the wrong profile and thickness. Serviceable butchering knives are affordable, and he finds that three tools handle 90% of thousands of pounds of carcass: a skinning knife (\(10), a 5" flexible boning knife (\)18), and a 10" cimeter (\(60), with a 6" curved and a 6" wide boning knife nice to have. He notes boning knives come in flexible, semi-flexible, and rigid steel, and that he prefers flexible. Source anchors: skinning knife (\)10); boning knife (\(18); cimeter (\)60); flexible; rigid.
Analysis: The case against chef's knives is really a case for purpose-built tools with replaceable edges, which keeps the butchering station cheap and repairable. Naming a \(10/\)18/$60 three-knife kit makes a complete working set startlingly affordable. The flexibility note is the kind of nuance only experience produces, and it saves readers from buying the wrong stiffness. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Butchering: Cleaver and Hammer

Summary: A cleaver-and-hammer pair is very useful for the price: a cleaver at $24 and a dead-blow hammer at \(10. If needed, a hacksaw or reciprocating saw can do the same jobs, so the pair is optional. Source anchors: cleaver (\)24); dead blow hammer ($10).
Analysis: Pricing the pair at $34 makes it an easy addition that accelerates splitting and chopping tasks. The substitution note (hacksaw or reciprocating saw) again keeps the tool set from becoming a barrier. Cleaver-and-hammer is one of the few areas where a cheap tool genuinely excels. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Butchering: Knife Sharpener

Summary: Corcoran contrasts his obsessive chef's-knife sharpening (Lansky kit, 20-degree bevel, microbevel, leather strop) with the reality of butchering hundreds of pounds of meat, where there is no time for such nonsense. He recommends a multi-slot electric sharpener he originally laughed at: the Chef's Choice 300 (\(55) or the Trizor XV (\)145). He also explains the butcher's steel (\(15), which realigns a merely bent edge without removing material, and can cut sharpening frequency by 50% or more. Source anchors: electric sharpener; Chef's Choice 300 (\)55); butcher's steel ($15).
Analysis: The contrast between precision chef sharpening and production butchery is a genuine insight: butchering runs on speed and consistency, not ritual. The butcher's steel is pedagogically important because it separates two failure modes of an edge — lost material versus a bent-over blade — that beginners conflate. Recommending an electric sharpener despite his own refinement speaks to the book's honesty about trade-offs. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Butchering: Butcher's Saw

Summary: Ninety-nine percent of butchering is knives; the rare bone-cutting jobs need a butcher's hacksaw (bone saw) at $22, or a faster DeWalt reciprocating saw at $89 with a $16 wood blade, which can bisect a large hog's spine in about 30 seconds. Professional breaking saws (Wellsaw-style) run \(900–\)1,800 and are unnecessary for a homesteader butchering a few animals a year. Source anchors: bone saw (\(22); reciprocating saw (\)89); wood blade; 30 seconds; breaking saw.
Analysis: The 99%/1% split tells the reader exactly where to spend money, and the reciprocating saw is a tool most homesteaders already own, making it the hidden value pick. The 30-second spine-bisect claim is the kind of concrete benchmark that makes technique learnable. Pro breaking saws are priced so the reader can see exactly what they are declining. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Butchering: Gloves

Summary: Corcoran always wears nitrile gloves when butchering, both to keep fat off his hands and because hands stay warmer in a cold garage; 5 mil gloves (\(8 per 100) are adequate 99% of the time, with 10 mil (\)13 per 50) for tougher operations, kept handy in two $13 Harbor Freight magnetic dispensers. He also covers slice-proof gloves on the non-dominant hand (under the nitrile), but reports he has ended up leaving his in the drawer because of dexterity concerns. Source anchors: nitrile gloves; 5 mil; 10 mil; slice-proof glove; magnetic dispensers.
Analysis: Glove thickness is a genuine economic-and-comfort trade most guides skip, and the 5 mil versus 10 mil split maps to specific tasks. The slice-proof glove debate is honest reporting: fear of tendon cuts is real, but dexterity loss may increase accidents, so Corcoran reports his own resolution rather than lecturing. The magnetic dispensers are a small logistics detail for one-handed access during butchering. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Butchering: Knife Storage Box

Summary: Corcoran prefers rigid storage for knives and settles on a Victorinox knife storage case (\(34) large enough for his butchering knives, cleaver, sharpening steel, and pastry scraper. Source anchors: Victorinox; knife storage case (\)34); rigid.
Analysis: A rigid case is a protection scheme for capital, keeping edges separated and tools in reach. The functional point stands: storage affects blade life and workflow. The $34 price makes proper storage a rounding error relative to the knives it protects. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Butchering: Miscellaneous Tools

Summary: Miscellaneous butchering tools include a $3 bone dust scraper (which removes bone chips, dirt, and stray hairs from cuts), a $10 meat hook (which Corcoran often forged himself and uses to hold cuts open), a $25 skinning tool for pigs (vise grips can substitute), a $6 dough scraper for cleaning boards, and \(3 stainless S-hooks for hanging sausages and pepperoni to cure. Source anchors: bone dust scraper (\)3); meat hook (\(10); skinning tool (\)25); dough scraper (\(6); S-hook (\)3).
Analysis: The bone dust scraper is the sort of cheap tool that prevents a subtle eating defect (grit in the bite), which is exactly the level of craft the chapter teaches. Several of these — meat hook, dough scraper — are tools already present in many homes, so the cost of entry is near zero. The S-hooks connect butchering forward to the charcuterie-curing pipeline. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Butchering: Scales

Summary: Corcoran identifies two scale types useful when butchering: hanging (crane) scales, placed between the gambrel and the supporting chain, and platform scales that sit on a work surface. Each variant is detailed in the subsections that follow. Source anchors: hanging scales (crane scales); platform scales.
Analysis: The two-type split frames scales as an optional record-keeping layer rather than mandatory hardware, and each variant answers a different weighing problem: whole carcasses suspended overhead versus meat portions weighed bench-side. Knowing which scale serves which task is exactly the kind of tool-matching the equipment chapter teaches. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Butchering: Scales: Hanging Scales

Summary: A hanging (crane) scale is placed between the gambrel and the supporting chain and tells you how much the entire carcass weighs, which is useful for record keeping; scales vary in the maximum weight they can measure. Corcoran owns an 800 lb crane scale (\(80), and notes alternatives including a 1,000 lb crane scale (\)330) and a 2,000 lb crane scale (\(360). A hanging scale is nice to have but not necessary. To measure several carcasses with one scale requires hanging carcass and gambrel from the scale, lifting them off, removing the scale, and re-hanging — and since a pig carcass can weigh 400 lbs or more and a beef carcass 800 or more, this cannot be done by hand. He manages it with a spare chain hoist (two carcasses on two gambrels on two chain hoists, with a third spare chain hoist between them), and concludes that unless there is some complicated Towers-of-Hanoi algorithm he has not figured out, for n carcasses the method requires n+1 hoists. Source anchors: hanging scale (crane scale); 800 lb crane scale (\)80); 1,000 lb crane scale (\(330); 2,000 lb crane scale (\)360); n+1 hoists.
Analysis: The hanging scale turns carcass weights into a permanent record, which is what later lets the book quote pork yield percentages and economics. Capacity ratings matter because the scale must exceed the heaviest animal it will weigh, and the n+1-hoist procedure is a rare moment where the manual shows an improvised mechanical trick in detail, revealing the real logistics of moving a 400–800 lb carcass between weighing and hanging stations. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Butchering: Scales: Platform Scales

Summary: Platform scales sit on a work surface and measure the weight of meat placed atop them; like hanging scales, they vary in the maximum weight they can measure. Corcoran owns a platform scale (\(70) and calls it fun to have — it lets you say things like 45 lbs of pork chops out of one pig — and, more usefully, lets him weigh pork bellies to mix the right amount of bacon cure, or weigh scrap meat headed for the grinder so as to measure the right amount of sausage spices; it is not necessary. Source anchors: platform scale; platform scale (\)70); pork bellies; bacon cure; sausage spices.
Analysis: The platform scale is the bench-side bookkeeping link between butchering and the downstream recipes, since the bacon-cure and sausage-spice arithmetic both depend on exact belly and trim weights. Its fun-plus-functional grading explains where the manual draws the line between useful tools and optional gear. The weights it produces also feed the trim-recycling pipeline of the sausage and charcuterie processes. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Butchering: Meat Tubs

Summary: Meat tubs give cuts a secure, organized place, support a two-person division of labor (one butchers and fills, another packages), and double as curing vessels for bacon bellies. They come in 5-inch (\(8) and 7-inch (\)10) depths with $5 lids; Corcoran has about ten tubs with lids and prices his whole set near \(130, which he calls well worth it. Source anchors: meat tubs; 5" deep (\)8); 7" deep ($10); lids; division of labor.
Analysis: Meat tubs are an organizational technology: they convert carcass sections into named, movable units that let two people work in parallel, a real throughput gain in the same space. Their reuse as bacon-curing tubs cross-links the butchering and charcuterie stations. Sizing the fleet (ten tubs break a sheep; a hog wants more) is exactly the kind of capacity math the book prizes. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Packaging

Summary: Unless you will eat an entire sheep or pig in a week, you must freeze most of it. Corcoran explains freezer burn as two air-mediated problems — osmotic water loss and oxidation — solvable by excluding air, and reports storing cuts three years and finding them as high quality as fresh. He compares conventional butcher wrapping (wax paper plus craft paper, from $23 freezer-paper rolls and \(22–50 dispensers) with consumer and pro vacuum sealers, expressing doubt that wrapped-only storage matches a pro-grade vacuum seal. Source anchors: freezer burn; osmosis; oxidation; freezer paper (\)23); vacuum sealer.
Analysis: Explaining freezer burn as air exposure rather than time is the single most useful paragraph in the packaging chapter, since it rewrites the common belief that food goes bad in freezers slowly. The three-year storage claim becomes the standard the rest of the section tries to hit. Presenting butcher wrapping as a legitimate but dubious alternative sets up the vacuum-sealer sections that follow. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Charcuterie

Summary: Charcuterie embraces unsmoked bacon, smoked bacon, fresh sausages, cured sausages, smoked sausages, and forcemeats, so not every project needs every tool. The full tool list is meat tubs, a grinder, a mixer, a smoker, and a sausage stuffing tool. Source anchors: bacon; sausage; forcemeats; grinder; mixer; smoker; stuffer.
Analysis: The breadth of charcuterie (six product families) explains why the equipment chapter treats it as a separate station: it is a whole post-butchering pipeline. The caveat that not every product needs every tool keeps beginners from overbuying. Naming the five tool categories sets up the five sections that follow. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Charcuterie: Grinder

Summary: Grinding exists to recycle the 10–15% of hanging weight that ends up as trim scraps. Corcoran compares hand grinders (\(25), a KitchenAid attachment (\)50, fine for a few pounds), benchtop grinders ($100 to \(1,770; he owns a 1.5 hp unit), and floor-standing machines (\)7,220), and is blunt that hand grinding does not scale to a 600 lb hog. A benchtop grinder with a mixer attachment is the right hobbyist ceiling. Source anchors: scraps; 10%; hand grinder (\(25); KitchenAid; benchtop grinder; floor standing (\)7,220).
Analysis: Anchoring grinding to what you do with trim is the honest motivation: grinding exists because carving creates scrap. The KitchenAid-versus-benchtop dividing line is set by batch size, not enthusiasm. The recommendation to grind coarse first, then fine, recurs later in the sausage process section. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Charcuterie: Mixer

Summary: Making sausage means mixing ground meat with spices and binder, and kitchen bowls are too small for pig-scale batches. Benchtop grinders can typically disconnect from their motor and accept a paddle-auger meat mixer costing \(150–\)300; Corcoran owns a seven-gallon mixer (\(300) and thinks well of it. Source anchors: seven gallon mixer (\)300); paddle auger; spice.
Analysis: The mixer is the piece that makes sausage-making scale beyond a KitchenAid bowl, and its $150–300 price fits the benchtop grinder tier. The seven-gallon capacity is a binding constraint the process section returns to (he mixes 25 lbs at a time in a mixer rated for 50). Buying the grinder and mixer from the same platform keeps the workshop coherent. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Charcuterie: Sausage Stuffing Tool

Summary: To make cased sausage you need a stuffing tool. Options include lever-action stuffers (\(40, inadequate for real volumes), the KitchenAid stuffing attachment (\)30, which Corcoran bought, returned, and says you should never buy), vertical gear-driven hand-cranked stuffers in 5, 7, and 11 lb capacities ($126, $150, $162), pro stainless models up to $1,089, and an industrial floor unit at \(4,900. Corcoran owns the 11 lb model and has stuffed hundreds of pounds with it, praising its steel gears, silicone piston ring, and one-way check valve. Source anchors: lever action stuffer (\)40); KitchenAid attachment (\(30); 11 lb vertical stuffer (\)162); 30 lb pro stuffer ($1,089).
Analysis: The rare flat-out don't-buy-it verdict on the KitchenAid attachment makes this section unusually decisive and saves readers a known bad purchase. The vertical gear-driven stuffer is the sweet spot and is described in mechanical detail (rack-and-pinion, splined shaft, check valve) because that is the class of tool worth owning. Pricing the industrial units establishes where home-scale ends. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Charcuterie: Smoker

Summary: Smoking began as preservation — smoke dehydrates the surface and coats it with chemicals inimical to bacteria — and now people smoke largely for flavor, with Corcoran noting humans plausibly evolved a taste for smoke. He distinguishes cold smokers (chambers separated by a long tube), hot smokers (vertical, same housing), and BBQ smokers (horizontal, designed to cook as they smoke). Source anchors: preservation; dehydration; cold smoker; hot smoker; BBQ smoker.
Analysis: The preservation origin explains the technology's logic (surface drying plus antibacterial coating), which is what makes the smoker a food-storage tool and not just an appliance. The three-way typology is the reader's first mental map of the section. Corcoran's own use — hot smoker for bacon, cold smoker for salmon — ties each type to a real project. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Charcuterie: Smoker: Types

Summary: All smokers have a heat-and-woodchip chamber and a separate meat chamber. A cold smoker separates the chambers with a long tube so smoke cools before hitting delicate foods like cheese and fish; a hot smoker stacks them vertically in one housing (bacon); a BBQ smoker arranges them horizontally and fully cooks while smoking (brisket). Corcoran made his own cold smoker and purchased a hot smoker, noting the chamber names are not a rigid standard. Source anchors: cold smoker; hot smoker; BBQ smoker; cheese; bacon; brisket.
Analysis: The type of smoker is determined by the target food's need for heat, which gives the reader a decision rule rather than a shopping list. Classifying by geometry (separated tube, vertical, horizontal) is a teachable way to identify an unfamiliar smoker. The confession that his cold smoker was built for lack of money and his hot smoker bought when time was short is an honest make-or-buy data point. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Charcuterie: Smoker: Cold Smokers

Summary: Cold smokers are rare on the market, so odds are you will build one. Corcoran built his from a steel garbage can with a hot plate (\(17) as the smoke generator, an 18"-deep office cabinet as the smoke chamber, 4" duct components, and two restaurant shelves, connected by duct, with a rain-cap vent; he says the whole thing assembles in about 15 minutes and produced his best smoked salmon ever. He notes Alton Brown famously built one from a four-drawer file cabinet. Source anchors: garbage can; hot plate (\)17); 4" duct; office cabinet; rain cap; Alton Brown.
Analysis: The cold-smoker build is a model example of Frontier Systems fabrication: a roughly $50 scavenged assembly beats a manufactured unit nobody sells. The detailed build (tin-snips holes, start collars, duct, rain cap) makes it reproducible, and the 15-minute claim lowers the perceived barrier. Naming Alton Brown's file-cabinet version proves the design space is forgiving.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Charcuterie: Smoker: Hot Smokers

Summary: Hot smokers are widely sold across brands and price points, from a low-tech Masterbuilt at \(314 to models with Bluetooth, smartphone apps, and pellet augers that Corcoran deliberately avoided. Most have a water tray that cools and humidifies the smoke, and he recommends building one from a chassis and heat source if you want, or even building a shed as a smoker in places like rural Alaska. He reports smoking hundreds of pounds of bacon, country ham, and pork chops with the Masterbuilt. Source anchors: Masterbuilt (\)314); water tray; Bluetooth; wood pellets; shed smoker.
Analysis: The Masterbuilt pick is a deliberate anti-gadget choice — the four-thousand-year-old praxis essay later in the book is the intellectual justification for the low-tech smoker. The water tray is the key mechanism to understand, since it lets more smoke be applied without drying the meat. The shed-as-smoker note reconnects the tool to its oldest form as a preservation building. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Charcuterie: Smoker: BBQ Smokers

Summary: BBQ smokers are horizontal hot smokers designed to cook while smoking, also called offset, horizontal, or reverse-flow smokers. Almost all are built into trailers (e.g., the RK 250 at \(4,065). Corcoran owns neither a BBQ smoker nor the book Franklin Barbecue, though he cites it, and quotes a brisket-making friend who recommends Meathead (\)23) and the amazingribs.com website for smoker types from kamado to pellet smokers. Source anchors: offset smoker; reverse flow; RK 250 ($4,065); Franklin Barbecue; Meathead.
Analysis: Grouping the trailer-style smoker under one section gives the reader the vocabulary (offset, reverse-flow) they will meet elsewhere. Corcoran's confession of owning none is a useful honesty marker — some topics are pointers, not personal experience. The friend's quote supplies the practical bridge to resources this volume does not cover. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Charcuterie: Smoker: Wood Chips

Summary: Smoking wood chips are dry, thin (1/4 inch or less) hardwood shavings — thicker material is wood chunks — sold as four-packs (\(23) and in stores in species like apple, mesquite, hickory, oak, and cherry. Resinous softwoods like pine, cedar, redwood, and spruce are inappropriate for health and flavor reasons. Corcoran makes his own chips with a hand plane (\)40) or power plane ($172), preferring thick shavings to dense sawdust, and warns that woodchipper chips may be too large or too moist (moldy) unless dried. His bacon runs consume about 80 cubic inches of chips per batch, roughly one 180-cubic-inch bag every two runs. Source anchors: wood chips; 1/4 inch; apple; mesquite; hickory; pine; plane.
Analysis: The chip-versus-chunk distinction and the species list (plus the softwood warning) are the actual domain knowledge of smoking, reduced to a short, checkable list. Making your own chips is an independence move consistent with the book's ethos, and the plane-shaving technique connects the smoker to workshop tools. The per-batch chip math turns a vague consumable into a budget line. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Equipment: Summary and Checklist

Summary: The equipment chapter closes with a priced checklist across three spending tiers. For large-animal processing, bare minimum is $221, very comfortable is $1,700, and top-of-the-line is $2,800; adding charcuterie equipment adds $123 / $1,542 / $9,209; adding fowl-processing gear adds $48 / $2,800 / $3,065. A complete fowl, large-animal, and charcuterie setup therefore runs $392 minimum, $6,042 comfortable, or $15,074 best-of-the-best. Source anchors: $221; $1,700; $2,800; $392 minimum; $6,042; \(15,074. Analysis: Three-tier pricing turns the entire equipment chapter into a single decision frame, which is exactly the book's arithmetic habit applied at book scale. The dramatic spread (\)392 to \(15,074) lets a reader locate their own budget immediately. These numbers also make the earlier outsourcing price (~\)600/pig) comparable dollar-for-dollar against home processing. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering

Summary: Slaughtering is defined as humanely killing an animal, and Corcoran states plainly that control over the quality of animals' lives and deaths is one of the reasons he moved to a farm. The section then dives into method, beginning with fowl. Source anchors: humanely; quality of life; quality of death.
Analysis: The framing sentence — I moved to a farm to control the quality of my animals' lives and the quality of their deaths — makes slaughter a chosen responsibility rather than a grisly chore, which colors the entire how-to. Treating the death as a designed process is what lets the manual spend page after page on technique. This is the moral thesis the emotional-impact sections later trade on. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Fowl

Summary: As in equipment, there are two ways to slaughter a chicken, turkey, or goose: hatchet and stump, or killing cone and knife. Corcoran leads with the hatchet method, which he uses personally. Source anchors: hatchet and stump; killing cone and knife.
Analysis: Keeping the two methods paired with their equipment section lets the reader reread the tool rationale right before the procedure. Personal endorsement of the hatchet method is a signal that the step-by-step that follows is field-tested. The species range means the technique has to scale across bird size. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Fowl: Hatchet Method

Summary: Corcoran gives a complete, almost choreographed method: lock the dogs in the house, inspect the chopping stump, set a Rubbermaid bin nearby, bury the hatchet corner in the stump for one-handed reach, have a helper present birds legs-first, hold the bird's ankles with an index finger cushioned between the legs, position the neck on the stump, aim as high on the neck as possible but below the head, swing with force sufficient to cut through into the log, take a second swing if a strip of skin remains, then hold the carcass for about 30 seconds so the heart pumps out the blood. Death is immediate, and the wings flap instinctively. He reflects that the first kill put a pall over a full day and part of the next, that practice brings desensitization, and that he is not sure that desensitization is a good thing — but he reframes the act as owning it compared with the field mice and chickens killed by commercial agriculture. Source anchors: chopping stump; index finger; neck below the head; 30 seconds; desensitization; factory farms.
Analysis: The exacting procedure is the book at its most pedagogical: grip, stance, swing arc, and follow-through are all specified because precision makes the kill humane. The explicit grief-logic — this is a real taking of life, desensitization is ambiguous, and commercial agriculture outsources far worse death — is the moral argument for doing one's own killing well. Comparing a homestead bird's life to a factory-farm bird's life is the ethical payoff that justifies the whole endeavor. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Fowl: Killing Cone Method

Summary: Corcoran has never used the killing cone method but describes it: secure the cone to a post, place the bird head-down so its head projects from the bottom, and either cut entirely through the neck or slice the throat to sever the artery and let it bleed out. He notes feathers are keratin and should not be cut, and that the sever-the-artery variant is the sort of thing best learned from an experienced hand. Source anchors: killing cone; head down; sever the artery; keratin.
Analysis: Reporting a method he has not personally used, and labeling it as such, is an honesty convention that protects the reader from parroted advice. The keratin warning prevents a rookie from ruining a knife on feathers. Explicitly deferring the artery-slicing technique to hands-on mentorship is one of the few times the manual admits a skill cannot be fully learned from a book. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Fowl: Scalding

Summary: Scalding heats the feather follicles so feathers loosen without tearing skin, done in a vat of hot water from a cast-iron bathtub to a turkey fryer to a thermostatically controlled Featherman scalder. Chickens want about 145–150°F; water must be preheated (Corcoran's Featherman takes about two hours on a fall day), agitation and a bit of dish soap help water penetrate water-repellent feathers, and the hardiest wing-tip feathers are the readiness test — if they pull free after 30–120 seconds, the bird is ready. He recommends an LED extension cord to verify the scalder circuit is live, a full propane tank with an extra, and covering the tub with foam to hold heat. Source anchors: 145-150°F; wing tip feathers; 30-120 seconds; dish soap; LED extension cord.
Analysis: Scalding is the temperature-and-chemistry lesson that separates easy plucking from torn skin, and the wing-tip test gives an unambiguous go/no-go signal. The assembly-line description makes the chore a team workflow, and the troubleshooting list (power, propane, pilot light, insulation) is the failure-mode table that anchors the procedure. The observation that later birds pluck harder is framed as an open experimental question. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Fowl: Plucking

Summary: Plucking can be done by hand — suspending the scalded bird feet-up and grabbing handfuls of feathers, re-dunking if the follicles cool — or by machine, which Corcoran says works decently but not perfectly, leaving a final 5–10% manual pass and sometimes a stuck bird to reposition. He cites lack of good lighting and poor ergonomics as the two enablers of the chore, and dreams of a garage addition with a scalder-plucker-restaurant-table assembly line. Source anchors: evisceration shackles; fence wire; re-dunk; plucker; lighting; ergonomics.
Analysis: The machine-versus-hand comparison is honest about throughput: even the expensive plucker needs supervision and a finishing pass. Naming lighting and ergonomics as the real bottlenecks is a systems-thinking insight often missing from tool catalogs. The one-bay assembly line sketch (scalder, plucker, stainless table with sink) is the recurring ideal-room daydream made concrete. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals

Summary: Slaughtering large animals is described as both physically fraught and emotionally impactful; Corcoran urges the reader to do it but to know it is not lightweight. The chapter then builds a preparation checklist that treats the slaughter as igniting a multi-day fuse. Source anchors: physically fraught; emotionally impactful.
Analysis: The warning is the honest framing for everything that follows: this is not a cosmetic procedure, and pretending otherwise would corrupt the instructions. Treating slaughter as the start of a multi-day project (aging, butchering, charcuterie, stock, canning) is what the next section formalizes. The emotional-impact subsection later explains exactly why the warning is there. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Preparation and Planning

Summary: Slaughtering a large animal starts a multi-day, multi-week project, so the entire process must be scheduled: dispatch, exsanguinate, gut, skin, hang 3–14 days in a cold room, butcher, make sausage, cure bacon, smoke, make stock and soup, pressure can, then slice and package. Corcoran's checklist runs from tools to cold weather, butcher tools and time, charcuterie gear and consumables, packaging tools, and a chest freezer big enough to hold the result, plus contingency plans for warm snaps (snow in the chest cavity), schedule slips, and self-injury (he once broke a rib slipping on ice mid-season and finished butchering two pigs anyway). Source anchors: 3-14 days; 1-2 week process; chest freezer; snow; broken rib.
Analysis: The dynamite-fuse analogy and the full checklist make the planning burden explicit, because the failure mode is running out of time, cold, or freezer space halfway through. The snow-in-the-cavity trick and the broken-rib story are the two remembered details that make the section vivid and practical. Adding self-injury to the contingency list is characteristic honesty about physical risk. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Dispatch

Summary: Dispatch works by stunning the animal — a high-energy impact to the skull over the brain — and then, while it is insensate, slicing an artery so the heart empties blood from the body; lack of oxygenated blood causes brain death about a minute later. Source anchors: stunning; skull; artery; brain death.
Analysis: Splitting dispatch into render-insensate and bleed-out is the procedural core, and it explains why stunning caliber matters (it must reliably knock out) and why exsanguination matters (the heart must keep pumping). The minute-long window frames the urgency of the choreography. Everything else in the large-animal method hangs on these two acts being done correctly in sequence. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Dispatch: Stunning

Summary: Stunning uses a captive bolt stunner or a firearm. Corcoran has dispatched about 30 large animals (three goats, a dozen sheep, 15+ pigs) with two requiring follow-up shots, which he still finds distressing. One failure came from inadequate caliber (.22 failing on a pig, solved with a .30-30); another from a pig moving at the trigger break — his math notes a pig brain is about 100 grams, roughly 6 cm across, so a 1.2-inch twitch defeats a perfect aim. His process includes a pre-slaughter walkthrough, hearing protection, a backup .30-30, checking the backstop and bystanders, and aiming at the intersection of imaginary eye-to-opposite-ear lines. He switched to .40 S&W (about 600 J, versus .22 LR at about 178 J). Source anchors: 30 large animals; follow-up shot; .22; .40 S&W; brain size; walkthrough.
Analysis: The two failure stories are the pedagogy: caliber insufficiency and shot placement are the two ways a stun goes wrong, and each has a fix. The brain-size math (a 6 cm target that a 1.2-inch flinch defeats) turns the aim into understandable geometry. The quoted energy figures give readers a decision rule for dispatch caliber, and the physical walkthrough ritual is exactly the kind of redundant preparation this book teaches. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Dispatch: Exsanguination

Summary: After the shot the animal is insensate, not dead, so the butcher must move efficiently to exsanguinate it before it regains consciousness: safely lay down the firearm (Corcoran keeps it loaded), glove up, roll the animal onto its side, approach from the dorsal side, and cut. The method differs by species. Source anchors: insensate; exsanguinate; dorsal approach; loaded firearm.
Analysis: Keeping the firearm loaded during exsanguination is a carefully explained safety trade (a second shot may be needed immediately). Approaching from the spine side and rolling onto one side are positioning details that make the cut safe from both the knife and the animal's kicks. Species differences are deferred to the next subsections. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Dispatch: Exsanguination: Sheep and Goats

Summary: On sheep and goats, grab the head, bend it back to expose the throat, and slice across the throat with a rapid, firm motion, cutting inches deep; there is no disadvantage to cutting an inch too deep, but not deep enough is a problem. Blood should flow immediately; if it does not, pull the head back further or cut again, more forcefully. The animal will kick reflexively — even a 60–80 lb animal leaves a big bruise — and can rotate 90–180 degrees while thrashing, so restrain the top front leg or step clear. Source anchors: throat; deeply; kick; 60 or 80 lb; rotation.
Analysis: The cut-an-inch-too-deep-rather-than-not-enough guidance is the classic engineering bias toward the safe failure — a deeper cut risks almost nothing while a shallow one risks a conscious animal. The kick and rotation warnings foreground the physical hazards of a thrashing body, which beginners underestimate. Species-splitting the anatomy keeps the directions precise. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Dispatch: Exsanguination: Pigs

Summary: Pigs hide their carotid artery beneath thick meat and fat, so throat-slicing does not work; instead you stick the pig — locate the top of the sternum with your fingers, place the knife tip just beyond it with the edge facing the pig's head, plunge the knife in 10–12 inches until it hits the spine, then pivot the handle toward the rear legs so the blade sweeps through the arteries using the sternum as a fulcrum. If collecting blood, hold a bowl under the wound and empty it into a 5-gallon bucket with vinegar to prevent coagulation. Source anchors: stick; sternum; spine; aorta; fulcrum; vinegar.
Analysis: The sternum-fulcrum plunge is the kind of specific, unrecoverable-from-a-blur technique the book most wants to teach precisely, down to knife orientation and depth. The no-harm-in-too-much-force rule matches the throat-slicing logic for the species where anatomy demands it. The blood-collection bowl plus vinegar reads as a direct handoff to the black pudding recipe later in the volume. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Dispatch: Emotional Impact

Summary: Corcoran answers a Hacker News question about cognitive dissonance in killing his children: they are not his children — my wife is my wife, my dogs are my dogs, pigs are livestock, chickens are walking, squawking vegetables — though the first year the wife named the goats, and slaughter was not fun. He describes a dissociative event elbow-deep in a still-warm creature four minutes dead, concludes that killing and eating animals is fundamental to who we are (the hand shapes the tool and the tool shapes the hand), and that the experience strengthened his commitment to raise animals ethically and use every bit of them. He calls factory farming a perversion of something good. Source anchors: cognitive dissonance; named goats; dissociative event; using every bit.
Analysis: This is the book's moral center: the emotional reality is named and given room rather than suppressed into technique. Framing factory farming as a perversion of a good practice gives the whole enterprise a positive ethics — the homesteader restores dignity to animal life and death. The dissociation and the admission that desensitization is ambiguous keep the manual from slipping into macho. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Gambrels and Lifting

Summary: After exsanguination, verify brain death by touching the cornea — no reaction means dead — then make an incision behind each rear ankle between the bone and the Achilles tendon (hamstring), preserving the tendon because it supports the carcass during aging, slide a gambrel through the slits, and use a tractor with a welded chain hook to lift the carcass pelvis-high for gutting. Corcoran includes a sidebar on the Achilles-tendon etymology and warns that quick links lose 75%+ of their strength if not closed. Source anchors: cornea; Achilles tendon; hamstring; gambrel; quick link; chain hook.
Analysis: Preserving the hamstring is the kind of irreversible detail (cut it and you cannot hang the carcass) that warrants the careful instruction. The safety sidebar on quick links is a real load-engineering warning tied to a vivid failure picture. The farm trivia about the tendon's name shows the digestible side of the manual's style. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Gutting

Summary: Gutting removes viscera (lungs, stomach, intestines) to discard inedible and contaminated bits, increase surface area, and speed cooling. The steps are opening the abdominal cavity, removing the viscera, and removing the lungs, using a skinning knife, boning knife, optional twine, a waste bin, and a pan for desirable viscera. Corcoran emphasizes avoiding contamination from outside (feces and dirt) to inside, while conceding a bit of cross-contamination is unlikely to kill anyone. Source anchors: viscera; lungs; stomach; contamination; cooling.
Analysis: Framing gutting as speed-cooling (removing a warm mass and adding surface area) gives the step its real purpose, connecting it to decomposition and aging. The contamination discipline (inside presumed clean, outside presumed dirty) is the food-safety spine of the whole procedure. Conceding that perfect technique is unattainable keeps the standard aspirational rather than paralyzing. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Gutting: Opening the Abdominal Cavity

Summary: Open the cavity by pinching skin high on the belly between the rear legs, making a slit through skin only — never so deep it strikes viscera and releases urine or feces — then unzip along the stomach with the hook on the back of the skinning knife. This is one of the two moments the knife must touch contaminated skin. The viscera stay attached top and bottom (anus and trachea) until later. Source anchors: pinched skin; slit; unzip; hook; contaminated skin.
Analysis: The cut-no-deeper-than-necessary rule protects against puncturing the bladder or gut, and the unzip technique turns a risky cut into controlled tearing. Flagging this as one of only two contaminated-skin moments gives the reader a contamination map of the whole procedure. Understanding the two attachment points (anus and trachea) is what makes the subsequent pulling sequence sensible. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Gutting: Abdominal Viscera

Summary: Removing the abdominal viscera follows four steps: optionally tie off the rear end of the intestines, cut around the anus from outside, sever the front end of the intestinal tract (the esophagus), and roll the viscera into a bin. Corcoran describes the awkward ergonomics of cutting the anus free, the risk of piercing intestine or fingers, and the technique of reaching over the ribs to find and cut the esophagus with exaggeratedly slow, small slices. Source anchors: tie off; anus; esophagus; roll out; slow slices.
Analysis: The abdominal-viscera sequence is where the manual's procedural patience peaks, with the anus cut and the esophagus sever treated as the two danger points (contamination and self-injury). The go-slowly instruction is repeated because the working position, hands buried under viscera, makes vision impossible. The four-step list gives a beginner a checklist they can follow by feel. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Gutting: The Edible Bits

Summary: Corcoran reviews the viscera for eating: the intestines are theoretically usable for sausage but so hard to clean that everyone tosses them and buys commercial casings; the heart is almost always eaten, the liver commonly, the kidney sometimes, and the spleen rarely (he quotes a vivid Atlantic description of spleen). He names the heart as the most solid organ, the liver as a purple-brown multi-lobe organ near the gallbladder, and the kidney as kidney-bean-shaped. Source anchors: heart; liver; kidney; spleen; gallbladder; casings.
Analysis: The edible-bits survey gives the reader a salvage map before the cavity is cleaned, so organs do not get thrown out by default. The gallbladder warning (nasty black-green bile) is a recurring motif that reappears in organ processing and recipes. The spleen aside is a rare moment of gustatory journalism, defending the practical decision to skip it. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Gutting: The Lungs

Summary: After the abdominal viscera come out, the lungs remain behind the diaphragm, a wall of thin muscle and membrane. Cut through the diaphragm with the tip of a boning knife (once punctured it tears by hand), then repeat the reach-and-cut pattern — this time severing the trachea instead of the esophagus — and scoop out the lungs. Source anchors: diaphragm; trachea; lungs; reach and cut.
Analysis: The diaphragm is the anatomical boundary dividing the cavity into two work zones, and recognizing it is what tells the reader the first stage is done. Reusing the same blinded reach-and-cut technique for the trachea shows the procedure has a repeating motif rather than a novel step each time. Congratulations — the carcass is now gutted — is the manual rewarding completion. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Gutting: The Head

Summary: Corcoran removes the head at gutting time — partly because he cooks pig heads immediately and wants them fresh, and because splitting is easier without the head — by finishing the belly cut to the neck, cutting around the neck, then separating vertebrae by manipulation, a reciprocating saw, or twisting the head (his usual method). He rinses the cavity with buckets of water to wash out blood and drop it out of the 40–140°F danger zone. Source anchors: remove the head; reciprocating saw; twisting; danger zone; rinse.
Analysis: The case for early head removal (pork-head recipes need no aging, and halving is easier) makes a real scheduling decision rather than a habit. The twisting technique is blunt but effective, and the kneeling-in-blood aside acknowledges the antisocial ergonomics. The danger-zone rinse ties gutting straight back to food-safety temperature logic. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Scalding and Scraping

Summary: Sheep, goats, and beef get skinned, but pork offers a choice of skin-on or skinless. Leaving skin on a pig requires scalding (dunking in very hot water and scraping hair loose with special tools), a process Corcoran has never performed and, citing Wittgenstein, declines to describe. He skins all his carcasses after transfer to the aging area. Source anchors: scalding; scraping; skin on; skin off.
Analysis: The Wittgenstein citation is the manual's most elegant way of refusing to fake expertise on a technique the author has not done. Skinning-all versus scalding is presented as a genuine regional and preference choice rather than a right answer. It also sets up the large skinning subsection later in the chapter. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Transfer To Aging Area

Summary: Transfer to the cold room is the last and easiest step of slaughtering. Corcoran describes positioning the gantry crane near the garage door and, for small animals (a 120 lb hanging-weight ram), carrying the carcass on one shoulder from the tractor; for large animals (a 400 lb hanging-weight hog), lowering it onto painter's plastic and dragging it. He notes cranes can walk sideways if the load is off-center, and that skins come off more easily while warm. Source anchors: gantry crane; painter's plastic; 120 lb ram; 400 lb hog; drag.
Analysis: The two transfer methods are scaled to the two real weights, so the reader can pick the carry or the drag without guesswork. The sideways-walking crane warning is a real equipment behavior most novices discover the hard way. Mentioning that skinning is easier warm lets the reader choose to do it now or later. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Cleanup

Summary: Post-slaughter cleanup means collecting tools, bagging and freezing the good viscera, moving the blood bucket, returning firearms to the house, and hauling unwanted viscera to the compost pile or to the chickens, which Corcoran calls little omnivorous dinosaur descendants. Source anchors: viscera; firearms; compost pile; chickens.
Analysis: Cleanup is treated as part of the procedure because blood, tools, and firearms left scattered are both a safety and a hygiene problem. The compost-plus-chicken destination for waste viscera closes a loop back to the soil chapter and the homestead's nutrient cycle. The dinosaurs quip keeps the tone humane. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Refrigerate Blood

Summary: Collected blood is refrigerated for up to a few days until black pudding day, stored in 1-quart wide-mouth mason jars using the canning kit's wide-mouth funnel. Source anchors: blood; refrigerate; 1 quart mason jars; wide-mouth funnel.
Analysis: Refrigerating blood in canning jars connects the slaughter station to the pantry's canning tools, a small example of one kit serving two pipelines. The few-day window bounds how quickly the black pudding recipe must run. This is the kind of handoff detail that makes the book's chapters interlock. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Process Liver

Summary: The liver is processed by cutting apart its lobes, carefully cutting off the green gallbladder and discarding it (err away from it rather than risk spilling bile), trimming veins and fibrous material, then either freezing it or cubing it and soaking in milk for a day or two to draw off strong flavors before cooking as pâté, liver and onions, or liverwurst. Source anchors: lobes; gallbladder; milk; pâté; liverwurst.
Analysis: The gallbladder warning is repeated because the failure (bile over organ meat) is unrecoverable and truly awful-tasting. Milk-soaking is the classic flavor-mellowing trick that makes a challenging organ cut usable. Listing liverwurst as a destination ties this processing step to the recipe chapter and the whole trim-recycling pipeline. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Process Heart

Summary: The heart is processed by cutting away tough white arterial material, opening it like a bell pepper, removing non-muscle material, rinsing to dislodge blood clots, and refrigerating — optionally under milk — before cooking or adding to the grind. Source anchors: arterial material; bell pepper; blood clots; grind.
Analysis: Treating the heart as a muscle like any other steak is the conceptual reframe the recipe chapter completes (heart steak). The bell-pepper opening technique makes the organ manageable, and the two destinies (cook or grind) keep it out of the waste bucket. Refrigerating under milk extends the liver trick to the heart. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Process Head

Summary: Sheep and goat heads are too small to bother processing and are thrown to dogs or chickens, but cow and pig heads have worthwhile meat: brain, tongue, cheeks, and even ears. Corcoran processes pig heads shortly after hanging because the head work can be done indoors at the kitchen counter after the cold outdoor work. Source anchors: brain; tongue; cheeks; ears; kitchen counter.
Analysis: The small-animal-versus-large-animal split is an honest labor-and-yield judgment: some heads are not worth the work. Moving head work to the kitchen counter is a morale and ergonomics choice that also sets up the specialty recipes (head cheese, guanciale, pig ears). Naming the edible parts of the head foreshadows the use-every-bit ethic. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Process Head: Cow

Summary: On the one cow he has processed, Corcoran butchered out the tongue and the cheeks: cut the tongue out at the base and reserve it for the grind, and peel back the skin to expose the cheek muscle (about 8 oz or less per side) for slicing close to the bone. He notes beef cheeks are rare cuts. Source anchors: tongue; cheeks; 8 oz; grind.
Analysis: The cow-head yield (tongue plus two small cheeks) shows why even a large animal's head is a niche payoff, and the grind destination prevents waste. The cheek-butchering description is precise enough to follow on a real head. This section demonstrates the use-every-part principle scaled to beef. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Process Head: Pig

Summary: Pig heads offer more: ears, tongue, cheeks/jowls, and brain, approached three ways from easiest to hardest — make the whole head into stock, do the 80/20 of ears-to-dogs plus boned-out jowls (mature guanciale, basically bacon) plus stock or compost for the rest, or make head cheese (a tasty cold terrine). Head cheese is the labor-intensive option that uses every bit for higher value than stock. Source anchors: ears; tongue; jowls; brain; guanciale; head cheese.
Analysis: The three-tier approach (stock, 80/20 guanciale, head cheese) is Corcoran's honest ranking of effort against reward, and the 80/20 route is his recommended default. Calling jowls processed like bacon guanciale, which is basically just bacon, demystifies a prestige cured meat. Head cheese gets name-checked as the despite-the-name-really-tasty option the recipe chapter delivers. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Process Head: Pig: Ears

Summary: To remove pig ears, hold an ear and cut through at the base while the head's weight keeps it taut; then either toss the ears to the dogs or cook them, with the recipe chapter providing a braised ear dish. Source anchors: ears; base; dogs; cook.
Analysis: The taut-cutting tip prevents an awkward slice, and the two destinations (dogs or a braised dish) mirror the book's flexible-waste principle — the ears are food for someone. Cross-referencing the recipe chapter turns this processing step into a grocery handoff. This is the smallest, quickest of the head parts, kept lean. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Process Head: Pig: Tongue

Summary: The pig tongue is removed by opening the mouth with a wooden spoon, cutting the frenulum as far back as possible, then cutting the tongue free at the back of the mouth. It can go into the grind pile for sausage or be cooked separately. Source anchors: frenulum; back of mouth; grind pile.
Analysis: The spoon-and-cut technique is a small mechanical tip that makes an awkward cut feasible, and offering both destinations keeps the tongue valuable. This is another demonstration of the nothing-wasted principle extended to parts most cooks never see. The recipe chapter's pork section lets the reader decide its fate. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Process Head: Pig: Jowls

Summary: Pig cheeks connect under the jaw into one U-shaped piece called the jowls. Corcoran's preferred use is guanciale, curing them like bacon so he can piggyback on an existing process. He describes skinning the jowl area down from each eye, separating the flesh from the bone with a thin boning knife, ending with a piece a foot or more wide, and inspecting for salivary glands to remove (whitish-yellow bundles like grains of rice). Source anchors: jowls; guanciale; skinning; bone; salivary glands.
Analysis: Choosing guanciale because it piggybacks on a process already in use is classic Corcoran economics — the marginal cost of another cured product drops once the bacon pipeline exists. The salivary-gland inspection is a fastidious detail that distinguishes good charcuterie. The description of the finished jowl piece tells the reader what success looks like. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Skinning

Summary: Skinning is the process of removing skin from the meat; skins removed cleanly can be tanned into leather, as Corcoran's wife has done with sheep hides. The technique uses a skinning knife with a hook on its spine: make circumferential cuts below the gambrel tendons, cuts down to the anus, connect to the belly cut, remove the tail, cut around the front ankles, and reduce the skin to a single flat sheet that can be peeled. Sheep and goat skin pulls loose in large sheets (fisting works); pig skin is more firmly bonded and needs mostly knife work, so he slices it into vertical strips. Skins go to dogs or compost. Source anchors: skinning knife; hook; gambrel; fisting; pig skin; tanning.
Analysis: The reduce-the-topology-to-a-flat-sheet framing is the manual's best explainer — once the cuts are made, mechanics replace surgery. The sheep-versus-pig difference (loose versus bonded) is a species fact that changes how much knife work to expect. Mentioning hide tanning extends the use-every-bit ethic to the skin itself. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Slaughtering: Large Animals: Splitting

Summary: Splitting cuts the carcass down the middle of the spine to advance breakdown into cuts and increase surface area for cooling. Corcoran works from the belly side, uses the saw (or reciprocating saw) to cut the pelvis and then the spine with the saw foot riding against the spine, controls the natural tendency of the halves to swing apart, watches for the blade to veer toward softer cartilage, and finishes by cutting the sternum. Source anchors: pelvis; spine; belly side; sternum; veer.
Analysis: Cutting from the belly side is the key navigational insight (you can see the spine), and the blade-veering warning is a real-world correction beginners need. The observation that the carcass separates itself turns the swing into an assistant rather than an obstacle. Finishing through the sternum makes the split complete in a defined order. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Aging: The Problem

Summary: Aging exists because rigor mortis — muscle fibers tightening after death as glycogen fuel runs out — plus cold shortening (below about 59°F, calcium release in cells contracts muscles) leaves freshly butchered meat tough and chewy unless long-cooked. Source anchors: rigor mortis; cold shortening; 59°F; tough.
Analysis: Naming both rigor mortis and cold shortening explains why simply cutting quickly does not give tender meat and why temperature control matters. The braise-and-pulled-pork aside is the manual honestly noting the cooking-based escape hatch. Identifying the chemical mechanism is what lets the next section propose the fix. The limits at work here are a live exercise in Ecological Constraint.

Meat Processing: Aging: The Solution

Summary: Aging fixes rigor mortis by holding meat between slaughter and butchering in a cool, humid environment: muscle cells continue consuming oxygen and producing lactic acid, which stays and slightly breaks down muscle, countering the stiffness. Dehydration and possible surface mold also alter flavor, usually for the better. Source anchors: lactic acid; humid; breakdown; mold.
Analysis: The lactic-acid mechanism turns aging from a vague tradition into a chemistry lesson with a known outcome. Dehydration concentrating flavor and mold adding beefier notes gives the reader a vocabulary for why aged meat tastes different. This sets the standard the How-To and My Experience sections meet. The limits at work here are a live exercise in Ecological Constraint.

Meat Processing: Aging: Types of Aging

Summary: Corcoran defines three types: plain aging (1–2 weeks hanging), dry aging (4–6 additional weeks on halves or primals), and wet aging (slaughter-to-plastic-sealed-refrigerated, motivated by economics since no weight is lost to evaporation, so vendors can sell more weight from a given carcass and need less warehouse space). He notes the boundary between aging and dry aging is fuzzy, and that warm-climate homesteaders often skip to immediate butchering. Source anchors: aging; dry aging; wet aging; evaporation; weight loss.
Analysis: Distinguishing dry aging (flavor development on open surfaces) from wet aging (economics-driven sealed storage) lets the reader understand restaurant marketing terms. The cost rationale for wet aging is a rare window into why industry does what it does. The fuzzy-boundary admission is characteristic intellectual honesty. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Meat Processing: Aging: How To

Summary: Aging itself is simple: after gutting, skinning, and splitting, leave the carcass in a cool (below 40°F), clean, pest-free area for 7–21 days, ideally about 80% relative humidity. Most homesteaders, including Corcoran, use a garage and achieve temperature by scheduling slaughter in the right season; a garage's thermal mass keeps meat in the 32–40°F zone, and warm snaps are handled by stuffing snow or ice bags into the chest cavity. Source anchors: below 40°F; 7-21 days; 80% humidity; garage; snow.
Analysis: The temperature target, time window, and humidity ideal condense aging into three numbers a reader can hit approximately. Thermal mass (of garage and carcass) is the mechanism that lets a non-climate-controlled space work, and the snow-in-chest trick is the emergency backup that saved his seasons. The I-don't-have-humidity-control-and-it's-fine caveat is reassuring and realistic. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem. The limits at work here are a live exercise in Ecological Constraint.

Meat Processing: Aging: My Experience

Summary: Corcoran ages his meat and considers it best practice, having only dipped a toe into dry aging. The common recommendation is 7–10 days for pork, which he followed for years, then tried 2–3 weeks after reading testimonials and found the longer aging gave better flavor — without a crisp A/B test. Source anchors: 7-10 days; 2 or 3 weeks; testimonials; better flavor.
Analysis: The admission that his longer-aging conclusion rests on testimonials, not a controlled test, is consistent with the book's evidentiary honesty elsewhere. It also models an experimental habit for the reader: keep changing one variable per season. The falling into dry aging by simply extending the window shows the fuzzy boundary in practice. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Meat Processing: Butchering

Summary: Butchering is defined as breaking a whole carcass down into primals, subprimals, and individual cuts, with the goal of useful sizes and consistent cooking qualities so a given cut is all-tough or all-tender, never a mix. Every familiar meat name — T-bone, ribeye, pork shoulder, ham — is a cut. Source anchors: primals; subprimals; cuts; T-bone; ribeye.
Analysis: Defining butchering as cut-quality engineering (consistent cooking behavior per cut) sets the standard for the whole chapter, so the reader cuts by muscle group and cooking method, not by historical habit. Anchoring to familiar retail names makes the abstract carcass map immediately navigable. This is the production step where the aging takes place and the freezer fills. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Butchering: Cut Definitions

Summary: Cut definitions are slippery for two reasons: regional naming variations and overlapping cut lines, since a carcass can be divided in several valid ways. Corcoran notes that NAMI (North American Meat Institute) created the IMPS (Institutional Meat Purchase Specifications), which enumerates every cut with a unique code — for example, beef brisket is IMPS 118 and beef plate is 122. Source anchors: regional names; overlap; NAMI; IMPS; brisket 118; plate 122.
Analysis: Introducing IMPS gives the reader a canonical reference for cuts, converting butchery nomenclature from folk knowledge into structured code. The acknowledgment that overlaps are real reduces the anxiety of picking a division scheme. This is the institutionalization move the book loves — turning a craft into catalogued knowledge. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Butchering: From Gambrel to Bench

Summary: The first butchering step is moving meat from the gambrel to the bench. On pork and sheep a half can be carried by one person, but removing one half from a shared gambrel can seesaw the other side and drop hundreds of pounds, so Corcoran secures the far side with a second chain hoist first. A 100 lb half can be shouldered, a 200 lb half can be staggered a few steps, but he recommends a wheeled lift table under the crane. Beef halves are too large to move intact, so quarters are cut off at the bench, between the last and second-to-last rib, ideally as a one-person job with a lift table. Source anchors: gambrel; seesaw; chain hoist; lift table; beef quarters.
Analysis: The seesaw failure and the n+1-hoist choreography are the kind of mechanical hazards that only experience exposes, and the lift-table recommendation turns heavy lifting into positioning work. The beef-quarter procedure (an average quarter weighs about 200 lbs) shows why beef needs a different plan than pork or sheep. This section is really about logistics: moving weight safely between stations. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Butchering: Pork

Summary: Pork butchering is the book's worked example, from the author's first self-taught pig (two days per pig, three pigs, quite a long week) to a practiced three-hour pig after several years. Source anchors: two days; three hours; three pigs.
Analysis: The two-days-to-three-hours arc is a realistic skill curve with no false starts suppressed — the book happily reports the beginner's slowness. It also sets expectations for readers who will butcher their first animal slowly. The improvement story is the manual's argument that practice, not talent, produces speed. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Butchering: Pork: Time Required

Summary: After several years Corcoran can butcher a pig from hanging halves to labeled meat tubs in about three hours, still much slower than a professional processing dozens per day, but in line with other homesteaders' reported times. Source anchors: three hours; professional; homesteader.
Analysis: Quoting his own time and matching it against professionals and peers calibrates the reader's expectations and prevents discouragement. Treating three hours as achievable, rather than a floor, motivates the practice routine. The modest framing also justifies the outsourcing comparison pricing earlier. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Meat Processing: Butchering: Pork: Meat Yield

Summary: Pork yield math: hanging weight is about 70% of live weight, and cut weight is about 70% of hanging weight, or roughly 50% of live weight. That cut weight breaks into sections — shoulder 20%, middle 36%, ham 36%, other 7% — and, in more detail, Boston butt 10%, picnic 10%, belly/bacon 16%, ribs 4%, chops and fat 16%, loin 16%, hams 20%, trim 5%, and jowl 2%. Source anchors: 70% hanging weight; 50% cut weight; Boston butt 10%; belly 16%; hams 20%.
Analysis: The 70%-of-70%-equals-50% chain is the core conversion of the entire meat chapter, because it lets the reader predict freezer output from a pig's live weight. Breaking sections into named primals turns the yield chart into a shopping list of future meals. These percentages are the kind of legibility table the whole book is built on. This converts a messy domain into the measurable, actionable quantities that define Legibility.

Meat Processing: Butchering: Pork: Sections

Summary: Each half is broken into three sections — shoulder (front third), middle, and ham (rear third) — by cutting straight lines: identify the anatomical landmarks (e.g., between the 4th and 5th rib), tick-mark the cut line, cut soft tissue with a knife, switch to a butcher's saw for bone, then separate. Corcoran recommends batching the same section across multiple carcasses to minimize context shifting and learn faster. Source anchors: shoulder; middle; ham; 4th and 5th rib; tick marks; batching.
Analysis: The rib-geometry instructions (4th and 5th rib for the shoulder line, last two vertebrae for the ham line) make the cut lines discoverable by touch. Batching identical sections across several pigs is a productivity insight that turns repetition into deliberate practice.
