Household: Kitchen: Tools: Vacuum Sealer: Chamber Style: Economics

Summary: Chamber machines use standard bags rather than costly channeled ones: 8x12-inch 3-mil bags cost $0.27 each at 100 count and $0.05 each in 1,000-count bulk. A two-person household eating one pound of meat per person per day uses about 365 2-lb bags a year, so a 1,000-count order lasts under three years. The $999 machine's payback against consumer machines is roughly 6,875 bags, or about seven years, plus the value of marinating and other uses. Source anchors: 3 mil; $0.27; \(0.05; 365 bags; 7 years. Analysis: The bulk-bag economics (\)0.05 versus $0.27) make a 1,000-count order an obvious win, and the yearly bag count gives the demand side. Household: Kitchen: Tools: Vacuum Sealer: Usage Hints

Summary: Two usage hints: freeze soft or delicate items overnight before sealing so pressure won't crush them, then pierce the bag before defrosting so thawing contents aren't crushed; and when sealing liquids, remove the plastic chamber spacers so the bag sits low and watch for outgassing froth, hitting finish to abort if it boils over. Source anchors: freeze first; pierce bag; spacers; outgassing; finish button.
Analysis: The freeze-first-then-seal sequencing is the solution to the 1,410-lb collapse problem for soft foods, and the pierce-before-defrost rule is its mirror image. Sealing liquids is taught as an operator-watch process with an abort button, exactly the hands-on detail recipes omit. These hints make the expensive machine actually usable across food types. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Tools: Freeze-Dryer

Summary: Freeze-drying freezes food then sublimates the ice directly to vapor under vacuum, preserving texture and rehydrating far better than dehydration, with the result vacuum sealed and stored at room temperature. The only home-market machine is the Harvest Right, from $2,700, processing 5, 10, or 15 lbs per batch over 20–36 hours, drawing about 10 amps and costing \(2–4 per batch (\)0.20–0.40/lb). Since a chest freezer preserves meat at about \(0.10/lb/year, the crossover where freeze-drying is cheaper is roughly 2–4 years of storage; Corcoran has minimal interest because he freezes rather than backpacks. Source anchors: sublimation; Harvest Right (\)2,700); 20-36 hours; crossover.
Analysis: The mechanism and the honest cost crossover (2–4 years) are the two things a reader needs to decide against the machine, which Corcoran himself does. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Baking: Bread

Summary: Corcoran bakes Mark Bittman's no-knead bread: 430 grams flour, 390 ml water, 1/4 teaspoon instant yeast, salt, and cornmeal, mixed into a shaggy dough, rested 12–18 hours, shaped, risen about two hours, baked 30 minutes covered and 22 minutes uncovered in a 450°F Dutch oven. He notes the dough can proof in a dehydrator. Source anchors: 430 grams flour; 12-18 hours; 450°F Dutch oven; no-knead.
Analysis: The no-knead loaf is the ideal homestead bread because it trades proofing time for kneading labor, matching the farm's schedule. The Dutch oven replicates a professional hearth at home, and the dehydrator-proofing aside reuses an earlier tool. Precise grams show that baking, unlike the meat recipes, rewards exact measurement. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Baking: Pie Crust

Summary: This crust recipe makes two open-pie bottoms or one closed pie: 1.5 cups flour, 12 tablespoons frozen lard, 4 tablespoons cold butter, optional sugar, a pinch of salt, and an ice-water trickle pulsed in a food processor until the fats are shredded and the dough binds. Corcoran rolls it 1/4 inch thick and cuts it against an inverted pie pan with the corner of a baker's scraper. Source anchors: frozen lard; cold butter; ice water; food processor; 1/4 inch.
Analysis: Starting with frozen lard ties the crust to the rendered-lard recipe in the pork chapter, making the pie a use for a byproduct. The food-processor pulse method and ice-water constraint are the chemistry details that produce flake. The inverted-pan cutting trick is the kind of small professional move that speeds assembly. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Baking: Apple Pie

Summary: Apple pie uses one batch of crust, 6 large apples, white sugar, cornstarch, cinnamon, nutmeg, lemon juice, and vanilla, peeled and sliced with the apple peeler, layered into the crust, topped, milk-brushed, and baked 10 minutes at 450°F then 35–45 minutes at 350°F. Source anchors: 6 large apples; apple peeler; cornstarch; 450°F.
Analysis: The recipe is a direct consumer of the orchard and the apple peeler, closing the farm-to-table loop for the apple harvest. The cornstarch and lemon juice are the chemistry that keeps the filling from running. This is the template the apple-sauce and apple-butter recipes extend. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Baking: Tortillas

Summary: A 24-tortilla batch from 930 g flour, 3 tablespoons baking powder, salt, 2 tablespoons melted lard, and 2.5 cups milk, mixed, rested, pressed on a tortilla press ($23), and cooked 30 seconds per side on cast iron griddles at 350–400°F. They freeze well and serve tacos, chimichangas, fajitas, and enchiladas. Source anchors: 930 g flour; lard; tortilla press; griddle; 350-400°F.
Analysis: The tortilla recipe feeds a whole family of Mexican dishes and uses home-rendered lard, tying baking to the pork pipeline. The assembly line (press, first griddle, second griddle, plate) is the book's production thinking applied to bread. The bubble-popping tip and the record that tortillas freeze carefully connect to pantry storage. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Baking: Lasagna Noodles

Summary: Homemade pasta, based on a Fine Cooking recipe, uses 2.5 cups flour and 4 eggs, kneaded in a stand mixer (or the traditional mound-and-well), rolled through a pasta machine until 1/16 inch thick, cut to baking-dish length, boiled 30 seconds, and shocked in ice water before assembly. Source anchors: 4 eggs; stand mixer; pasta machine; 1/16 inch; 30 seconds.
Analysis: Fresh pasta is the from-scratch companion to the pork lasagna recipe, and the 30-second boil with ice-water shock is the timing lesson. The initial fold-and-turn kneading sequence (a dozen passes before thinning) is the technique that develops gluten. A very large stock pot matters here, since it lets all the noodles cook at once. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Eggs

Summary: Corcoran debunks the refrigeration myth: intact eggs with the bloom (cuticle) intact store safely at room temperature, while US factory eggs must be washed (removing the bloom) and then refrigerated. He stores his eggs on the counter in a wooden bowl he turned on his lathe, and teaches one-handed cracking and palm yolk separation since chickens mean a steady surplus. Source anchors: bloom; cuticle; room temperature; palm separation; one-handed crack.
Analysis: The bloom explanation matters because it tells egg owners their storage rule depends on whether the egg was washed. The counter-top wooden bowl is the visual of self-sufficiency in action. Treating egg handling as a skill matches the volume's emphasis on practiced competence. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Eggs: Quiche

Summary: This two-quiche recipe uses a batch of pie crust, a pound-plus of bacon or sausage, onions, garlic, frozen spinach, 12–18 eggs, half-and-half, and a pound of shredded cheese, layered into two crusts and baked at 350°F for 45 minutes until a chopstick comes out damp but not wet. Source anchors: pie crust; bacon; 12-18 eggs; 350°F; chopstick.
Analysis: The quiche is the master dish for an egg surplus plus meat and cheese from the homestead, and the loose egg count explicitly accommodates variable egg sizes. The chopstick doneness test is the book's signature checkable endpoint. This recipe shows how seasonal gluts get absorbed into a single casserole. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Eggs: Egg Salad Sandwich

Summary: Egg salad uses 12 soft-boiled eggs, minced celery, mayonnaise, mustard, salt, pepper, and lettuce on toast; Corcoran experiments with sous-vide-poached eggs in a sprayed bag at 165°F for an hour to skip peeling. It is his go-to for spring and fall egg gluts. Source anchors: 12 eggs; soft boil; mayonnaise; sous vide; keto.
Analysis: The recipe exists to solve the seasonal egg surplus, and the sous-vide-in-a-bag experiment models the book's incremental technique testing. Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Eggs: Maple Crème Brûlée

Summary: A maple crème brûlée from 2 cups heavy cream, half a vanilla bean, an egg plus 3 yolks, and 7 tablespoons maple syrup, baked in ramekins in a water bath at 325°F for 40 minutes, with optional brown-sugar caramelizing that Corcoran prefers to skip because the hard shell fights the spoon. Source anchors: vanilla bean; maple syrup; ramekins; water bath; 325°F.
Analysis: This dessert connects the egg yolk surplus, the maple operation, and the crème brûlée technique, making it a farm-pride dish. The water bath and 40-minute bake are the precision the egg chapter permits. Preferring to skip the brûlée shell is a taste judgment that keeps the recipe honest. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Eggs: Mayonnaise

Summary: Mayonnaise is a simple emulsion of egg yolks and vegetable oil with mustard; Corcoran makes it in a food processor, drizzling oil in very slowly, and warns that over-blending breaks the emulsion, with a rescue method of re-emulsifying the failed batch into a fresh yolk base. Fresh mayo without preservatives lasts only about a week. Source anchors: emulsion; yolks; mustard; drizzle; broken.
Analysis: Mayonnaise is the classic emulsion the freezer section warned about, and making it fresh explains why the freezer cannot store it. The slow-oil-drizzle and the broken-emulsion rescue are the two skills that make it reproducible. The one-week fridge life sets honest storage expectations. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Eggs: Cured Egg Yolks

Summary: When eggs are truly surplus, yolks can be salt-cured: lay yolks in dimples in a thick kosher-salt bed, cover, refrigerate for several days to two weeks, and the cured yolks grate or slice over salads, pasta, and vegetables. Source anchors: kosher salt; yolks; refrigerate; grate.
Analysis: Salt-curing is a preservation sink for the egg glut that produces a gourmet product, exactly the book's value-added philosophy. The two-week envelope is the patience trade for a long-shelf ingredient. The whites' fate (pigs, stock rafts, or discard) keeps the whole egg accounted for. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Summary: Corcoran's wife's Cajun eggs stack layers, from the bottom up: cornmeal fritters, bacon or ham, fried tomato, eggs, sauce, and shredded cheese. The tomato-Cajun sauce simmers while the cornmeal patties fry in lard, then eggs are steam-cooked under a lid with a spoonful of water. Source anchors: corn fritters; bacon; tomato; eggs; sauce; cheese.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Watermelon

Summary: Pickled watermelon rinds, based on a John Besh recipe: peeled rind cut into 1-inch cubes, boiled 15 minutes with sugar, vinegars, mustard seed, star anise, fennel, peppercorns, and salt, then packed into three quart jars and refrigerated, storing 2+ months. Source anchors: rind; sugar; vinegar; star anise; mason jars.
Analysis: The recipe turns a usually-discarded melon rind into a pantry item, an emblem of the waste-not ethic. Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Mushrooms

Summary: Pan-fried mushrooms with demiglace and garlic — scrubbed, quartered, and fried in lard (higher smoke point than butter) in batches so they brown rather than steam, finished by coating them in frozen demiglace blocks and adding garlic when the pan cools. Duxelles are made from the same recipe with a food-processor mince. Source anchors: lard; batches; Maillard; demiglace.
Analysis: The browning mechanism (water must boil off before Maillard browning) is the real lesson, and the cook-in-batches rule is its practical consequence. Using demiglace turns the reduced stock from the butchering chapter into an everyday flavor bridge. This recipe shows the charcuterie pipeline feeding the vegetable side of the plate. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Cabbage: Sauerkraut

Summary: Fermented cabbage in a ceramic crock set ($30) with a water seal: cabbage shredded on the deli slicer, salted, squeezed, packed under its own juices with weights, and left at room temperature for a month or more; it is then portioned, vacuum sealed, and frozen. Source anchors: crock; water seal; deli slicer; salt; one month; vacuum seal.
Analysis: Sauerkraut is the book's flagship lacto-fermentation project, and using the deli slicer for the shredding step cross-uses the butchering tool. The water-seal-and-weights crock is a low-tech controlled environment that needs no electricity. Vacuum-sealing the finished batches ties it to the Reuben recipe. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Pumpkin

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Pumpkin: Soup

Summary: Pumpkin soup is a large-batch fall canning project: halved pumpkins roasted face-down until a steak knife slides through, scooped and blended with stock into a smooth soup, seasoned with salt, pepper, and apple cider vinegar to add vibrancy, then pressure canned or served fresh with optional sour cream, half-and-half, or pan-fried kielbasi. Source anchors: roast; stock; stick blender; vinegar; pressure can.
Analysis: The soup is built for scale (2–4 pumpkins at a time) and explicitly for pressure canning, making it the archetypal fall batch product. The seasoning advice (add salt, pepper, vinegar, taste until the soup has complexity but no identifiable notes) is the chapter's best instruction on seasoning. Canning before the dairy and meat additions is the correct food-safety sequencing. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Potato: Hash Browns

Summary: Shredded potatoes and onions wrung of moisture, mixed with garlic, eggs, salt, and pepper, formed into patties, pan-fried in melted lard in cast iron until browned, and finished in a 350°F oven. Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Pork: Head Cheese

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Pork: Liverwurst

Summary: Liverwurst solves the freezer backlog of pig livers: 10 lbs cubed liver, 7.5 lbs lean pork, 2.5 lbs backfat, onions, powdered dry milk, and a spice set, ground coarse then fine, mixed, packed into fabric cases or vacuum bags, simmered 3 hours, cooled, and frozen in portions. A pig liver weighs about 2% of hanging weight, so the yield is substantial. Source anchors: 10 lbs liver; 25 lbs liverwurst; fabric casing; simmer 3 hours.
Analysis: Liverwurst is the recipe that rescues a large, strong-tasting organ meat most cooks would discard, converting the liver backlog into a sliced delicatessen product. The fabric-sewing-machine casing is a homemade tool that avoids spending on commercial casings. This is the use-every-bit ethic at its most creative. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Pork: Stock

Summary: Pork stock begins by breaking the skeleton (spine disjointed at the bench, leg bones cut with a reciprocating saw), roasting bones and onions at 450°F then 350°F, simmering with water covering about 80% of the bones for 24–48 hours, draining through a basket or pour-off, straining, and either pressure-canning or converting into tomato soup. Corcoran deliberately skips Escoffier refinement in favor of hearty peasant fare. Source anchors: skeleton; roast; 80% water; 24-48 hours; 5 gallons; peasant fare.
Analysis: The stock recipe is the capstone of the whole pig pipeline, converting the skeleton into 5 gallons of broth that becomes soup and demiglace. The 80%-water rule prevents dilution, and the deliberate rejection of Escoffier's refinement is the book's honest energy budget. The careful counter-sliding drain procedure reads as genuinely hard-won technique. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Pork: Tomato Soup

Summary: The 9–12 gallon follow-on to pork stock: 7 gallons stock, roasted diced tomatoes (18 28-oz cans), sweated onions and garlic, basil, pepper, salt, and bay leaves, blended and pressure canned into 36–48 quart jars. Source anchors: 7 gallons stock; 18 cans tomatoes; roast; 36-48 jars; pressure can.
Analysis: This recipe is the definitive fall batch product, turning the stock into shelf-stable lunches for the year. The multi-oven practicalities (two ovens for the tomatoes, spice grinder for pepper) make it a real production project. The canned-jar output is the bar chart of the household's entire food storage system. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Pork: Lard

Summary: Lard is rendered pig fat with impurities removed, ideal for frying (374°F smoke point) and flaky pastry; leaf lard (from the caul over the viscera) is superior in whiteness and flavor to back fat. Cubes boiled with water, then simmered until rendered and strained through a filter funnel, stored as 1-tablespoon pucks in silicone molds, with cracklings fed to dogs, chickens, or compost. Source anchors: leaf lard; back fat; 374°F; filter funnel; pucks.
Analysis: The lard recipe is the identity of the whole book's cooking style — every fried and flaky dish depends on it — and the leaf-versus-back grading is the connoisseurship detail. The tablespoon-puck storage is portioning technology that makes the fat instantly usable. Filtering through the multipurpose funnel ties the recipe to the kitchen tool section. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Chicken: Chicken Liver Pâté

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Chicken: Stock

Summary: Chicken stock from 4–16 vacuum-sealed carcasses, roasted with vegetables at 450°F, simmered 24 hours, strained, then pressure canned or converted into soup; Corcoran vacuum-seals carcasses as he generates them, leading to seasonal big batches. Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Chicken: Demiglace

Summary: Demiglace is stock reduced 2:1 to 4:1 in wide shallow pans to concentrate flavor, then frozen into 1-tablespoon blocks in silicone molds for instant umami in sauces and mushrooms. Source anchors: reduce; 2:1 to 4:1; silicone mold; blocks.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Duck: Duck Liver Pâté

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Duck: Pan Fried Breast

Summary: Duck breast scored in a cross-hatch, started skin-side down in a cold cast iron skillet over medium-low heat to render the fat before searing, then flipped for a shorter cook, and browned on the edges, roughly three-quarters of the time on the skin side. Source anchors: score; cold pan; render; skin side down.
Analysis: Starting from a cold pan is the single crucial duck technique — it renders fat before the pan gets hot enough to scorch — and the three-to-one cook-time ratio prevents overcooking the meat. Scoring lets the fat escape cleanly. This recipe is a masterclass in heat management on one protein. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Duck: Duck Leg Confit

Summary: Duck legs rubbed with salt, pepper, thyme, juniper, and garlic, refrigerated 1–2 days, washed, then submerged in rendered duck or goose fat (topped up with butter) and cooked 2–4 hours at 300°F; legs store under the fat for a month, and the fat is filtered and reused. Source anchors: render fat; 300°F; 2-4 hours; store under fat; reuse.
Analysis: Confit is the from-scratch version of the preservation method the canning-history section described, and the month-long submersion storage is the payoff. Reusing the fat through the filter funnel ties the recipe back to the kitchen tools. This is the definition recipe for duck-leg preservation. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Turkey: Deep Fried

Summary: A marinade-injected, 350°F deep-fried turkey (aiming for 380–400°F, since the bird drops the oil temperature), with oil level set by a water-displacement pre-test, cook time about 3.5 minutes per pound, and a meat thermometer target of 175–180°F; Corcoran emphasizes severity of the safety conditions (no children, dogs locked inside, sober operator). Source anchors: inject; displacement test; 350°F; 3.5 min/lb; 175-180°F.
Analysis: The water-displacement oil-quantity test is the safety-critical trick that prevents boilover, and the overshoot-to-380/400 technique compensates for the bird's thermal mass. The explicit security and sobriety requirements treat deep-frying as a serious operation. This is the showpiece recipe with the book's strongest safety lecture. Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Beef: Jerky

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Beef: Steaks

Summary: The simple steak method: dry both sides, salt generously, preheat a cast iron skillet until it just begins to smoke, sear, flip, and rest briefly; Corcoran calibrated 5 minutes per side on a 10,000 BTU range for medium-rare, and 2 minutes on his 25,000 BTU range. Source anchors: salt; cast iron; smoke; 5 minutes; 25,000 BTU.
Analysis: The recipe is pure technique with the burner-specific timing as the calibration lesson, since every kitchen differs. The salt-before and dry-surface rules are the two variables that produce crust. This is the minimal recipe that the sous vide and pan sections serve. Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Dairy: Sour Cream

Summary: Sour cream (and yogurt) thickens milk by microbial action: heat unpasteurized milk to 180°F to kill competitors, cool below 105°F (or use pasteurized store milk directly), stir in one tablespoon of live-culture sour cream or yogurt, and hold at 105–110°F for 8–12 hours in a dehydrator. Source anchors: 180°F; 105°F; culture; dehydrator; 8-12 hours.
Analysis: The recipe turns the dehydrator into a culture chamber, reusing a tool for an unintended purpose, which is the book's frontier-systems reflex. The pasteurize-if-raw, add-culture, hold-temperature pattern is the full microbial method. This recipe is the base for the sour cream used across the salad dressings. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Brewing: Beer

Summary: Using extract kits from Northern Brewer (Imperial Stout $46, Kama Citra IPA $36, Spiced Winter Ale $31, Irish Red and Nut Brown at $27), the process is: boil 5 gallons of water with malt extract, steep the grain bag, add hops on schedule, cool, transfer wort to a carboy, pitch yeast, cap with an airlock, shake, and check back in several weeks. Source anchors: extract kit; malt; hops; carboy; airlock.
Analysis: Extract kits are the deliberately easy on-ramp to brewing, and the process list doubles as the equipment handoff. Recommending specific kits with prices makes the entry decision concrete, and noting which beers feed recipes (Stout in beef ribs, Citra in fish-taco batter) ties brewing to the kitchen. This is the beer pipeline feeding the cookbook. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

A nearby orchard fills his carboy for $8/gallon. Source anchors: cider apples; Wyeast 4766; Campden; 5 gallons; airlock.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Brewing: Mead: Basic

Summary: Basic mead dissolves 15 pounds of honey in water (with or without heat, since heat slightly degrades flavor), optionally sterilizes with a Campden tablet, adds yeast, yeast nutrient, and water, caps with an airlock, shakes, and checks in several weeks. Source anchors: 15 lbs honey; Wyeast 4184; nutrient; Campden; airlock.
Analysis: The recipe's simplicity IS the point, and the heat-degrades-flavor note is a genuine brewing decision made explicit. The 15-pound-for-5-gallon ratio is the standard mead strength. This base is what every variant below extends. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Analysis: The variant list is the book's way of showing recipes as dials on a base rather than one-off works, which is the same recipe-ecology logic as the dressings. This is the famous ancient orange mead. Cross-adding spices and fruit produces a spiced melomel hybrid. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

This is berry-glut processing for the fermenter. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Brewing: Dandelion Wine

Summary: A one-year dandelion harvest grounds the recipe: 5 quarts of yellow dandelion flowers, 5 gallons of boiling water, 10 lbs of sugar, a sliced lemon, wine yeast, and yeast nutrient, fermented under an airlock then bottled and stored horizontally in a cool place. Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Pig Roast

Summary: A whole-pig barbecue for a crowd: cooking one pig self-serve lowers work per guest versus preparing 50 steaks, and it impresses city and suburban friends (who are amazed you raised and cooked a pig). It is still a mountain of work; Corcoran ran his first roast without a mentor and succeeded. Source anchors: whole pig; self-serve; crowd; city friends.
Analysis: The self-serve-one-pig framing is the throughput logic that justifies the whole undertaking, and the impress-your-friends note owns the social payoff candidly. The section then breaks the roast into logistics, pig farming, pit construction, grate construction, cooking, and sauces. This is the volume's biggest project recipe. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Pig Roast: Logistics

Summary: The schedule is the plan: four months ahead buy the piglet; one month ahead order 50 cinderblocks, three sheets of 14-gauge steel, expanded steel mesh, and steel tubing; three weeks ahead take delivery and verify serving gear; two weeks ahead build the pit and weld the grate; one week ahead buy cabbage, sauce ingredients, foil, mop, and disposables; and days ahead assign volunteer roles. The morning of, the firemaster starts the fire by 8 am, the pig is slaughtered by 10 am and into the pit by 11, for a serving time no earlier than about 9 pm unless you cook smaller pigs — Corcoran prefers two roughly 100-lb live-weight piglets to start at 8 am and eat at 6 pm. Source anchors: 4 months; cinderblocks; 8 am fire; 11 am pig; two piglets.
Analysis: The week-by-week schedule is the actual content of the section, converting a wish into a chart of concrete tasks. The two-small-pigs-over-one-big-pig decision is the scheduling insight that keeps a roast on a human dinner clock. Recruiting volunteers (firemaster, pit master, lieutenant) institutionalizes the party's roles. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Pig Roast: Pig Farming

Summary: Pig-roast planning works backward from party date and guest count: assume one pound of meat per person, double that to live weight (cut weight is half of live weight), so 50 guests means 100 lbs of piglet. Piglets are weaned at about 8 weeks (56 days, roughly 35 lbs), reach 80 lbs at 13–14 weeks, so buy 8-week piglets 5–6 weeks before the roast; fallback plans include canceling, switching to sheep, or buying whole pigs from a butcher. Source anchors: 1 lb per person; 35 lbs; 80 lbs at 13-14 weeks; 5-6 weeks ahead.
Analysis: The backward scheduling from party date through weaning age to purchase date is the volume's cleanest example of inverse logistics. The 35-lb-wean to 80-lb target translates a livestock fact into a calendar. The contingency list (cancel, sheep roast, butcher) is exactly the belt-and-suspenders thinking the book recommends. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Pig Roast: Pit Construction

Summary: The firepit is built from 48 unmortared cinderblocks in a U shape about 7 feet long, 4 feet wide, and 32 inches tall, covered with three 4x4 sheets of 14-gauge steel (two over the top, one leaning on the open front, each about 50 lbs), on bare ground away from structures and branches; construction takes about half an hour. Optionally expanded steel mesh can be laid between courses. Source anchors: 48 cinderblocks; 7 feet; 4 feet; 32 inches; 14 gauge steel; half hour.
Analysis: The itemized build makes a huge-looking structure into an afternoon project, and the half-hour estimate deflates the mystique. The open-front U design with liftable top plates is the geometry that converts a fire into a smoker. The bare-ground requirement and the fire-department check are the safety obligations. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Pig Roast: Grate Construction

Summary: Corcoran welds his own grate: a 30x70-inch rectangular frame of 1.5-inch square steel tubing covered with expanded steel mesh, with four L-shaped lift handles welded at each corner, built with a MIG welder and 90-degree magnets in about two hours; he deliberately diverges from Myron Mixon's built-in-grate method because his lift-handle design lets the pitmaster carry the pig out easily. Source anchors: 30x70; 1.5-inch square tubing; expanded mesh; lift handles; MIG.
Analysis: Publishing a full cut list and build order makes the grate buildable by a neophyte welder, and the two-hour estimate invites participation. Choosing lift handles over Mixon's stretcher is Corcoran defending his own design against a famous authority. The mesh-flip-and-weld sequence is the welding section's practical payoff. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Pig Roast: Pit Cooked Whole Pig

Summary: The pit-cook procedure: start the fire hours ahead to coals, rake coals forward, season the gutted pig inside and out with a vinegar-salt-pepper-pepper-flake mix, lower the grate with four men into the U-pit, cover with steel, hold about 250°F, optionally roll at five hours, mop hourly with the seasoning mix, and cook about 10 hours until tender. Source anchors: 250°F; vinegar; salt; pepper; five hours; 10 hours.
Analysis: The temperature target (250°F) and the 10-hour window are the roasting spec, and the vinegar-mop is the bark-seasoning mechanism. The four-man carry and cover/uncover choreography treat the pit as a reproducible oven. Rolling at five hours is the evenness accommodation. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Pig Roast: Basic BBQ Sauce

Summary: The base BBQ sauce from Franklin Barbecue lineage: a 64-oz bottle of ketchup, water, white and apple vinegars, brown sugar, Worcestershire, chili powder, cumin, salt, and pepper, simmered and stored in half-gallon jars. Source anchors: ketchup; vinegars; brown sugar; Worcestershire; simmer.

Corcoran's scrape-the-ketchup-bottle frugality is a comic but real cost-saving habit. This is the base recipe of the roast's finish. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Pig Roast: Vinegar BBQ Sauce

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Viking Party: Logistics

Summary: The Viking feast schedule: three-plus months ahead order the tent and start mead; one month ahead set up the tent, build the fire ring, and verify hose reach; one week ahead buy ingredients; two days ahead bake seed cakes; six hours ahead carry and erect the tent; three hours ahead build the fire; and 30 minutes ahead lay out cheese, uncork mead, and start the stew. Source anchors: 3 months; tent; fire ring; hose; seed cakes.
Analysis: The linear countdown is the entire planning content, and it mirrors the pig roast's backward scheduling. The fire-control verification (hose reach, tools) is the safety thread. The encampment distance (800 feet) is a deliberate logistics figure that forces hauling decisions. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Viking Party: Mutton Stew

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Sheep Roast

Summary: Corcoran's friends ran a pit-roasted sheep for one year's gathering, and he reports their methods secondhand: firepit, wood, slaughter, and a 10-hour bury-roast. Source anchors: sheep; pit roast; friends; 10 hours.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Sheep Roast: Logistics

Summary: Sheep-roast logistics run on a tight clock: split wood weeks ahead, build the dug stone-lined pit a day or more ahead, start the fire by 4 am, slaughter and skin the sheep at 9 am, prep at 10, wrap and place on the hot stones at 10:30, and uncover and serve 8–10 hours later. Source anchors: stone pit; 4 am fire; 9 am slaughter; 10 hours.
Analysis: The hour-by-hour timeline is the section's content, and the buried-stone method differs fundamentally from the above-ground pig pit, giving the reader two pit archetypes. The schedule-discipline warning (any slip means dinner at 11 pm) repeats the pig-roast lesson. This is baked-earth cooking in the book's inventory. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Sheep Roast: Pit Roasted Sheep

Summary: A whole sheep rubbed with red wine, salt, pepper, garlic, and onion, wrapped in multiple layers of parchment, foil, and wire or chicken mesh, placed on hot stones, banked with coals, covered with metal sheeting and soil, and left for 10 hours. Source anchors: red wine; garlic; parchment; foil; chicken wire; 10 hours.
Analysis: The multiple-layer wrap (parchment, foil, wire) is the engineering that keeps a buried carcass clean, flavored, and retrievable, and the soil cap creates a slow earthen oven. The garlic-and-onion stuffing inside the cavity flavors from within. This is the book's purest buried-pit cookery. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Analysis: The recipe scales a vegetable side to feast volume and cooks it in the same fire as the sheep, so one fire feeds the whole table. Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Sheep Roast: Roasted Cauliflower Approach Two

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Old Fashioned Thanksgiving

Summary: The Corcorans' Thanksgiving spread lists roasted turkey, gravy, mashed potatoes, dark rum sweet potatoes, stuffing, cranberries, potato au gratin, pumpkin pie, and apple pie, each linked to its own recipe, with the gravy, sweet potatoes, stuffing, cranberries, and au gratin detailed in the subsections that follow. Source anchors: roasted turkey; gravy; sweet potatoes; stuffing; cranberries; au gratin.
Analysis: The feast page is a menu that routes every dish to a recipe, showing how the book's single dishes assemble into a complete holiday. Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Old Fashioned Thanksgiving: Gravy

Summary: Gravy is one of the best uses of homemade stock: butter and flour cooked into a roux, then slowly whisked with turkey or chicken stock, seasoned with salt, pepper, sage, rosemary, and thyme, simmering half an hour or more. Source anchors: roux; stock; sage; rosemary; simmer.
Analysis: The roux-then-slowly-add-stock technique is what prevents clumping, and the simmer half-hour-plus is what deepens the flavor. Gravy is flagged as a prime destination for the stock pipeline, tying the Thanksgiving table back to the butchering chapter. This is the meal's sauce centerpiece built entirely from pantry stock. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Old Fashioned Thanksgiving: Dark Rum Sweet Potatoes

Summary: Peeled, cubed sweet potatoes boiled soft, mashed, and enriched with heavy cream, butter, maple syrup, and dark rum, rewarmed gently. Source anchors: sweet potatoes; cream; maple syrup; dark rum.
Analysis: The recipe combines the sweet-potato harvest with maple syrup and home-spirit flavor, tying the side to the orchard and brewing operations. The boil-mash-enrich structure is the same as the mashed-potato recipe. The optional spiced rum keeps it a holiday special. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Old Fashioned Thanksgiving: Stuffing

Summary: Bread-cube stuffing from sweated onions and celery in butter, seasoned with sage, marjoram, savory, thyme, and nutmeg, moistened with stock and beaten eggs, baked under foil 30 minutes plus 20 uncovered at 350°F with pats of butter on top. Source anchors: bread cubes; celery; stock; eggs; 350°F.
Analysis: The stuffing is the second large consumer of homemade stock, and the moistening-with-stock-and-eggs step is what binds the bread without sogging it. The foil-then-uncover bake sequence gives a crisp top over a moist interior. This is the meal's bread-based center that cycles the pantry. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Old Fashioned Thanksgiving: Cranberries

Summary: Cranberries simmered 30+ minutes with 2–3 cups of cherry juice, cloves, and sugar (only if unsweetened), then cooled and refrigerated. Source anchors: cranberries; cherry juice; cloves; sugar; simmer.
Analysis: Building the cranberry sauce on cherry juice rather than water adds fruit depth, and the only-if-unsweetened sugar note is the frugality and flavor rule. The simmer-and-chill method is forgiving and make-ahead. This is the meal's tart, bright counterpoint to the sweet potatoes. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Old Fashioned Thanksgiving: Potato Au Gratin

Summary: Thin-sliced potatoes layered in a greased dish, topped with a cream sauce of sweated leeks and garlic, salt, pepper, a bay leaf, and Swiss cheese, baked 40 minutes covered plus 20 uncovered at 350°F. Source anchors: potatoes; leeks; Swiss cheese; cream; 350°F.

Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Old Fashioned Christmas Dinner: Roast Goose

Summary: A dry-brined roast goose: salt rubbed into the skin a day ahead, then lard-brushed, seasoned with pepper, rosemary, sage, and thyme, and roasted at 350°F for 3 hours or until juices run clear. Source anchors: goose; dry brine; lard; 350°F; 3 hours.
Analysis: The goose recipe mirrors the turkey dry-brine method exactly, showing that the technique transfers across birds, and the longer 3-hour roast reflects the goose's higher fat and size.
