Household: Kitchen: Recipes: Feasts: Old Fashioned Christmas Dinner: English Pudding

Summary: A steamed English pudding — dried fruit soaked in sherry, mixed with suet, breadcrumbs, flour, sugar, spices, and eggs, gathered in a linen cloth, suspended over boiling water in a stock pot for 4+ hours on a wooden spoon — served with a butter-and-rum sauce. Source anchors: dried fruit; suet; sherry; linen; 4 hours.
Analysis: The pudding is the book's definition recipe for the older English sense of pudding (something boiled or steamed), and the linen-wrapped suspension method is a genuine historical technique. The suet and sherry tie it to the fat and brewing pipelines. The 4-hour steam is the patience payoff of a once-a-year tradition. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Farmhands

Summary: With roughly three person-days per week available after errands and relaxation, most homesteading couples need delegation, and Corcoran sorts tasks into three kinds: those only pros should do (e.g., logging), those pros or farmhands can do (fencing, shed construction), and those only farmhands can do (moving hay to a loft, weeding a vineyard). Farmhands are less productive per hour than pros but cheaper, and are more adaptable and easier to course-correct mid-job; the downside is that you supply tools, and farmhands are often less reliable. Source anchors: three person-days; delegation; pros; farmhands; adaptability.
Analysis: The three-task taxonomy is the delegation framework of the whole chapter, and the farmhand-versus-pro cost arithmetic is classic Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure. The reliability stories (a farmhand who raided the kitchen fridge, one who drank until he vanished, one who refused cold fence posts) keep the advice grounded in the realities of cheap labor. This opening sets up hiring, managing, and firing as a real management problem.

Farmhands: Liability

Summary: Before hiring farmhands, check homeowner's insurance for coverage of problems caused by, or to, people working on your land; a five-minute phone call and optional $20/year coverage could save you from bankruptcy. Source anchors: homeowner's insurance; liability; $20 per year.
Analysis: The liability warning makes hiring a legal-and-financial act, not just a labor act, and the trivial cost of coverage removes the excuse to skip it. This is the formal-institution layer the chapter adds to relationships otherwise governed by reputation. It protects the homestead itself, which is the book's core asset. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Farmhands: Hiring

Summary: Hiring starts with prevailing wages (bid above the $10/hour convenience store floor for hard outdoor work), sources including word of mouth, feed-store ads, and Craigslist, and a specific ad template for a weekend-and-summer job that mentions both the fun tasks and the grubby ones, suggests two friends applying together, and requires references. Corcoran's reference call asks how well the person performed, strengths and weaknesses, and whether he would hire again, then hosts a walk-around for active opt-in to specific work. He notes some people use WWOOFers as seasonal workers, which requires a bunkhouse. Source anchors: prevailing wages; feed store; ad template; references; walk around; WWOOFers.
Analysis: The published ad template and reference script turn hiring from gut feeling into a repeatable process, and calling out the grubby tasks filters for realistic applicants. The two-friends suggestion is a retention insight (people stay when they work with people they like). The active-opt-in framing makes the job feel chosen, and the WWOOF alternative expands the labor pool. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Farmhands: Managing

Summary: Management starts on day one with a timesheet and clipboard, then the tractor and woodchipper manuals and an Acknowledgement of Self Training form the farmhand must read and sign, acknowledging safe use of equipment and the right to re-read manuals on the clock. Source anchors: timesheet; manuals; Acknowledgement of Self Training; signed.
Analysis: The signed self-training acknowledgment converts casual help into documented responsibility, which is both a safety and a liability tool. Requiring the manual read before gear use protects the farmhand and the machinery. The form's emphasis on being able to re-read manuals while paid is a fair and enforceable standard. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Farmhands: Scheduling

Summary: Corcoran has worked alongside weekend farmhands and has had weekday farmhands who work while he is at his day job; working alongside makes the expected pace of labor clear and calibrates expectations of what gets done. Source anchors: weekends; weekdays; work alongside; pace.
Analysis: Working alongside is recommended because it teaches pace in both directions — the farmhand learns the standard, and the owner learns realistic throughput. The scheduling choice is presented as a fit decision, not a rule. This is the management-feels section that makes delegation workable. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Farmhands: Foreman

Summary: A foreman is the leader of a crew, like an NCO, and promoting your most skilled farmhand to foreman at a premium pays off in doubling line-worker productivity. Corcoran's example: three farmhands dug a fence-post hole wider and wider to remove a rock at 3 feet 9 inches against a 4-foot spec, and a good foreman paid an extra $2/hour could have saved him roughly $100 in labor. Source anchors: foreman; NCO; premium; 80/20; judgement call.
Analysis: The foreman concept extends management structure to the smallest farm crew, and the rock-hole cost example is a perfect micro-lesson in empowered judgment. Paying an extra $2/hour to save $100 is the kind of tiny arbitrage the book loves. This section teaches when to delegate judgment, not just labor. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Farmhands: Task Management

Summary: Corcoran gives farmhands a printed spreadsheet of seasonal tasks grouped into high, medium, and low priority, free to choose within the constraint that high priority is done first. Self-directed choice improves morale and retention, echoing psychologist Martin Seligman's learned-helplessness findings, and the W-2 versus 1099 distinction is influenced by how closely you direct the work. Source anchors: spreadsheet; priorities; self-directed; learned helplessness; W-2 vs 1099.
Analysis: The self-directed task list is both a morale tool and a subtle legal lever, since less direction pushes farmhands toward independent-contractor status. Citing Seligman gives the management advice a scientific veneer. This is the book's most transferable management technique for unpaid-free workers. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Farmhands: Finances

Summary: Some farmhands want daily cash (hence day labor), others weekly pay; Corcoran's rules are absolute: pay your people on time, always, have cash on hand in advance, make change yourself, never short someone, pay even bad work (the sweat is real; you pay for hours, not completion), absorb tool breakage as cost of business (never dock wages, which is also illegal), and round ambiguous timesheet hours up. Source anchors: daily cash; pay on time; make change; hours not completion; tool breakage; round up.
Analysis: The pay-your-people passage is the book's strongest ethical statement about labor, built on the power imbalance between an air-conditioned day job and a sweating field hand. The not-docking-wages rule is both moral and legal, and rounding up is practical generosity that buys loyalty cheaply. This section is the employer-side counterpart to the firing section's dignity. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Farmhands: Taxes and Laws

Summary: US law offers two ways to pay someone: W-2 employee or 1099 independent contractor, with tax consequences either way. The IRS uses heuristics in three categories — behavioral control (how closely you direct the work), financial control (tools, expenses, profit opportunity), and relationship (contracts, benefits, permanence) — and Corcoran argues that self-directed farmhands choosing from a long list can plausibly be 1099 contractors, while disclaiming tax-lawyer status. Source anchors: W-2; 1099; behavioral control; financial control; relationship.
Analysis: Quoting the actual IRS three-category framework makes the W-2-versus-1099 choice legible rather than mysterious, and ties the task-management advice back to legal classification. The self-directed work style is explicitly chosen partly to support contractor status, which reduces the homestead's payroll burden. The consult-a-pro disclaimer keeps the advice honest. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Farmhands: Firing

Summary: Farmhands are pre-selected for flakiness by low wages and day-labor, so some fraction will not meet expectations; after clear feedback fails, termination is better than stewing. Corcoran says you should never hire anyone you are not prepared to fire, a good firing is never a surprise, and the termination should be clear, dignified, and final — he gives a model script that conveys the decision, pays out generously (rounding $95 up to $120), and refuses to reopen it. Source anchors: flakiness; feedback; terminate; surprise; model script; final.
Analysis: The firing section is the book's rare explicit treatment of terminating a relationship well, and the model script is genuinely instructive: message, dignity, no excuses, no bargaining room. The rounding-up payout shows that even firing can be framed generously. Recommending Discipline without Punishment closes the chapter with a management reference. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Selling Products From Your Farm

Summary: Farm production will rarely match consumption exactly, so the obvious solution is selling surplus. Corcoran defines four tiers: Tier 0 give surplus away to friends and neighbors; Tier 1 sell casually on an ad-hoc basis; Tier 2 sell as a small merchant; Tier 3 sell as a production commodity farmer. Source anchors: surplus; Tier 0; Tier 1; Tier 2; Tier 3.
Analysis: The four-tier ladder is the chapter's decision framework, letting each reader choose the depth of market participation that fits their life. The framing that unsold surplus is a problem, not a virtue, anchors selling in the book's logistics view. This taxonomy then structures every subsection that follows. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Stage 0 — Giving Gifts

Summary: Surpluses that store well (apple butter, beer and wine, dried fruit, pepperoni, vinegar) work in Christmas gift baskets, while perishable surpluses like eggs go to neighbors, church, or the food pantry. Corcoran notes gifts hold high subjective value (a dozen $3 eggs may be worth far more to a friend) and that he prefers giving to selling because market consumers compare to commodities, converting $20 of gift-value into $2 of cash. Source anchors: gift baskets; eggs; neighbors; subjective value; commodities.
Analysis: The gift-stage analysis is a genuinely economic argument: the same product creates more utility given away than sold as a commodity, and gifts build social capital that would be destroyed by over-asking. The declining-marginal-utility of zucchini (the third is an obligation, the fifth ends a friendship) is the book's funniest economics line. This stage is the informal-institutions default before any market step. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Stage 1 — Sell Casually An On An Ad-Hoc Basis.

Summary: To avoid both cash pressure and ruined friendships, ad-hoc selling options include a self-serve farm stand (a table and $35 lockable cash box at the end of the driveway), partnering with a friend on a main road, selling to the local feed store (which buys chicken eggs for $3/dozen and goose eggs for \(0.50 each, with rules like dung-free eggs), or consigning at the farmers market. Source anchors: farm stand; cash box (\)35); feed store; \(3/dozen; consignment. Analysis: The self-serve stand and feed-store channel give the homestead an income line with near-zero interaction, which Corcoran's introversion clearly prizes. The concrete prices (\)3/dozen chicken, $0.50 goose) make the egg surplus a real budget line. The store's egg rules preview the legal chapter's detail-obsession. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Stage 2 — Sell As A Small Merchant.

Summary: Stage 2 is the serious business step, and Corcoran cautions from e-commerce experience that 90% of business work is not the underlying craft, profit is harder than it looks, opportunity cost means another career may beat farming, and selling requires enjoying people. He recommends Curtis Stone's The Urban Farmer (\(22) and Josh Kaufman's The Personal MBA (\)17). Source anchors: 90%; profit; opportunity cost; people; Urban Farmer.
Analysis: The four cautions are the book's honest cost-benefit framing for turning a homestead into a business, and they explain why the author mostly stays at Tiers 0–1. The 90%-not-the-craft line is the sobering math of any small business. Recommending two business books keeps the door open for readers who do want the merchant tier. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Stage 3 — Sell As A Production Commodity Farmer.

Summary: Corcoran states plainly that he is not a commodity farmer and, citing Wittgenstein, declines to lecture on it. Source anchors: commodity farmer; silent.
Analysis: The one-line section is the book's most elegant form of topical honesty, deliberately leaving a whole tier to other writers. It also clarifies the author's position so the laws and marketing sections can serve readers who are at different tiers. The Wittgenstein echo ties this to the scalding-and-scraping refusal. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Laws

Summary: The creation and sale of meat, eggs, and dairy are regulated at federal (FDA and USDA) and state levels, and each product class has its own exemption structure. Source anchors: FDA; USDA; meat; eggs; dairy; regulations.
Analysis: The legal overview is the chapter's bridge from marketing to compliance, and it converts a homestead into a regulated enterprise with specific tiers. The federal-versus-state split sets up the four product-specific subsections. This is the formal-institution layer of the whole selling chapter. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Laws: Beef, Pork, and Mutton

Summary: Under the Federal Meat Inspection Act and USDA FSIS, meat can be sold in multiple sizes — whole, half, quarter, primal, or individual cuts — with different legal hoops per size. The four legal tiers of butchering are: DIY for personal use, custom-exempt butchering for personal use, custom-exempt into quarters/halves/wholes for direct resale (no intermediaries; he has sold pig and sheep halves and bought beef halves from a friend), and FSIS-certified into any size for resale or indirect resale. Source anchors: FMIA; FSIS; whole; half; quarter; custom exempt; FSIS certified.
Analysis: The four-tier legal ladder is the chapter's most important content, because it defines exactly when a homesteader can sell what, and the direct-resale caveat (no intermediaries) is the trap most beginners hit. Corcoran's own sales (pig and sheep halves to friends, bought beef halves from a neighbor) anchor the tiers in practice. This is the definitive legibility section for meat law. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Laws: Poultry

Summary: Under the Poultry Products Inspection Act, producers below volume cutoffs are exempt from most requirements, and the chapter simplifies to three tiers: personal use (any amount for family and employees), producer/grower up to 1,000 birds per year in sanitary conditions, and a higher producer tier up to 20,000 birds with more requirements. Source anchors: PPIA; personal use; 1,000 birds; 20,000 birds.
Analysis: The 1,000-bird exemption is the friendly number that lets small homesteads sell poultry without FSIS inspection, and the chapter's willingness to collapse the full flowchart to three tiers keeps it usable. The sanitary-conditions condition is the real ongoing requirement. This is the poultry equivalent of the beef tiers. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Laws: Eggs

Summary: Under the Egg Products Inspection Act, farms with fewer than 3,000 laying hens are exempt from most egg regulations, and it is common for homesteaders to sell eggs via the local feed store or an unattended farm stand. Source anchors: EPIA; 3,000 laying hens; feed store; farm stand.
Analysis: The 3,000-hen threshold makes almost every homestead egg sale legal, which matches the ad-hoc sales the book endorses. Recognizing that eggs are the entry product for most homesteaders explains why the exemption is so generous. This closes the loop on the egg-surplus channels discussed in Stage 1. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Laws: Dairy

Summary: Dairy is regulated by the FDA under the PHSA, FDCA, and Pasteurized Milk Ordinance, but FDA rules largely govern interstate sales, leaving local sales to state law. New Hampshire RSA 184 requires pasteurization for all milk sales and then exempts direct sales to the final consumer and indirect sales via stores, which Corcoran celebrates with the state motto Live Free or Die. Source anchors: FDA; PMO; interstate; state law; RSA 184; pasteurized.
Analysis: The interstate-versus-intrastate split explains why dairy law looks contradictory, and the New Hampshire example shows a state carving out raw-milk space. The chapter's explicit reliance on state reach shows how different this area is from the federal meat tiers. This is the book's informal-institutions argument that legal reality is local. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Marketing

Summary: Marketing is misunderstood by people who fled the suburbs, but Corcoran argues it is a form of information flow that can benefit both parties, educating customers that a product exists and has benefits, with the core being finding product/market fit. Source anchors: marketing; information; product/market fit.
Analysis: Flipping marketing from scam to service sets up the entire subsection series, and the product/market-fit phrase is the frame every later marketing subsection serves. The empathy toward the anti-marketing bias (these are the people who left the city) makes the reframe land. This is the theoretical preface to the how-to. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Marketing: Definition

Summary: Marketing is not advertising; it is researching what you can do and what customers want, finding the intersection, finding potential customers, and educating them. The core is product/market fit — a product you can produce and a market you can sell to at a profit — which must be found before scaling; premature scaling is actively a mistake. Source anchors: definition; research; product/market fit; premature scaling.
Analysis: The definition deliberately separates the craft (finding fit) from its loudest tool (ads), which reframes marketing as research. The premature-scaling warning is the startup-discipline the book imports from the business world. Product/market fit then becomes the two-knob problem (product and market) the next sections tune. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Marketing: Product Definition

Summary: Product definition starts by brainstorming everything the farm could produce (software, lumber, furniture, fruit, meat, firewood) and subdividing each into marketable variants — pork, for example, as commodity cuts, farmer's-market cuts, mail-order sausage, dog bones, or a premium heritage-pig subscription box. The next step is talking to people with open-ended questions and watching for intense asides, like customers fixating on the wooden boxes bacon once came in, which become story and differentiation. Source anchors: product definition; variants; market research; asides; story.
Analysis: The pork-as-six-products example is the book's clearest lesson in differentiation, showing the same primal splitting into wildly different offerings. The listen-for-intense-asides technique is genuine, reproducible market research. This section teaches that product attributes include size, location, experience, and story, not just taste and quality. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Marketing: Market Definition

Summary: The same product can fail in one market and thrive in another: Bild Lilli dolls sold as risqué adult toys in Germany became Barbie in US toy stores, and Krating Daeng (Red Bull) sold to truck drivers in Thailand became a premium party drink for German youth. The difference was not the product but the market discovered for it. Source anchors: Bild Lilli; Barbie; Krating Daeng; Red Bull; market.
Analysis: The Barbie and Red Bull case studies are the book's most memorable proof that market definition, not product, determines success. The advice to think outside the box (wooden vases as dice cups for gamers) makes the lesson actionable. This is Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure at its most strategic.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Marketing: MVP

Summary: The biggest startup mistake is trying to do too much; Corcoran cites Saint-Exupéry's idea that perfection means nothing left to take away, applying it as minimum viable product. A plan with six parts, $100,000 in machinery, and three partners will fail; a gift-basket business should start as grape preserves at one market, and a woodworking business as a single hinged box made from purchased wood. Source anchors: MVP; Saint-Exupéry; grape preserves; single box.
Analysis: The MVP doctrine is the risk-management core of the marketing chapter, reducing every over-ambitious plan to a single strong link. The two worked examples (preserves, wooden box) make the abstraction concrete. This is the book importing lean-startup discipline into the farm direct-marketing tier. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Marketing: Brand and Story

Summary: Brands serve two good purposes — communication and guarantee — where invested reputation acts as a surety bond against cutting corners. A small, narrow brand is better than a wide weak one because it can evolve and extend later, and brands need stories: a wooden box of bacon with maple shavings and a hand-planed logo implies an entire honest, hard-working lifestyle without saying it. People buy stories as much as products. Source anchors: brand; guarantee; communication; story; wooden box.
Analysis: The communication-and-guarantee framing turns brand equity into economics, and the wood-shavings bacon box example is the chapter's best demonstration that a curated set of facts builds a story. The evolution-and-extension examples (Starbucks, Jack Daniel's) show brands growing slowly. This section is the product-side payoff of the market-research asides. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Marketing: Framing

Summary: Framing uses surrounding information to cast a product in a certain light: a wooden bookshelf alone frames cheap, next to an Ikea shelf it looks expensive, and with cross-sections and workshop photos it reads as durable hand craft. Framing can also juxtapose your own products, as nonprofits do with \(10/\)20/$100 donation boxes, or the seller does with pine, oak, and maple bowls priced to make the oak look like a bargain. Source anchors: framing; anchoring; Ikea; donation boxes; oak bowls.
Analysis: The framing concept gives the seller a set of controllable levers over perception, and the competing-product and own-product variants cover both market situations. The oak-bowl example is a clean, repeatable pricing trick. This is the chapter's psychology section, grounded in concrete display decisions. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Selling Products From Your Farm: Marketing: Ad Copy

Summary: For text ads (Craigslist, flyers, websites) Corcoran's three takeaways from the direct-mail tradition are sell the sizzle (describe benefits and problems solved), length sells (conversion rises linearly with ad length), and give a call to action (explicitly tell the reader the next step). He recommends Tested Advertising Methods ($37). Source anchors: sell the sizzle; length sells; call to action; Tested Advertising Methods.
Analysis: The excavator-ad example (model number versus capabilities versus problems-solved) is a masterclass in copy, and the call-to-action rule fixes the most common dead-end ad. The length-sells claim contradicts web-minimalism and stands on decades of mail-order A/B testing. This closes the marketing chapter with an actionable writing guide. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Alcohol

Summary: Alcohol is made by growing a plant product, converting long-chain carbohydrates to sugars as needed, adding yeast, and letting yeast turn sugars into ethanol; distilling adds a water-removal step for hard alcohol. Corcoran lists the brewable varieties — beer (hops and barley), corn beer, wine (grapes), mead (honey), cider (apples) — and the five-step brewing process from growing to bottling, plus the legal complications around distilling. Source anchors: brewing; ethanol; beer; wine; mead; cider; distilling.
Analysis: The single-page chemistry and process overview gives the reader the framework for everything else in the chapter, and the mention of legal complications flags distilling as the one branch with real risk. Listing the varieties maps each beverage to its plant source, connecting brewing to the growing chapters. This is the chapter's thesis paragraph. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Alcohol: Growing and Harvesting Ingredients

Summary: All brewing inputs can be grown on a homestead; Corcoran recommends the book Gardening for the Homebrewer and then treats each ingredient class, pointing to volume 1 for corn, grapes, honey, and apples. Source anchors: hops; barley; corn; grapes; honey; apples; Gardenging for the Homebrewer.
Analysis: The ingredient-by-ingredient survey ties the harvest chapters (hops trellis, grain field, vineyard, bees, cider orchard) to the fermentation chapter, closing a loop across the two volumes. Cross-referencing volume 1 avoids duplication while keeping each crop's brewing use explicit. The grouped treatment makes the chapter skimmable by the crop a reader grows. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Alcohol: Growing and Harvesting Ingredients: Hops

Summary: Corcoran has grown Nugget, Centennial, Mount Hood, and Willamette hops, trained vertically on sisal twine from greenhouse eyebolts down to ground stakes, pruning all but the strongest vine in spring, then cutting the string to harvest buds for immediate brewing or dehydration. He cites two hop-growing books. Source anchors: Nugget; Centennial; Mount Hood; Willamette; sisal twine; dehydrate.
Analysis: The four-variety list and the vertical-string training method make hop growing concrete, and the dehydrate-for-later step connects hops to the pantry's dehydrator. The simple greenhouse-eyebolt trellis shows that commercial telephone-pole hop yards are not required. This section turns a specialized crop into a homestead-scale project. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Alcohol: Growing and Harvesting Ingredients: Barley and Wheat

Summary: Barley and wheat are grasses grown for their seed, either broadcast by hand or sown in drills. Corcoran's wife grew wheat and he grew barley; a plausible yield is about half a bushel of grain per 1–2 lbs of seed, and since beer needs about 6 lbs of grain per 5-gallon carboy, 1 lb of seed can grow enough for three or four carboys. Harvesting at homestead scale splits into reaping (scythe, $200 with accessories, or a PTO sickle-bar mower), threshing (flails or a $1,000 12V portable combine), and winnowing (a sheet and a box fan). Source anchors: barley; wheat; broadcast; seed ratio; scythe; winnow.
Analysis: The yield math (1 lb of seed into three or four carboys of beer) is the chapter's legibility anchor, showing precisely why growing your own grain is plausible. The seed-ratio concept (about 1:14 in the Middle Ages) and the three-step reaping/threshing/winnowing sequence preserve the craft's real mechanics. The reference to Against the Grain by James C. Scott adds a historical-institutions hook. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Alcohol: Growing and Harvesting Ingredients: Corn

Summary: Corn growing is deferred to the volume 1 section on the crop. Source anchors: corn; volume 1.
Analysis: The one-line cross-reference keeps the alcohol chapter focused on fermentation while acknowledging the crop's farming details live elsewhere. This is the book's consistent anti-duplication editorial rule. Corn returns as the corn-beer and distilling-mash input later. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Alcohol: Growing and Harvesting Ingredients: Grapes

Summary: Grape growing is deferred to the volume 1 vineyard section. Source anchors: grapes; vineyard; volume 1.
Analysis: As with corn, the wine-grape farming mechanics live in volume 1, leaving this chapter to own only the fermentation side. The deferral keeps the two volumes' boundaries clean. Grapes enter this chapter only as a wine input. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Alcohol: Growing and Harvesting Ingredients: Honey

Summary: Honey sourcing is deferred to the volume 1 beekeeping section; mead makers can either buy honey or keep hives. Source anchors: honey; beekeeping; volume 1.
Analysis: The same deferral pattern applies, with honey flagged as the mead input that a hive-owning homestead can produce itself. This is the chapter's quiet argument that mead is the most self-sufficient beverage in the book. The beekeeping cross-reference keeps detail in volume 1. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Alcohol: Growing and Harvesting Ingredients: Apples

Summary: Apples for cider are deferred to the volume 1 cider orchard section, which is the prerequisite for making cider from scratch. Source anchors: apples; cider orchard; volume 1.
Analysis: Deferring again, the chapter makes clear that from-scratch cider requires planting a cider orchard and waiting years, which the hard-cider recipe in the Household chapter then uses. This is the orchard's long-term payoff framed as a beverage. The deferral rule keeps the book's chapters in their lanes. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Alcohol: Brewing

Summary: The brewing section opens with equipment and mead specifics rather than a full commercial-brewing treatise. Source anchors: brewing; equipment; mead.
Analysis: Corcoran deliberately keeps brewing modest, so the brewing pages cover only the equipment needed and the mead family. This keeps the chapter proportional to its use in the book. The equipment and mead subsections are the practical core. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Alcohol: Brewing: Equipment

Summary: Minimal brewing needs a stock pot, a carboy (or even a 5-gallon bucket), and an airlock. Starter kits run $110 (plastic buckets) to $189 (glass carboys); home-grown ingredients add a $167 barley crusher and a $320 all-grain cooler system; bottling mead or wine in bottles needs a \(160 corker. Source anchors: stock pot; carboy; airlock; starter kit (\)110); barley crusher; corker.
Analysis: The equipment ladder keeps the entry price low and then prices the upgrades needed to move from extract to all-grain and to bottle aging. The bucket-versus-glass-carboy choice is presented as a starter decision, not a creed. This is the beer-versus-homestead-scale shopping list. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Alcohol: Brewing: Mead

Summary: Mead ferments honey; honey-plus-juice is a melomel; spiced mead is a metheglin. Corcoran has made meads, melomels, and metheglins from purchased honey, and recommends two recipe books plus a history of the Anglo-Saxon mead hall. He notes mead ages for years and that if you like it, you should brew more than you drink to prime the pipeline for 3, 5, or 10 years ahead. Source anchors: mead; melomel; metheglin; Make Mead Like a Viking; age.
Analysis: The melomel/metheglin vocabulary is the only formal taxonomy the chapter needs, and the brewery-ahead-of-demand advice is the book's pipeline logic applied to alcohol. Recommending a Viking mead book matches the feast chapter's recreationist streak. This section frames the mead recipe family in the Household chapter. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Alcohol: Distilling

Summary: Distilling is the one branch with serious legal complications, and Corcoran reports interest in the craft while laying out why the risk/reward looks terrible in the US. Source anchors: distilling; legal; risk; reward.
Analysis: The distilled-beverage dream meets the legal wall here, and the chapter's honest hedging (interested, but the risk is bad) is the book modeling rational discretion. The three privacy-and-legal subsections that follow explain exactly why. This is where frontier self-sufficiency hits real federal constraint. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Alcohol: Distilling: Legality Of Distilling Beverages

Summary: Federal law makes distilling alcohol for human consumption illegal without a commercial Federal Distilled Spirits Permit, and state rules may add more; the claim that home distilling is legal up to 200 gallons is false. Corcoran walks through six ways a still owner could be discovered (new BATF policy, false reports, chases through the property, email warrants, future data-mining, police vendettas) and the felony consequences — up to $10,000 fines and 5 years per offense, stacked charges, a possible felon record that costs guns, voting, adoptions, and clearances, plus lawyer fees that can hit $25,000. Source anchors: Federal Distilled Spirits Permit; BATF; felon; $10,000; 5 years; lawyer.
Analysis: The distilling-legality section is the book's most detailed risk analysis, translating a hobby into a probability-weighted legal exposure. The enumeration of discovery paths is genuinely paranoid-in-a-good-way risk planning. The best-case-scenario math (a $10,000 worst case even if charges drop) is the reason Corcoran declines: process is punishment for the accused. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Alcohol: Distilling: Legality Of Owning A Still

Summary: Owning a still is legal as long as it is not used for potable alcohol, and some claim they will buy a moonshine still and say it is for water. Corcoran declines this defense, pointing out how thin it looks facing multiple felony charges with a still, moonshining books, and batches of corn beer as evidence. Source anchors: still; water distilling; moonshine books; evidence.
Analysis: The water-defense critique is the chapter's strongest realism lesson: possession acquires meaning from context and evidence, not from your stated intent. This is a caution against legal gray-zone gamesmanship. Corroborating books-and-batches as evidence shows the standard prosecutor's picture. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Alcohol: Distilling: Legality Of Distilling Fuels

Summary: Distilling alcohol for fuel is legal in the US, requiring registration as an alcohol fuel producer on form TTB 5110.74, which asks for the input materials, the locks on the still building, the still's serial number, and a diagram of the farm. Produced alcohol must be rendered unfit for human consumption, and Corcoran notes gasoline engines convert to ethanol better than diesels, so he has little interest. Source anchors: fuel; TTB 5110.74; registration; unfit for humans; diesel.
Analysis: The fuel-distilling path is presented as the legal alternative with real bureaucratic requirements, and the form's questions (locks, diagram, serial number) show exactly what the state wants to inspect. The gasoline-versus-diesel conversion asymmetry explains why the author is unmoved. This closes the distilling section with the one legal outlet. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Workshop

Summary: The Workshop chapter is the largest of the two-volume set, covering reference books, benches and vises, hand tools, solvents, electrical work, pressure washers, pumps, moving heavy things, chains, fasteners, lubricants, fuels, rust, painting, sharpening, blacksmithing, timberframing, welding, compressors, storage, small engines, hydraulics, projects, concrete, and manuals. Source anchors: workshop; tools; equipment; reference books.
Analysis: The Workshop chapter is the mechanical spine of the whole book, giving the homestead the ability to build, repair, and maintain every machine the earlier chapters introduced. Its size reflects the book's central belief: skills and repair capacity are the capital that keeps homesteads running. Grouping so many trades in one chapter lets the reader acquire a complete tool-and-knowledge system in order. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Reference Books

Summary: Corcoran recommends Pocket Ref (\(12) as a cheap pocket encyclopedia for everything from air-tool CFM to steel pipes, and Machinery's Handbook (\)63, now on its 31st edition) as the deep reference every shop should own, with older editions just as serviceable (his 15th edition even adds leather-belt drive data). He notes machinist's tool chests have a drawer sized for the Handbook. Source anchors: Pocket Ref (\(12); Machinery's Handbook (\)63); machinist's chest.
Analysis: The two-reference-book recommendation sets the standard for a workshop's knowledge base, distinguishing the daily quick-reference from the deep manual. The old-edition advice keeps the cost near zero, and the leather-belt-drive anecdote is a fun defense of older editions. This is the chapter's own bibliography. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Work Benches

Summary: For most homestead work you want a general utility bench, not a cabinetmaker's bench: 8 feet long, 2 feet deep, 38–39 inches tall, built from a 3/4-inch plywood top, 2x8 stretchers, 4x4 legs, carriage bolts, and deck screws. Corcoran describes the full build, adds a plywood back and sides to stop racking, and mounted locking casters ($30 kit) to two benches. Source anchors: 8 feet; 2 feet; 38-39 inches; plywood; carriage bolts.
Analysis: The full dimensioned build makes the utility bench a reproducible project, and the racking fix (back and side panels) is the structural detail beginners miss. The caster addition is the mobile-base thinking the storage section repeats. This is the work surface every other workshop tool depends on. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Vises

Summary: A good machinist's vise is critical; enthusiasts prize Wilton bullet vises (\(560), but a Harbor Freight 6-inch (\)70) or a used vise at \(20–free works fine once restored with a wire wheel and paint. Corcoran mounts it at the front right corner so the handle and jaws clear the bench. Source anchors: machinist's vise; Wilton (\)560); Harbor Freight ($70); front right corner.
Analysis: The vise section demonstrates the used-and-restore path, where a $20 garage-sale vise equals a $560 premium one after an hour of cleaning. The front-right-corner mounting position is the ergonomic detail that makes the bench usable. This is the book's cheapest-to-quality wisdom condensed. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Hand Tools

Summary: Hand tools split into mechanic's and carpenter's sets. Corcoran recommends building a mechanic's kit over time at Harbor Freight ($40 visits) rather than one $2,350 1,025-piece set, lists the essentials (sockets, wrenches, screwdrivers, Allen keys, pliers, magnetic bowl), and picks socket storage trays with separate metric/SAE and standard/deep rows. Carpentry additions include a framing hammer, awl, files, squares, a Stanley tape, tool bags, and an apron. Source anchors: sockets; wrenches; Allen; socket trays; Stanley tape.
Analysis: The build-it-over-time versus one-giant-set comparison is the book's recurring cost philosophy, and the socket-tray feature list (separate metric/SAE, labeled pegs) shows attention to legibility. Recommending a specific tape brand as the one that matters is a rare moment of concrete brand advice. This section is the toolbox entry point. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Solvents

Summary: A modest solvent cabinet handles hundreds of jobs: alcohol (dissolves Sharpie ink), mineral spirits (clean oil-paint brushes and HVLP sprayers), xylene (dissolves toner for linoleum transfers and cleans a letterpress), and acetone (removes oils before painting; with xylene, partially dissolves ABS plastic for gluing). Source anchors: alcohol; mineral spirits; xylene; acetone; ABS.
Analysis: The four-solvent list with their one-line uses is the entire chemistry a shop needs, and each has a concrete job already shown elsewhere (Sharpie labeling, letterpress, pre-paint prep). The xylene-and-acetone ABS-gluing trick is a genuinely useful fabrication detail. This section makes solvents mundane rather than dangerous. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Electrical Work

Summary: DIY electrical work ranges from new circuits and outlets to barn wiring; a book like Ultimate Guide: Wiring helps. Corcoran keeps a dedicated electrical tool kit (pliers, wire stripper, screwdrivers, level, cable cutter, punchdown tool, conduit bender, fish tape, drill bit) plus supplies (wire nuts, staples, lubricant) in a 5-gallon bucket with Bucket Boss organizers, all under \(200, which a single avoided service call pays for. Source anchors: circuits; outlets; wiring book; bucket; Bucket Boss. Analysis: The under-\)200-total-kit claim is the strongest justification for DIY electrical, and the dedicated kit solves the trip-to-the-pegboard tax. The bucket-and-organizer storage makes a mobile, self-contained electrician's station. This is the book's frontier-systems version of an electrician's bag. Workshop: Pressure Washers

Summary: Pressure washers take garden-hose water, pressurize it with a pump, and spray it through a wand; a dump valve spills pressurized water when the trigger is off. Corcoran owns a gas 6-HP Excell/Devilbiss and, unusually, wishes he had an electric model to avoid winterizing one more fuel tank and carburetor. Source anchors: pressure washer; dump valve; gas; electric; winterize.
Analysis: The counterintuitive preference for a less-powerful electric model is the book's clearest example of thinking about maintenance burden over capability. Admitting the gas unit's ongoing parts failures (pump, carburetor, hose, wand, dump valve) builds the case. This is equipment ownership weighed by its whole lifecycle cost. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Pressure Washers: Gas versus Electric

Summary: Pressure washers come in both gasoline- and electric-powered versions, and Corcoran owns a gas unit yet counts this one of the very rare areas where a slightly less powerful electric tool might be a big win over a gasoline tool: a gas machine means owning one more complicated fuel tank that has to be run empty before winter, and one more carburetor that has to be rebuilt every ten years. Source anchors: gasoline powered; electric powered; fuel tank run empty; carburetor rebuilt.
Analysis: The preference for a less powerful electric model is the book's clearest example of owning equipment by total lifecycle burden rather than peak capability, since a pressure washer is only broken out every year or two. Winterizing a fuel tank and periodically rebuilding a carburetor are standing costs that an electric machine simply does not carry. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Pressure Washers: My Experience

Summary: Corcoran has had his pressure washer — an Excell / Devilbiss model with a 6HP Briggs & Stratton gas engine — for about 25 years, and in that time he has had to replace the pump, the carburetor, the hose, the gun handle, the wand, and the dump valve. The carburetor replacement was inevitable, since gas, especially ethanol-blended gas, eats seals and gums up parts; the dump valve likewise failed because of age and corrosion. The $150 pump replacement was avoidable — his fault for leaving water in the pump over winter, where the water expanded and cracked something inside, so he urges running the pressure washer dry before storing it. The good news is that most parts conform to standards: hoses are either 1/4 or 3/8 inch with M22 or quick-connect fittings, and wands and spray guns have matching threads. Source anchors: Excell/Devilbiss; 6HP Briggs & Stratton; pump; carburetor; hose; gun handle; wand; dump valve; $150; run dry; M22.
Analysis: The winterization lesson — run the pressure washer dry before storing it or trapped water freezes, expands, and cracks the pump — is the unforgettable safety-and-finance tip, made vivid by the expensive self-inflicted failure. The standardized-parts fact (1/4 or 3/8 inch hoses, M22 or quick-connect fittings, matching wand and gun threads) explains why a 25-year-old machine remains cheap and easy to repair. The year-by-year parts list is characteristic honesty about what long-term ownership of a rarely used tool actually costs. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Pumps

Summary: Utility pumps empty basements and ponds and move water to livestock; they come electric or gas, submersible or suction-fed. Corcoran covers suction and discharge hoses (2-inch suction at $70, 3-inch discharge at \(120), and the semi-trash versus trash-pump distinction for silty or sludge water, with capacities from 1,268 gph submersible (\)53) to 21,000 gph trash ($1,430). He has thought about a PTO-driven utility pump for his tractor. Source anchors: utility pump; submersible; suction hose; trash pump; PTO.
Analysis: The pump section maps each type to a job (submersible for a flooded basement, semi-trash for silty foundation holes, trash for a manure pond), which makes the bewildering spec sheet legible. The suction-hose stiffness requirement (to resist vacuum collapse) explains a physical detail most buyers ignore. The PTO-pump daydream extends the tractor's usefulness. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things

Summary: Moving heavy loads covers lowering friction, applying horizontal force, and applying vertical force, and Corcoran recommends the book Moving Heavy Things by January Adkins. The three sub-tasks structure the entire section. Source anchors: friction; horizontal force; vertical force; Moving Heavy Things.
Analysis: Decomposing heavy lifting into friction, horizontal, and vertical sub-tasks is the book's core insight for a class of jobs (boulders, presses, carcasses) that otherwise feel impossible. Each sub-task gets its own tools in the following subsections. This is the manual's version of a mechanical engineering primer. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: Lowering Friction

Summary: Lowering friction uses pipe-clamp black-steel pipes as rollers, either directly on a smooth floor or riding on dimensional lumber over rough ground, recycling the rear pipe to the front as the load moves. Corcoran notes pipe clamps double as woodworking clamps when not rolling loads. Source anchors: rollers; black pipe; Pony clamp; 2x6 rails.
Analysis: The roller-and-recycle method turns a heavy mill or press into a pushable load, and the pipe-clamp dual-use is the book's efficient reuse. Layering 2x6 rails under the roller pipes extends the technique to grass and dirt. This is the friction-lowering toolkit in one paragraph. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: Applying Horizontal Force: Pry Bars

Summary: A pry bar (Burke bar) multiplies force via mechanical advantage; Corcoran works an example where a 60-inch bar contacting a 2,000-lb tiller 2 inches above the ground needs under 20 lbs of force to budge it, versus 500 lbs of static friction. He owns a Harbor Freight 60-inch bar (\(25) and a better Burke Bar (\)98), and says the cheap one does the job at a quarter the price. Source anchors: pry bar; Burke bar; mechanical advantage; 30:1.
Analysis: The worked statics example (30:1 advantage turning 500 lbs of friction into under 20 lbs of effort) is the book at its most pedagogical, making leverage concrete. Recommending both a cheap and premium bar, with the cheap one endorsed for most work, is classic make-or-buy balance. Archimedes' lever quotation ties the math to deep history. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: Applying Horizontal Force: Come-Along

Summary: A come-along (winch puller) moves loads horizontally over tens of feet, using a ratchet so each lever pull takes up a few inches of cable and holds it. Corcoran finds come-alongs reliable in pulling but stiff and awkward in unspooling, and recommends a $35 Harbor Freight or $50 Tekton unit. Source anchors: come-along; ratchet; $35; $50; unspool.
Analysis: Dividing tools by travel distance (pry bar inches, come-along feet) is the selection rule the book gives, and the honest complaint about unspooling stiffness sets realistic expectations. The price spread (both cheap) keeps the entry barrier low. This is the horizontal-movement middle ground. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: Applying Horizontal Force: Hydraulic Ram

Summary: A hydraulic ram kit (a hand pump, hose, and effectors like push rods and clamshell pry tools) moves and pries loads short distances under high force. Corcoran used it to spread a crimped truck window frame, saving a $1,000 repair. Source anchors: hydraulic ram; pump; clamshell; \(1,000 saved. Analysis: The truck-window anecdote is the model of a tool paying for itself in a single repair, which is the book's favorite justification. Distinguishing the hydraulic ram's short-travel high-force niche from the come-along's long-travel role completes the horizontal toolkit. The cost (~\)200) is framed against the single saved bill. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: Applying Vertical Force: Tractor Jack

Summary: A tractor jack (high-lift or farm jack) lifts thousands of pounds up to about three feet via a lever-operated ratchet with quantized steps; it has a narrow baseplate needing a pad on soft ground. Corcoran contrasts its step-wise lift with the continuously divisible hydraulic ram, and notes the ratchet switch disables free-slide under load for safety. Source anchors: tractor jack; ratchet; quantized; baseplate; three feet.
Analysis: The quantized-versus-continuous lift distinction is the mechanical insight that prevents misuse, and the load-safe ratchet feature is a real safety design worth explaining. The three-foot ceiling sends heavier lifts to the gantry crane. This is the vertical-force entry tool. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: Applying Vertical Force: Engine Hoist

Summary: An engine hoist is a rolling U-base cantilever lift designed to pull car engines; Corcoran uses his (about $190) once every two years for tasks like moving a bench lathe, and admits it is probably not worth owning but is useful when the best tool for the job. Source anchors: engine hoist; cantilever; $190; bench lathe.
Analysis: The honest not-worth-owning-but-I-have-it admission is the book's most candid tool-economics statement, and it models the sunk-cost reality of a well-stocked shop. The design explanation (U-base sliding under a car) makes the tool's specificity clear. This is a rare anti-recommendation that still documents the tool. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: Applying Vertical Force: Lift Cart

Summary: A foot-pumped hydraulic lift cart ($220) serves as a movable shop table and a way to raise heavy loads to workbench height; Corcoran uses it to move milling machines, file cabinets, and pig carcass halves, and says he would buy it again. Source anchors: lift cart; $220; hydraulic; workbench height.
Analysis: The lift cart earns wholehearted endorsement because it solves the recurring butchering-and-shop problem of raising heavy things to bench height, and the carcass-half use ties it to meat processing. Its dual role as a rolling table gives it constant, not occasional, value. This is the mobile bench that links the workshop and meat chapters. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: Applying Vertical Force: Gantry Crane

Summary: A gantry crane is a rolling steel A-frame with a horizontal beam, height-adjustable from about 6 to 12 feet, used to support loads beneath it; Corcoran bought his for hanging pig, sheep, and cow carcasses and has used it for CNC machines and presses. To lift you need the crane, a hoist, and a connection between them. Source anchors: gantry crane; 6 to 12 feet; carcasses; hoist; connection.
Analysis: The gantry crane is the centerpiece of the vertical-force section, and its carcass-hanging origin ties it directly to the meat chapter. The three-part need (crane, hoist, connection) is the exact scaffold the whole heavy-moving section builds. The adjustable height plus mobility makes it the homestead's traveling gantry. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: Applying Vertical Force: Gantry Crane: The Crane

Summary: Gantry cranes come in weight ratings from 1,000 to 4,400 lbs and prices from $350 to \(729; Corcoran recommends thinking through your maximum load plus lifting gear plus margin and owns a 4,000-lb unit. Source anchors: 1,000 lb (\)350); 2,000 lb (\(729); 4,400 lb; margin. Analysis: The rating-versus-margin advice (weight your max load, add the hoist, add margin) is the engineering discipline that prevents an overloaded crane. The price range (\)350–$729) makes the gantry affordable relative to the carcass value it protects. This is the sizing subsection for the chapter's key tool. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: Applying Vertical Force: Gantry Crane: Hoists: Rope

Summary: Rope hoists are the cheapest option, using pulleys for mechanical advantage (e.g., 6:1), but Corcoran reports his rated-1,000-lb unit could not lift a 600-lb carcass due to thin rope and friction, so he recommends against rope hoists entirely. Source anchors: rope hoist; 6:1; friction; recommend against.
Analysis: The failed-1000-lb-hoist story is the book's empirical rejection of a cheap-looking option, and the friction-under-load explanation teaches why ratings alone mislead. Recommending against a whole tool class is the strongest editorial verdict in the workshop chapter. This frees the reader to skip to chain hoists. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: Applying Vertical Force: Gantry Crane: Hoists: Chain

Summary: Chain hoists use a hand chain to retract a load chain at ratios from 5:1 to 250:1 (50:1 common), lock without slipping, and cost \(60–\)200; Corcoran owns a 2,000-lb unit (\(60) and recommends them for aging carcasses. He details how to tell the hand chain from the load chain by side of the body, and suggests a chain bag to keep the surplus loop off the carcass. Lever chain hoists (\)100) are the cranked variant. Source anchors: chain hoist; hand chain; load chain; 2,000 lb ($60); chain bag.
Analysis: The two-chains-don't-confuse-them explanation is the practical mastery moment of the section, and the chain-bag tip solves the machine-oil-on-carcass problem. The lock-without-slip property is why chain hoists beat rope. The affordable $60 entry makes the gantry solution complete. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: Applying Vertical Force: Gantry Crane: Hoists: Electric

Summary: Electric hoists (\(110–\)200, with doubled-cable rating variants that halve speed and length) are mounted with a bracket sized to a 1 3/4-inch square bar; Corcoran is curious but has not upgraded because three 2,000-lb chain hoists already do the job and replacing them with three 1,100/2,200-lb electric units would cost almost $1,000. Source anchors: electric hoist; 1 3/4-inch bar; $1,000; bracket.
Analysis: The bracket-sizing constraint is the hidden spec that forces fabrication, and the cost comparison (three existing chain hoists versus three electric units) is why Corcoran stays put. This is the honest economics of not upgrading. The doubled-load-doubled-cable feature is a useful pulley trick. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: Applying Vertical Force: Gantry Crane: Connection

Summary: Connecting a hoist to overhead steel must be done safely, since heavy weights overhead rank with spinning PTO shafts and gasoline fumes as serious dangers. Corcoran recommends chain over rope (rope stretches and gets cut on I-beam corners) for the connection, and describes wrapping chain over the beam and hooking grab hooks, or hooking the gambrel directly to the free hook when no hoist is used. Source anchors: overhead; chain; rope; grab hook; gambrel.
Analysis: The chain-over-rope recommendation is grounded in two real failure modes (stretch, corner-cut), and the direct-gambrel-to-hook option shows the minimal configuration. The safety preamble treats overhead work with the seriousness the book reserves for the most dangerous tools. This is the connection that completes the gantry system. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: Applying Vertical Force: Gantry Crane: Dimensional Considerations

Summary: Gantry solutions must respect vertical space: measure ceiling height, subtract the crossbar, the hoist height (hook to hook), connection chain inches, any adapter plate, and the gambrel; Corcoran has 11-foot ceilings and only just fits a beef carcass. Source anchors: ceiling height; subtract; hoist; 11 feet.
Analysis: The subtraction stack is the book's most literal dimensional checklist, and the just-fits confession at 11 feet shows real measurements. This prevents the classic error of buying a hoist that cannot clear the floor. It is the vertical-space analog of every sizing section in the book. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Moving Heavy Things: My Recommendations

Summary: Corcoran ranks heavy-moving tools by bang-for-buck: first-tier is a pry bar, a chain, weld-on chain hooks for the tractor, and a tractor jack (all cheap, used most weekends); second tier is a gantry crane, chain hoist, and lift table (for butcherers); the come-along, engine hoist, and hydraulic ram are rarely used and he cannot generally recommend them. Source anchors: pry bar; chain; chain hooks; tractor jack; gantry crane; lift table.
Analysis: The tiered recommendation is the chapter's buying guide in miniature, saving the reader from buying the rarely-used tools. The first-tier set is affordable enough to acquire immediately, and the second tier is explicitly gated to butchering need. This is the book's signature cost-ladder conclusion applied to heavy moving. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Chains

Summary: Chains are linear series of metal links used for supporting and pulling loads, and Corcoran lists ways he uses them: lifting a rototiller, dragging trees, lifting a hog for butchering, binding a hoist to a crane, securing trailer loads, and fastening a quick spade. He notes he owned no chains until he moved to a farm. Source anchors: chains; rototiller; dragging trees; hog; trailer.
Analysis: The anecdote-led use list makes chains real before the terminology starts, and the no-chains-until-the-farm detail shows the tool learns from necessity. Chains connect the butchering-rig, forestry, and fencing chapters into one hardware family. This is the overview before the terminology sections. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Chains: Overview

Summary: Chain terminology includes grab hooks (which grip a link), width (the link bar's cross-section), grade (NACM strength rating), the working load limit (WLL), and the standard grades 43 / 70 / 80 / 100, where only 80 and 100 are approved for overhead lifting. Corcoran recommends standardizing on 3/8-inch chain, which in grade 43 gives a 5,400 lb WLL, and notes Harbor Freight and Northern Tool as sources. Source anchors: grab hook; width; grade 43; grade 80; WLL; 3/8 inch.
Analysis: The grade-and-WLL table is the safety core of the whole chapter, since overhead lifting demands grade 80 or 100 regardless of looks, and the DOT-versus-OSHA split (43/70 for securing loads, 80/100 for overhead) assigns each grade its legal role. Standardizing on 3/8-inch keeps the fleet interoperable. This is the legibility table that prevents a fatal chain substitution. This converts a messy domain into the measurable, actionable quantities that define Legibility.

Workshop: Chains: Hooks

Summary: A chain is useless without hooks since you cannot tie it to itself; the useful farm types are grab hooks, lift (hoist) hooks, choker (slip) hooks, and J hooks. Source anchors: grab; lift; choker; J hooks.
Analysis: Classifying the four hook types gives the reader the vocabulary to identify and buy the right connector for each job. Each hook's mechanism (grab-holds-link, lift-has-throat, choker-cinches, J-threads-through) is the selection criterion. This overview sets up the four subsections. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Chains: Hooks: Grab

Summary: A grab hook grabs tightly onto a chain link to form a loop or join two chains, either terminated with a clevis pin or welded to equipment; Corcoran has terminated almost all his chains with grab hooks and welded three onto his tractor bucket. Source anchors: grab hook; clevis pin; weld; tractor bucket.
Analysis: The grab hook is the foundational connector, and welding three to the tractor bucket creates the lifting points the butchering chapter presumes. The clevis-pin version keeps chains field-adjustable. This is the connector that makes the chain useful everywhere. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Chains: Hooks: Lifting

Summary: Lifting (hoist/crane) hooks have a larger throat with a spring-loaded safety gate and often swivel, accepting chain, rope, or straps; they appear on both ends of chain hoists and come-alongs. Source anchors: lifting hook; safety gate; swivel; hoist.
Analysis: The lifting hook's throat-and-gate design exists to accept arbitrary materials safely, and the swivel prevents twist loading. Recognizing them on chain hoists and come-alongs ties the connector taxonomy back to the heavy-moving tools. This is the connector class for overhead work. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Chains: Hooks: Choker

Summary: A choker (slip) hook has a smooth interior that lets the chain slide and cinch tight around a load, ideal for dragging logs; a logging chain with choker hook runs about $70. Source anchors: choker hook; slip; logs; $70.
Analysis: The choker's slide-and-cinch mechanism is what makes log dragging work without a special knot, and the logging-chain product is the ready-made version. This is the connector that turns the tractor into a skidder. It connects to the firewood chapters in volume 1. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Chains: Hooks: J

Summary: A J hook is a large fishhook-shaped grab for load rings, car frames, or lifting points; the book also sorts out the confusion with transit-style J (and R and T) hooks used to tie cars to flatbeds. For farm work Corcoran says skip the transit J hook and get the lifting J hook. Source anchors: J hook; transit hook; R hook; T hook; flatbed.
Analysis: The J-hook sidebar is the book at its taxonomy-best, untangling two meanings of the same word that could cause a wrong purchase. The advice to pick the long-shank lifting J for farm use over the transit J is the decisive consumer note. This is the connector chapter's vocabulary trap solved. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Chains: Slots and Crosses

Summary: Grab hooks bind chains by a slot that admits one link orientation but not the perpendicular neighbor; the same idea appears as slots cut in equipment edges (like his bolt-on forklift tines) and as cross-shaped chain holes in steel plates, where the perpendicular first and second links pass through and then lock. Source anchors: slot; cross hole; forklift tines; chain holes.
Analysis: The slot-and-cross geometry lesson explains a design pattern, once learned, appears everywhere in tractor implements and truck bumpers. It teaches the reader to recognize built-in chain-attach points rather than improvising. This is small-mechanical-systems thinking the book loves. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Chains: Load Binders

Summary: Load binders tension chains, needed for securing flatbed loads or mounting tools; ratchet binders (\(43) apply tension slowly and stay adjustable, while lever binders (\)23) cinch fast but can pop open under vibration and are only coarsely adjustable. Source anchors: load binder; ratchet; lever; $43; $23.
Analysis: The ratchet-versus-lever comparison gives the reader the two failure modes (vibration release, coarse adjustability) that decide the purchase. The recommended tie-the-lever-to-the-chain with baling wire is a cheap fix for the vibration problem. This is the tensioning toolkit for the use cases that follow. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Chains: Load Binders: Vibration

Summary: Lever binders hold tension by passing dead center, but vibration (a running tractor) can throw the lever back, loosening the whole chain; Corcoran's fix is tying the lever to the chain with baling wire. Source anchors: dead center; vibration; baling wire.
Analysis: The mechanical explanation of why lever binders self-release, plus the wire fix, both solves a real equipment failure and teaches the operator the mechanism. This is one of the most practical two-paragraph entries in the shop chapter. It prevents a potentially dangerous load shift. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Chains: Load Binders: Adjustability

Summary: Ratchet binders adjust in steps of a fraction of a chain link via a gear, while lever binders shorten by a fixed number of link lengths, forcing the operator to re-hook to an adjacent link when the slack does not fit — the granularity problem. Source anchors: ratchet; gear teeth; lever; link; granularity.
Analysis: The adjustability contrast explains the daily annoyance pattern of lever binders, which is why ratchet binders are worth the extra cost for frequent use. The 32-tooth example makes the mechanism concrete. This is the mechanical nuance that determines which binder you should buy. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Chains: Accessories

Summary: Chain accessories include slings (assemblies connecting multiple chains for lifting), load rings (weld-on anchor points), hoist rings (the subset that holds a device's weight when lifted), shackles (gyves, openable chain links), connecting links, quick links, and clevis pins. Corcoran uses connecting links to join gambrels to chains and clevis pins to attach hooks. Source anchors: sling; load ring; hoist ring; shackle; quick link; clevis pin.
Analysis: The accessories list is the connector glossary that finishes the hardware vocabulary, and each item's one-line definition maps to a specific assembly. The author's own usage (connecting links for gambrels, clevis pins for hooks) shows which matter daily. This is the parts-bin reference of the chain system. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Chains: Use Cases

Summary: Corcoran walks through worked use cases (drag a tree, chain two chains together, lift a pig for gutting, pull a wrecked car, lift a rototiller, secure a hoist to a gantry, lift a printing press), each solved with the right combination of choker, grab, J, and shackle, plus a vivid account of a 2,000-lb printing press rotating backward when the center of gravity sat behind the lift points. Source anchors: drag tree; pig carcass; wrecked car; printing press; center of gravity.
Analysis: The worked-use-case table is the chapter's best teaching device, showing the exact hardware combos for real farm jobs. The printing-press failure is the book's best lesson in center-of-gravity physics, with the tractor-assisted rescue as the recovery method. This closes the chains section by making the parts list operational. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Fasteners

Summary: Corcoran's practice is to buy full boxes of fasteners even when only a few are needed, building an inventory that eliminates hardware-store trips; he notes full boxes hide in the back of pull-out drawers at smaller hardware stores. Source anchors: full boxes; inventory; drawers.
Analysis: The buy-full-boxes practice is the inventory-for-legibility philosophy applied to nuts and bolts, converting downtime into preparedness. The hidden-boxes note is genuine retail knowledge. This sets up the deck-screw and bolt sections that give the practice content. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Fasteners: Deck Screws

Summary: Deck screws are Corcoran's default for utility woodwork: strong steel, a Torx T-25 bit that rarely slips, a free bit included, an unthreaded last inch that prevents bridging, self-tapping threads, rust resistance, and price. He inventories 1.5, 2, 3, and 5-inch lengths, and tells a long, funny story about a farmhand who insisted on using deck screws in structural asslambling against his nails preference, which ended with the opposite result. Source anchors: Torx T-25; unthreaded shank; self-tapping; lengths.
Analysis: The feature list gives the reader real reasons deck screws win for utility work, and the farmhand argument about nails-versus-screws is the book's best Chesterton-fence anecdote. The story models both the difficulty of delegating judgment and the price of exhaustion in a dispute. This is the screw chapter's memorable lesson about when tradition beats cleverness. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Fasteners: Bolts

Summary: Bolts are cheap, ubiquitous miracles used to assemble everything; their specs are diameter, length, thread pitch (TPI for SAE, mm for metric), and grade, and Corcoran recommends carrying a sample bolt to the store to match threads by interleaving. He notes for a given diameter there are coarse and fine pitches (e.g., 1/4-20s in 11 and 20 TPI variants). Source anchors: diameter; thread pitch; TPI; coarse; fine; sample bolt.
Analysis: The sample-bolt-to-the-store habit is the practical rule that eliminates the most common fastener mistake, and the coarse-versus-fine pitch explanation makes milli-specs legible. Bolt grade matters mainly for shear pins, which maps to the volume 1 tractor chapter. This is the fastener-section vocabulary core. This converts a messy domain into the measurable, actionable quantities that define Legibility.
