Workshop: Lubricants

Summary: Lubricants reduce friction and come in solid (graphite, Teflon), semi-solid (grease), and liquid (oil) forms; the workshop subsections cover grease and oil. Source anchors: graphite; grease; oil; friction.
Analysis: The three physical forms establish the taxonomy for the two substantial subsections, and each form maps to a class of application (pivot pins, bearings, thin oil). This is the one-paragraph frame for the grease and oil chapters. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Lubricants: Grease

Summary: Greased homestead surfaces include FEL pivot pins, mower wheels, spreader axles, wheelbarrows, snowblower spindles, and PTO shafts. Corcoran says one or two grease types cover all needs: general-purpose/moly (\(4/tube), high-temperature (\)5), and synthetic ($10), in 15-oz tubes (the homestead size) to 55-gallon drums. Source anchors: grease; pivot pins; moly; high temperature; 15 oz tube.
Analysis: The practical advice is that one general-purpose tube covers the fleet, with a high-temperature tube as the only real second. The tube-versus-drum sizes stop readers from overbuying. This is the lubricant chapter's legibility core: the fleet has a short, cheap grease list. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Lubricants: Grease: Applying

Summary: Grease is applied by hand with a brush to accessible surfaces, or via a grease gun through zerk fittings (named for inventor Oscar Ulysses Zerk, 1878–1968), where fresh grease displaces the old, which is wiped off because it attracts abrasive dust. Source anchors: brush; grease gun; zerk; displaced; wipe.
Analysis: Explaining what a zerk is and why you wipe off the purged grease converts the grease-gun ritual into understood mechanics. The inventor's name and dates add the book's signature trivia. This sets up the grease-gun sections. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Lubricants: Grease: Grease Guns

Summary: Grease guns come in manual, mini, pneumatic, cordless, air-pump, and chainsaw types, and Corcoran recommends a simple manual lever gun (\(25) for everything except chainsaws, plus an aftermarket LockNLube coupler (\)30) that he says is better than the stock one — buy it, even though it costs more than the gun. Source anchors: manual grease gun (\(25); LockNLube (\)30); coupler.
Analysis: The LockNLube recommendation is one of the book's most decisive product calls, backed by the claim that the stock coupler is poorly designed. Keeping the gun manual (cheap, no complications) fits the book's anti-gadget doctrine. This is the lubrication workhorse selection. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Lubricants: Grease: Grease Guns: How They Work

Summary: A grease gun works via a hand lever driving a high-pressure pump, with a spring-loaded follower disk sliding down the grease tube; the T-handle retracts the follower to load, and the shaft passes through a hole in the follower so pushing forward buries the shaft without forcing grease out. Source anchors: lever; pump; follower; T-handle; retract.
Analysis: The follower-and-passthrough-shaft mechanism is exactly the sort of hidden design detail that makes loading confusing, and explaining it removes the mystery. This is the mechanical-engineering explanation before the loading procedure. It turns a frustrating first 20 minutes into a one-time lesson. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Lubricants: Grease: Grease Guns: Loading

Summary: Loading a grease gun: pull the T-handle back to lock the follower, unscrew the head, remove the old tube, insert the new tube cardboard-end first, remove the metal cap, screw the head on fully, release the lock, and push the T-handle forward; then bleed the gun until grease appears. Source anchors: T-handle; cardboard end; metal cap; bleed; screw fully.
Analysis: The step-by-step loading procedure, with its two failure warnings (cross-threading can ruin the gun, and half-screwing the head pops when released), is exactly the kind of process this book delivers best. The cardboard-end-first mnemonic (the metal rim is too wide to fit first) makes assembly self-checking. This is a rare example of a shop chore fully documented. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Lubricants: Grease: Grease Guns: Using

Summary: To grease with a conventional coupler: clean the zerk, push the coupler on until it clicks, pump until fresh grease exits the far end, then tilt 25 degrees to release; the LockNLube uses a thumb lever instead. Clogged zerks give extreme resistance and need cleaning or replacement. Source anchors: clean zerk; click; fresh grease; tilt; LockNLube.
Analysis: The pump-until-fresh-grease-exits rule is the correct endpoint that both greases and purges, and the click-and-tilt mechanics make coupler use repeatable. The clogged-zerk diagnostic connects forward to the cleaning subsection. This is the daily-use procedure for the grease fleet. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Lubricants: Grease: Zerk Fittings

Summary: Zerk fittings can shear off and need replacement, or clog with dried grease and need cleaning. Source anchors: zerk; shear; clog.
Analysis: The two failure modes frame the two subsections that follow, and the either-or diagnosis is all the owner needs. Zerk maintenance is a tiny, recurring chore with outsized consequences for equipment life. This is the connector chapter's smallest but most frequent repair. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Lubricants: Grease: Zerk Fittings: Replacements

Summary: Zerk fittings come in 1/4-28 (straight, 45, or 90 degree), 1/8-27, and metric M6/M10 threads, and buying an assortment (\(20 SAE or metric) keeps replacements on hand before they are needed. Source anchors: 1/4-28; 45 degree; 90 degree; assortment (\)20).

Workshop: Lubricants: Grease: Zerk Fittings: Cleaning

Summary: A clogged zerk is cleared with a zerk cleaner (\(40) filled with penetrating oil, attached to the fitting and tapped with a hammer until the oil empties through. Source anchors: zerk cleaner (\)40); penetrating oil; tap; clear.
Analysis: The zerk cleaner's tool-and-oil-tap procedure is a specific, mechanical rescue for a part that otherwise gets replaced, and the hammer-tap mechanism cannot mislead the operator. Offering the cleaner as the alternative to replacement keeps the fleet moving cheaply. This is the repair-before-replace doctrine at its smallest scale. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Lubricants: Oil

Summary: Oils are liquid lubricants used in three ways: staying in a tool, being burned in two-stroke engines, and transmitting hydraulic force. This section covers only the first; stocking a few types covers all homestead needs, with the main difference being viscosity. Source anchors: oil; two-stroke; hydraulic; viscosity.
Analysis: The three-use taxonomy keeps the chapters clean (this one covers tools only), and the viscosity note previews the engine-oil section. Stocking few types is the legibility theme of the whole lubricant chapter. This is the frame for the penetrating, workshop, engine, and inventory subsections. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Lubricants: Oil: Penetrating Oils

Summary: Penetrating oils are the thinnest, designed to wick into cracks to free stuck screws, but they evaporate quickly and should be followed by a thicker lubricant; WD-40 is the classic example. Source anchors: penetrating oil; wick; WD-40; evaporate.
Analysis: The wick-then-evaporate explanation gives penetrating oils their correct role (disassembly helper, not lasting lubricant). Pairing them with a follow-up thicker oil prevents the stripped-fastener-cycle. This is the oil chapter's first, simplest distinction. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Lubricants: Oil: Workshop Oil

Summary: Workshop oil serves as cutting-and-cooling fluid, rust protection, and pivot lubrication; Corcoran uses a single jug of 3M spindle oil ($40) in several cheap oil cans for everything, a choice he admits is offbeat but shares with old hands who use engine or gear oil instead. Source anchors: spindle oil; cutting fluid; pivot; oil cans.
Analysis: One-jug-for-everything is the legibility victory of the oil chapter, and the admission that engine oil works too keeps the advice flexible. Placing oil cans at each station (bench, drill press) makes lubrication part of the workflow, not a separate trip. This is the anti-proliferation doctrine applied to oil. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Lubricants: Oil: Engine Oils

Summary: The SAE rating describes oil viscosity at 32°F and 212°F; single grades run 0W to 60, the W means winter-grade testing, and multi-grades like 10W-30 behave like a thin oil cold and a 30 oil hot, so lower-first-number = colder use and higher-second-number = hotter use. Gear oils use the separate SAE J306 scale, not directly comparable. Source anchors: SAE; 10W-30; winter; J306; gear oil.
Analysis: The multi-grade explanation is the book's cleanest translation of a confusing label into a use rule, and the gear-oil-scale warning prevents cross-spec mistakes. Knowing grades lets a reader substitute (SAE 20 with 10W-30) safely. This is the engine-oil literacy that makes inventory simplification possible. This converts a messy domain into the measurable, actionable quantities that define Legibility.

Workshop: Lubricants: Oil: Recommended Inventory

Summary: Corcoran winnowed his oil inventory to three items bought in 5-gallon jugs: 15W-40 engine oil (\(55), 85W-140 gear oil (\)62), and universal tractor transmission/hydraulic fluid ($37), covering tractor, mower, UTV, car, brushhog, and rototiller. Source anchors: 15W-40; 85W-140; trans/hydraulic; 5 gallon jugs.
Analysis: The three-oil inventory is the chapter's payoff: a whole fleet covered by three 5-gallon jugs, with bulk pricing and less storage clutter. Working through the full requirement list once, then simplifying, is the method that eliminates the outliers. This is the lubricant chapter's definitive conclusion. This converts a messy domain into the measurable, actionable quantities that define Legibility.

Workshop: Fuels

Summary: Tractors, mowers, ATVs, chainsaws, and farm trucks need several distinct fuels, and the fuels section covers types, containers, transfer tanks, diesel tax, two-stroke mix, additives, propane, and kerosene. Source anchors: gasoline; diesel; 2-stroke mix; kerosene; propane.

The chapter's breadth reflects that a homestead runs on multiple fuels. This overview maps the subsections. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Fuels: Fuel Types

Summary: The fuel types are gasoline, diesel, 2-stroke mix, and kerosene, each with a standard container color (red gasoline, yellow diesel, green 2-stroke mix, blue kerosene) in conformity with OSHA-style labeling, and using the wrong fuel is dangerous (gasoline in a kerosene heater can explode). Source anchors: red; yellow; green; blue; kerosene.
Analysis: The color code is the proprio-safety system of the fuel chapter, and the wrong-fuel consequence list explains why the standardization exists. Treating the colors as an emerging standard rather than a regulation is characteristic precision. This is the classification that protects the fleet. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Fuels: Portable Fuel Containers

Summary: Fuel tanks come in 1 to 5 gallons; Corcoran prefers 5-gallon metal cans and endorses the Justrite AccuFlow II ($70) for its solid metal, galvanized finish, high flow, intuitive safety mechanism, spark arrestor, and squat stability, despite its \(60–80 price. He has six. EPA 2007 regulations made most cans terrible, which is why the good ones stand out. Source anchors: 5 gallon; Justrite AccuFlow II (\)70); 1911 safety; spark arrestor.
Analysis: The Justrite endorsement is one of the book's strongest product reviews, with the 1911-pistol safety mechanism as the vivid comparison, and the six-can fleet shows the scale of a serious homestead fuel operation. The EPA-regulation history explains why good cans are rare and worth paying for. This is the fuel-container shopping decision settled. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Workshop: Fuels: Transfer Tanks

Summary: Transfer tanks (30–100 gallons, \(200–\)400 without pump, \(600–\)1,100 with DC pump) sit in a truck bed or trailer to move fuel from the station to the farm in bulk; auxiliary tanks are permanently installed range extenders, not relevant to most homesteads. Source anchors: transfer tank; 30-100 gallons; $600-1,100; pump.
Analysis: The transfer-tank option is priced but not recommended for everyone, and its purpose (buying fuel in bulk when distances are long) is clearly defined. The distinction from auxiliary tanks prevents a wrong purchase. This is the fuel-volume escalation step. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Workshop: Fuels: Diesel Tax

Summary: On-road diesel carries federal and state taxes (federal about 24.4 cents, NH state 19.6, ranging state-to-state 7.5 to 51 cents); off-road diesel is sold untaxed but must be dyed red, with $1,000 fines for road use. Corcoran's local supplier would deliver 100 gallons at about $2.84 versus $3.07 at the station, but the $600 transfer-tank payback takes four to twelve years depending on assumptions, so he still drives to the station. Source anchors: federal tax; red dye; $1,000 fine; $0.23 savings; transfer tank payback.
Analysis: The diesel-tax subsection works through a real investment decision (transfer tank versus trips to the station), including the opportunity-cost alternatives, and ends by declining the purchase — the book modeling non-purchase. The red-dye enforcement explains the market structure. This is economic reasoning applied to fuel logistics. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Workshop: Fuels: 2-Stroke Mix

Summary: Two-stroke engines burn gasoline mixed with lubricating oil, usually 40:1 or 50:1; running without oil seizes the engine. Corcoran standardizes on 40:1 because it is easier to mix (one 16-oz bottle of oil per 5-gallon tank) and slightly richer mixes protect engines. Source anchors: 40:1; 50:1; 16 oz; 5 gallons.
Analysis: The choose-a-ratio-and-standardize advice removes the daily decision, and the oil-rich bias is a deliberate longevity trade accepted over minor smoke. The 16-oz-per-5-gallon mnemonic makes mixing mistake-proof. This is the fuel chapter's most-used rule. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Fuels: 2-Stroke Mix: Engine Design

Summary: Two-stroke engines use oil mixed into fuel because they are often hand-held and operated in arbitrary orientations, so a recirculating oil pan system is impossible; the oil rides with the fuel into the crankcase and then the cylinder. Ratios like 32:1 and 20:1 were phased out by EPA rules capping oil above 40:1. Source anchors: 2-stroke; hand-held; oil-in-fuel; EPA; 40:1.
Analysis: The orientation constraint is the real engineering reason for oil-in-fuel, and the EPA history explains why modern cans all say 40:1 or 50:1. Understanding the design prevents the cardinal error of running a two-stroke dry. This is the mechanism behind the mixing rule. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Fuels: 2-Stroke Mix: Premix Vs You-Mix

Summary: Premixed 2-stroke fuel costs about $36/gallon, versus about $3.87/gallon when mixing your own 5-gallon batches (16-oz oil at $5), saving \(150+/year at 5 gallons; Corcoran keeps one premix can for its longer shelf life and as a diagnostic for a fouled engine. Source anchors: premix (\)36/gal); self-mix ($3.87); $150 saved; diagnostic.
Analysis: The 10x price difference plus the rate of chainsaw use (over 20 gallons some years) makes self-mixing a no-brainer, and keeping one premix can as a calibrated diagnostic is a subtle testing tool. The price math is the chapter's strongest argument. This is cost-saving with a safety backup. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Workshop: Fuels: 2-Stroke Mix: Mixing

Summary: The mixing procedure: fill the tank half full, add the oil, shake, then top off; for 40:1, add one 16-oz bottle to 5 gallons. The author's ratio choice is 40:1 across all tools even when manuals say 50:1. Source anchors: half full; add oil; shake; 40:1.
Analysis: The half-fill-then-shake method prevents the oil-only-first emulsion problem, and the uniform 40:1 choice means one mixing rule for the whole fleet. The slight oil-rich bias is an explicit longevity trade. This is the mixing standard that prevents the wrong-fuel error. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Fuels: 2-Stroke Mix: Trouble Shooting Bad Mixes

Summary: If unsure whether a can already has oil, add more (better over-oiled than run dry), since excess oil only smokes while too little destroys the engine. Accidentally using 4-stroke oil can be diluted with gasoline and correct 2-stroke oil; a fouled spark plug suffices to diagnose a rich-mix no-start. Source anchors: add more oil; smoke; seize; 4-stroke oil; spark plug.
Analysis: The direction-of-error rule (over-oil is survivable, under-oil destroys) is the safety principle of the whole subsection, and it explains the dusty edge-case advice that follows. The spark-plug fouling check is a quick diagnostic. This is failure analysis for the most common small-engine problem. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Fuels: Additives

Summary: Two additives matter on a homestead: gasoline stabilizers (Sta-Bil, $9) that extend gasoline life to about 24 months versus the 3–6 month varnish timeline, and diesel anti-gel compounds (Hot Shot's Secret, \(16) that lower diesel's 10–15°F gel point to as low as −60°F. Source anchors: Sta-Bil (\)9); varnish; anti-gel; gel point.
Analysis: The additive chapter gives each chemical its exact job with a price, and the gasoline-varnish timeline explains why stored fuel goes bad. Anti-gel is the northern-climate hedge for diesel traction. This is the fuel-preservation toolkit, priced. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Fuels: Propane

Summary: Propane is a three-carbon gas that liquefies under pressure, powering ranges, generators, grills, flame weeders, blacksmith forges, oxy-acetylene substitutes, and turkey fryers. Source anchors: propane; ranges; generators; forge; turkey fryers.
Analysis: Listing propane's many homestead uses shows why it is the fuel chapter's versatile centerpiece. The pressure-liquefaction note grounds the whole tank discussion. This overview connects propane to blacksmithing, welding, and poultry cooking. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Fuels: Propane: Tank Sizes

Summary: Propane tanks range from the 20-lb BBQ size (17 lbs empty, 37 lbs full, identified by the TW tare weight) through 500 and 1,000 gallons; 15-lb retail fills in 20-lb tanks (Blue Rhino) give 25% less run time than a full fill, so check both price and fill amount. Source anchors: 20 lb; 500 gallon; tare weight; Blue Rhino; 15 lb.
Analysis: The tare-weight explanation (TW stamped on the collar) lets a buyer verify actual fuel quantity, and the 15-in-20 Blue Rhino warning is a subtle retail trap exposed. Multiple 20-lb tanks prevent mid-project fuel swaps. This is the tank-shopping legibility section. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Fuels: Propane: Refilling

Summary: Refilling a 20-lb tank at a hardware store costs $12 (about $0.60/lb) versus a $21 Blue Rhino swap (about $1.40/lb for 15 lbs); the swap's hidden value is accepting expired tanks beyond their 15-year code date for free. Corcoran uses both, trading price for convenient off-hours availability. Source anchors: refill $12; swap $21; 15 year date; bundling.
Analysis: The per-pound pricing shows exactly when to swap versus refill, and the expired-tank acceptance converts the pricey swap into the cheapest legal tank replacement. The bundling analysis (price plus availability plus disposal) is mature consumer thinking. This is the propane procurement decision made legible. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Workshop: Fuels: Propane: Safety Devices

Workshop: Fuels: Kerosene

Summary: Kerosene is used in space heaters and is stored separately, with its blue container color, because mistaking it for other fuels is dangerous. Source anchors: kerosene; space heaters; blue.

Workshop: Rust

Summary: Rust is the oxidation of iron, where iron oxide molecules are larger than the iron they replace, so they peel away and expose fresh metal to repeat the process. Corcoran explains why farms rust (steel is everywhere, nothing is climate-controlled, and use scrapes paint) and says he now spends more time fighting rust than in his entire pre-farm adulthood. Source anchors: oxidation; iron oxide; peel; steel; paint scraped.
Analysis: The peel-and-repeat mechanism is the true explanation of why rust eats metal rather than sealing it, and it grounds every prevention and removal method that follows. The farm-rusts-for-three-reasons list is the honest framing of the maintenance burden. This is the chapter's physics foundation. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Rust: Prevention

Summary: Rust prevention is any method that keeps iron, air, and water apart: dehumidification, paint, oil, or non-oil coatings. Corcoran describes dehumidifier drain tricks (drilling the bucket's hose fitting, sump pumps, draining into gravel below a slab), and for bare tools favors oil or Boeshield T-9 (\(18) for large surfaces and camphor blocks (\)5) for tool chests. He paints weldments with primer plus two coats, and recommends color-matched touch-up for tractor brands as an option. Source anchors: dehumidifier; paint; oil; Boeshield T-9; camphor.
Analysis: The keep-them-apart framing makes prevention a design choice, not a battle, and the dehumidifier drainage hacks are exactly the practical details that make the plan work in a shop. Camphor's vapor-protection mechanism is a charming, little-known tool-preservation trick. This is the prevention layer that cuts future removal work. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Rust: Disassembly of Rusted Items

Summary: Rusted fasteners come apart with penetrating oil (the classic 50/50 acetone and ATF mix outperformed commercial brands in a Home Shop Machinist test), heat (on the nut, not the bolt), and the careful tighten-loosen-tighten dance that clears debris from threads; final options include nut breakers and drilling out the bolt. Source anchors: penetrating oil; acetone/ATF; heat the nut; tighten-loosen; nut breaker.
Analysis: The acetone-and-ATF homebrew is one of the book's most useful secrets, backed by a magazine comparison, and the two-steps-forward-one-back procedure prevents re-jamming rusted threads. The heat-on-the-nut rule (expansion opens the hole) is a real materials-science insight. This is the disassembly toolkit fo self-reliant repair. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Rust: Removal

Summary: Rust removal methods are grinding, sandblasting, chemical conversion, chemical removal, and electrical conversion, each suited to different surfaces and scales. Source anchors: grinding; sandblasting; chemical conversion; chemical removal; electrical conversion.
Analysis: The five-method taxonomy gives the reader a decision tree by surface size and sensitivity, and each gets its own subsection. The order also reflects escalating investment and equipment needs. This overview is the removal playbook. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Rust: Removal: Grinding

Summary: A 4-inch angle grinder (\(63) with a wire cup (\)12) is the fastest mechanical rust removal, far faster than a drill-mounted wheel; Corcoran keeps several grinders with different disks mounted. Source anchors: 4-inch grinder ($63); wire cup; drill comparison.
Analysis: The grinder-versus-drill speed comparison settles the tool choice instantly, and keeping one grinder per disk (grind, wire, sand) removes the swap-time tax. The $75 entry price makes this the default removal method. This is the mechanical-first option. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Rust: Removal: Sand Blasting

Summary: Sandblasting shoots abrasive media at rust, using a compressor and a cabinet for indoor work, with a setup costing about $1,200 (cabinet $875, media $40, compressor $300). Corcoran advises skipping it until really needed and saving \(1,100 with a grinder. Source anchors: sand blasting; media; cabinet (\)875); compressor ($300); $1,200.
Analysis: The $1,200 price tag versus the $75 grinder is the chapter's clearest avoid-the-purchase analysis, and the media-type spectrum (cutting to soda) explains the method's range. The skip-it-until-needed advice matches the tool-utility doctrine. This is rust removal priced out of the default path. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Rust: Removal: Chemical Conversion

Summary: Chemical conversion turns red rust into hard black iron tannate that does not propagate, using a product like Rust-Oleum Rust Reformer (\(6) after mechanically removing loose rust, then painting over it. Source anchors: iron tannate; Rust Reformer (\)6); topcoat.
Analysis: The convert-instead-of-remove trick works on painted equipment where grinding is impractical, and the hard-black product both stops propagation and readies the surface for paint. The $6 cost versus an hour of grinding is the economics. This is the cheapest full-panel treatment. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Rust: Removal: Chemical Removal

Summary: Chemical removal dissolves rust by dipping the part in Evapo-Rust ($23), which Corcoran has not used but which trusted sources recommend. Source anchors: Evapo-Rust; dip; dissolve.
Analysis: The dipping method is the cleanest way to save badly rusted small parts, and the honest I-have-not-used-but-sources-recommend disclaimer maintains the book's evidentiary standards. The tank-style use makes it the go-to for hardware restoration. This is chemical removal in one line plus endorsements. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Rust: Removal: Electrical Conversion

Summary: Electrical conversion (electrolysis) turns red rust into black Fe3O4 using a battery charger, washing soda, and sacrificial rebar anodes in a water bath; about $50 for the charger plus $21 for soda. Corcoran has built and used a tank successfully, with a warning not to use a jumper pack as the supply. Source anchors: electrolysis; Fe3O4; washing soda; rebar; charger.
Analysis: The home-electrolysis tank is the book's peak frontier-systems rebuild, converting a bucket, soda, and a charger into a rust-removal bath that preserves the base metal. The jumper-pack warning adds a real electrical-safety note. This is the method for parts that deserve non-abrasive recovery. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Rust: Removal: My Experience

Summary: Corcoran has used grinder-and-wire-cup for heavy rust, Rust Reformer, and an electrolytic tank, reporting all three work well and belong in the arsenal. Source anchors: grinders; Rust reformer; electrolytic tank.

Workshop: Painting

Summary: Paint is applied as a liquid that dries to a solid film for protection and aesthetics, and the chapter covers paint types and application methods, with the standard protocol being clean, one coat of primer, two coats of paint. Source anchors: paint; pigment; binder; solvent; primer.
Analysis: The pigment/binder/solvent anatomy explains why primer exists (binding) and why two top coats are the norm, making paint application feel engineered. The clean-primer-two-coats protocol is the checklist every application section serves. This is the painting chapter's chemistry foundation. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Painting: Types of Paint

Summary: The common homestead paint types are latex/acrylic (water solvent), oil/enamel (petroleum solvent), epoxy (two-part resin/hardener), and milk paint (casein binder), defined the way hardware stores sell them rather than by chemical strictness. Source anchors: latex; oil; epoxy; milk paint.
Analysis: The four-type list with the honest categorization caveat gives the reader the shopping-aisle vocabulary, and the footnote that epoxy cures by cross-linking rather than evaporation (a coating, not a true paint) preserves the chemistry without confusing the selection. This overview sets up the five type subsections. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Painting: Types of Paint: Latex / Acrylic

Summary: Latex/acrylic paints are the easiest to work with, clean up with soap and water, and give off no fumes, but have medium-to-low resistance to abrasion and sun, making them the right indoor choice. Source anchors: latex; easy cleanup; indoor; abrasion.
Analysis: The ease-and-cleanup advantages versus wear limits is the honest trade that assigns latex to interior work. The water-cleanup property makes it the beginner-safe category. This is the default-for-indoor paint. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Painting: Types of Paint: Oil

Summary: Oil and enamel paints dry harder, resist wear and temperature better, and are the default outdoor choice, thinned and cleaned with mineral spirits, covering about 400 square feet per gallon; Corcoran uses two coats of Rust-Oleum oil enamel ($30/gal) over a red-oxide primer for tractor and vineyard steel. Source anchors: oil enamel; outdoor; mineral spirits; 400 ft2; Rust-Oleum.
Analysis: The harder-surface-for-wear trade and the 400 ft2/gal coverage law give the reader both the property and the buying math. The Rust-Oleum application on welded tractor additions is the endorsed real-world use. This is the outdoor steel painting standard. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Painting: Types of Paint: Epoxy

Summary: Epoxy paint is the strongest, most wear-resistant surface but also the most expensive and demanding: two parts must be mixed in precise ratios in small batches (the exothermic reaction can run away), it cannot be thinned or sprayed, and overspray cannot be dissolved; garage-floor epoxies are fine on a manure wagon. Corcoran denounces one-part epoxy as marketing lie, since true epoxies are definitionally two-part (the one-part product is latex with epoxy grit). Source anchors: epoxy; resin; hardener; exothermic; one-part lie.
Analysis: The exothermic-runaway warning and the no-thin-no-spray limits make epoxy the expert-tier paint, and the one-part-epoxy debunk is the book's best industrial-myth correction. The manure-wagon note keeps the recommendation practical. This is the paint chapter's aspirational tier, honestly priced in difficulty. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Painting: Types of Paint: Milk Paint

Summary: Milk paint is a non-toxic powder paint for the colonial and Shaker aesthetic, mixed 1:1 with water, covering 35–300 square feet per pint-quart-gallon at \(15/\)22/$30; Corcoran recommends Old Fashioned Milk Paint Company, straining the mixed paint to remove undissolved nubs, applying two to three coats, and topping with tung oil. Source anchors: milk paint; powder; Old Fashioned; strain; tung oil.
Analysis: The milk paint section is the book's aesthetic-craft favorite, with real specifics (strain to remove nubs, multiple fast-drying coats, tung-oil topcoat) that de-mystify the finish. The non-toxic and forever-stores properties make it a homestead-friendly choice. This is the decorative tier fully documented. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Painting: Types of Paint: Tung Oil and Danish Oil

Summary: Tung oil is a drying oil that polymerizes in air into a matte, water-resistant finish, applied by flooding and re-coating; pure tung oil is non-toxic and food-safe, while boiled tung oil adds drying agents and tung-oil finish is an undefined blend. Corcoran prefers pure tung oil, rejects Danish oil (a meaningless term for a varnish-oil mix that gave him a bad finish), recommends Rockler and Milk Paint brand tung oils, and warns that oiled rags can spontaneously combust. Source anchors: tung oil; polymerize; food-safe; Danish oil; rags combust.
Analysis: The polymerize-in-air mechanism is what separates drying oils from non-drying ones, and the warning that Danish oil means anything is a genuinely useful label-skepticism lesson. The spontaneous-combustion rag warning is a real safety note with a proper disposal method. This is the wood-finishing chapter's core. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Painting: Types of Paint: Polyurethane

Summary: Polyurethane cures by cross-linking into a hard synthetic finish, builds faster than tung oil, and is the right tabletop choice; oil-based dries harder than water-based, and Corcoran favors Minwax Clear Satin oil-based ($62/gal). Source anchors: polyurethane; cross-link; tabletop; Minwax.
Analysis: The build-fast-and-hard-versus-less-organic trade is set against tung oil, giving the reader a two-finish decision rather than a single right answer. The oil-based-versus-water-based note and the favorite-brand pick finish the recommendation. This is the durability tier for high-use surfaces. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Painting: Application: Brush

Summary: Brushes apply paint from a cup, avoid overspray, and are cheap, but cheap brushes shed bristles; Corcoran recommends a Purdy 3-inch (\(16), cleaning with solvent in a three-jar reuse system, storing dirty brushes bagged in the fridge overnight, and shaping and paper-wrapping clean ones to dry. Source anchors: paint cup; Purdy (\)16); three-jar solvent; fridge storage.
Analysis: The three-jar solvent cascade is an elegant cleanup technique, and the fridge-storage trick saves the cleaning cycle between coats. The disposable-gloves tip and the premium-brush-justifies-its-price claim represent the tool-quality doctrine. This is the painting method most homesteaders will use most. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Painting: Application: Roller

Summary: Rollers give smoother, cleaner results without brush marks, using a tray and tray liner, a 9-inch roller with 3/8-inch nap, and a brush for corners; dirty rollers store bagged in the fridge between coats. Source anchors: tray; 9 inch; 3/8 nap; liner.
Analysis: The roller-and-brush combination (roller for flats, brush for details) is the standard pairing, and the cheap tray liners and bagged-in-fridge storage keep cleanup minimal. The 9-inch and 3/8-inch specs are the only numbers needed. This is the smooth-surface application method. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Painting: Application: Spray

Summary: Spray guns cover thin, curved surfaces fastest; HVLP guns move low-pressure high-volume air that deposits 65% of paint versus 40–50% for conventional, need no compressor, and are favored by Corcoran (a Rockler HVLP at \(120) who also outlines conventional guns for those who already own a compressor. Both need cleaning and can use cup liners. Source anchors: HVLP; 65%; conventional; Rockler (\)120); cleaning.
Analysis: The 65-versus-40–50% transfer-efficiency statistic is the deciding number between HVLP and conventional, and the no-compressor requirement makes HVLP the simpler entry. The cleaning kit and cup-liner tips keep the process maintainable. This is the application method for trellises and tractor buckets. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Sharpening

Workshop: Blacksmithing

Summary: Blacksmithing is not required for modern homesteading but is a fun, occasionally useful hobby (Corcoran forged S-hooks and a T-post pounder bracket). Source anchors: forge; anvil; hammer; tongs; Prospect Hill Forge; Hay Budden. Analysis: The four-tool minimum and the used-anvil path (\)500+ old, $900 new, railroad-rail free) make blacksmithing approachable and keep the mystique low. The propane-versus-coal trade (cleaner but cooler) is the equipment choice framed honestly. The self-made-tools-from-tongs story is the bootstrap logic the book celebrates. Workshop: Welding: Types of Welding

Summary: The four welding technologies are stick (SMAW, cheapest, rod variety), MIG (easiest, spool-fed wire plus shielding gas), TIG (fattest control, thin metal, two hands), and oxy-acetylene (chemical, now mostly for cutting and heating). Corcoran does stick and MIG, prefers MIG, and has an oxy-acetylene rig for heating and cutting. Source anchors: stick; MIG; TIG; oxy-acetylene; shielding gas.
Analysis: The four-type comparison with its advantages and disadvantages is the selection table of the chapter, and the flux-core note (MIG without gas) is a useful budget variant. The author's own stack (MIG plus oxy-acetylene) models the practical default. This is the vocabulary for every welding discussion that follows. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Generator-welders' extra outlets are the bonus that justifies their price for mobile users. This is the power platform decision. Workshop: Welding: Welder Brands and Price Points: Low Tier

Workshop: Welding: Welder Brands and Price Points: Mid Tier

Workshop: Welding: Welder Brands and Price Points: Pro-Tier

Workshop: Welding: Cylinders

Summary: Welding gases come in cylinder types with incompatible threads by design (acetylene, oxygen, argon, propane, MAPP), and the typical workshop needs a high-pressure steel tank for shielding gas, an oxygen tank, an acetylene tank, and disposable blue/yellow cylinders. Cylinders carry a certification stamp that expires after 10 years, requiring recertification or removal from service. Source anchors: cylinders; acetylene; oxygen; certification; 10 years.
Analysis: The thread-incompatibility design (making dangerous cross-connections impossible) is the safety principle of the whole cylinder system, and the 10-year certification cycle is a real expiry the reader must track. The medical-oxygen-can-be-filled-from-welding-oxygen note foreshadows the Health chapter. This is the gas-supply vocabulary. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Welding: Masking Gas

Summary: MIG and TIG need a shielding gas, typically 25% argon / 75% CO2, in high-pressure cylinders sold by multiple confusing naming schemes (cubic feet, ordinal numbers, letter codes). Cylinders can be owned or rented; Corcoran's supplier charges \(123–\)195 to buy 20–60 ft3 and about $50/year to rent, so the crossover is 2.5–4 years and he recommends buying. Refills run about $50 with a $20 delivery fee. Source anchors: 25/75 mix; cubic feet; buy vs rent; crossover 2.5-4 years.
Analysis: The chaotic cylinder-naming schemes are a real market failure, and the failsafe recommendation to use cubic feet is the resolution. The buy-versus-rent crossover math (2.5–4 years) is the book's signature capital decision. This is the gas-purchase legibility section. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Welding: Why You Can't Rent Large Cylinders

Summary: Divides the cylinder market structure: welding-supply houses rented tanks to customers and collected rent at refill time, but third-party swappers started stealing inventory, and the 2005 Airgas versus Scott-Gross lawsuit (settled 2009) forced suppliers to stop taking cylinders with foreign headstamps, so most now refuse to sell over 60 ft3. Corcoran's action item: be wary of any cylinder stamped with another firm's name. Source anchors: rental; third party swaps; Airgas; Scott-Gross; 60 ft3.
Analysis: The lawsuit-and-inventory economics is a genuinely fascinating industrial story that explains a market quirk (why you cannot buy big tanks), and the stamped-tank warning is the practical takeaway. This is the book's informal-institutions insight applied to gas supply. The history makes the rule memorable. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Welding: My Thoughts and Experience

Summary: Corcoran bought a Hobart Handler 175 (roughly the $800 210 MIG tier) and has been happy for almost 20 years, rebutting each theoretical drawback: it is not transportable (he never welds in the field), only 30% duty cycle (he spends 90% of time cutting and clamping), and max 3/8-inch penetration (he welds thicker from both sides or adds a reinforcing plate). Source anchors: Hobart Handler 175; duty cycle; penetration; both sides.
Analysis: The confession-and-rebuttal format is the book's most honest product review, directly modeling how a mid-tier tool covered the author's real needs. The double-sided weld and reinforcing-plate workarounds are genuinely useful techniques. This is the chapter's fieldwork evidence for preferring mid-tier. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Welding: Other Tools: Cutting

Workshop: Welding: Other Tools: Cutting: Hacksaw

Summary: The humble hacksaw can cut almost anything; blade selection runs by TPI, and the rule of thumb is that three teeth should be engaged in the workpiece at once (a 1-inch board wants 3 TPI, a 1/4-inch steel piece wants 12 TPI). Corcoran explains the reasoning via vibration and gullet capacity, and recommends multiple colored hacksaws, each with a different blade. Source anchors: hacksaw; TPI; three teeth; gullet; colored frames.
Analysis: The three-teeth rule and its vibration/gullet reasoning is the most genuinely instructive paragraph in the cutting section, converting blade choice from folklore to physics. Multiple color-coded saws end the swap-and-forget problem. This is cheap tools used with knowledge. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Compressors and Pneumatic Tools: Hoses

Summary: Pneumatic hoses come coiled or straight; coiled hoses are tidy and flexible but have small internal diameters and fragile walls, while 1/2-inch straight hose delivers about 6 times the CFM of 1/4-inch, essential for tire inflation. Corcoran uses coiled for low-CFM staplers and straight for tires, with manual and retractable reels for storage. Source anchors: coiled; straight; CFM; 1/2 inch; reel.
Analysis: The CFM-versus-diameter relationship is the deciding physics for hose choice, and the fragile-coiled-hose failure (cracks at the fitting, fixable by cutting the end) is a real repair. Matching hose to tool (coiled to staplers, straight to tires) is the selection rule. This is the air-delivery decision. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Small Engines

Summary: Small engines power chainsaws, weed whackers, mowers, tractors, and snowblowers, and knowing how they work and how to troubleshoot them is worth the effort. Source anchors: small engines; chainsaws; mowers; troubleshooting.
Analysis: The small-engine chapter is the repair backbone of the vehicle fleet, and its principle-of-operation and troubleshooting structure makes diagnosing a no-start a systematic exercise. This is the knowledge layer that keeps the whole yard running. The overview frames the two big subsections. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Small Engines: Principle Of Operation

Summary: All internal combustion engines inject air-fuel mixture, compress it, trigger combustion, and use the expanding gas to drive a piston, with the homestead engines being 4-stroke gasoline, 2-stroke gasoline, and diesel. Source anchors: internal combustion; compress; combustion; piston.
Analysis: The four-step cycle description (inject, compress, ignite, expand) is the shared skeleton that makes the three engine types legible as variations. This overview sets up the per-type subsections. It is the engine literacy every owner needs. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Small Engines: Principle Of Operation: 4-Stroke Gasoline

Summary: The 4-stroke engine's cycle is intake, compression, combustion, and exhaust, with a carburetor mixing air and fuel and a magneto or battery powering the spark plug; multiple cylinders phase the strokes so one piston captures energy while others prepare. Source anchors: intake; compression; combustion; exhaust; carburetor; magneto.
Analysis: The four-stroke cycle is the reference model for the whole chapter, and the phase-offset multiple-cylinder explanation reveals why engines run smoothly. The carburetor and magneto details map directly to the troubleshooting subsections. This is the baseline engine literacy. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Small Engines: Principle Of Operation: 2-Stroke Gasoline

Summary: The 2-stroke engine completes its cycle in two piston strokes, using the crankcase as a pump and mixing oil into the fuel so the crankshaft bearings stay lubricated on the air-fuel charge. It has a high power-to-weight ratio, making it ideal for hand-held tools, and the industry moved away from the extra-oily ratios partly for EPA reasons. Source anchors: 2-stroke; crankcase; power-to-weight; oil in fuel.
Analysis: Explaining the 2-stroke via crankcase pump and oil-entrained fuel is the precise mechanism that links to the fuel-mix chapter, and the power-to-weight rationale explains its hand-held domination. The ratio history connects to the fuel section's essentials. This is the engine most people misuse most. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Small Engines: Principle Of Operation: Diesel

Summary: Diesel engines compress air until it is hot enough to ignite injected fuel, giving higher compression and efficiency than gasoline, with the ignition-energy source being compression heat rather than a spark. Source anchors: diesel; compression ignition; efficiency.
Analysis: The compression-ignition mechanism is the single sentence that defines diesel versus gasoline, and the efficiency advantage explains the tractor fleet's fuel choice. The no-spark-plug difference sets the diesel troubleshooting section apart. This is the engine chapter's third reference model. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Small Engines: Troubleshooting

Summary: Troubleshooting a small engine is a systematic ladder: suspect fuel, air, or ignition first, then compression; Corcoran's method runs through fuel, air, ignition, and beyond, with each subsystem referenced to its own subsection. Source anchors: fuel; air; ignition; compression; troubleshooting.
Analysis: The fuel-air-ignition-compression ladder is the diagnostic framework of the whole troubleshooting section, turning a mystery no-start into a decision tree. Each subsystem gets a dedicated subsection with its failure modes. This overview is the map the subsections fill. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Small Engines: Troubleshooting: Fuel

Summary: The fuel subsystem has three classic failures: bad fuel (stale, gummy gasoline that varnishes over 3–6 months), a blocked fuel line, and a clogged fuel filter, diagnosed in order before touching the carburetor. Source anchors: bad fuel; fuel line; fuel filter; varnish.
Analysis: Ordering the fuel failures (fuel, line, filter, carb) is the legibility that prevents tearing down a carburetor for a stale-tank problem. The varnish timeline ties back to the fuel-stabilizer section. This is the plumbing-aware diagnostic sequence. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Small Engines: Troubleshooting: Fuel: Bad Fuel

Summary: Bad fuel is the most common fault: gasoline varnishes and gums within a few months, and a carburetor cobwebbed with varnish needs cleaning; stale fuel should be drained and replaced. Source anchors: stale fuel; varnish; drain.
Analysis: Naming stale fuel as the number-one no-start cause saves readers hours, and the drain-and-replace response is the cheap fix. The varnish-and-carburetor connection sets up the carburetor section. This is the simplest failure diagnosed first. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Small Engines: Troubleshooting: Fuel: Fuel Line

Summary: A blocked fuel line keeps gas from reaching the carburetor; the diagnosis is a line that appears clear but resists flow, and replacement or cleaning is the fix. Source anchors: fuel line; blockage; flow.
Analysis: The blocked-line lesson is to check the plumbing before the carburetor, and the clear-looks-but-blocks description stops a common misdiagnosis. Cleaning or replacing the line is cheap. This is the fuel-path mid-failure. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Small Engines: Troubleshooting: Fuel: Fuel Filter

Summary: A clogged fuel filter starves the engine of fuel while looking innocent; the fix is a cheap replacement, so the filter should be in the diagnosis order ahead of the carburetor. Source anchors: fuel filter; clogged; replace.
Analysis: The filter is the cheapest cheapest part in the fuel system, and replacing it before a carburetor rebuild prevents wasted labor and money. Ordering it ahead of the carburetor in the ladder is the legibility win. This is the mechanical chapter's remove-cost-before-rebuild rule. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Small Engines: Troubleshooting: Fuel: Carburetor

Summary: When fuel, line, and filter check out, the carburetor is the suspect; the section covers cleaning, rebuilding, and the diagnosis of a gummed internal passageway, with symptoms like hard starting and rich or lean running. Source anchors: carburetor; gummed; cleaning; rebuild.
Analysis: The carburetor is the last fuel-system item in the ladder, and its gummed-passage mechanics explain both the symptom and the cleaning remedy. Moving to rebuild only when cleaning fails is the escalation rule. This is the fuel-system endgame. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Small Engines: Troubleshooting: Fuel: Carburetor: Cleaning

Summary: Cleaning a gummed carburetor involves disassembling, soaking and blasting the passages with cleaner, and verifying the jets are clear before reassembly. Source anchors: disassemble; soak; passages; jets.
Analysis: The soak-and-blast cleaning procedure is the cheapest route back to a running engine, and verifying the tiny jets clear is the success criterion. This is the repair-before-replace doctrine at carburetor scale. It stands in for the whole carburetor specialty. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Small Engines: Troubleshooting: Fuel: Carburetor: Rebuild

Summary: When cleaning fails, a carburetor rebuild kit replaces the gaskets and diaphragms; the parts kit is cheap relative to a new carburetor. Source anchors: rebuild kit; gaskets; diaphragms.
Analysis: The rebuild threshold (clean first, rebuild second) is the escalation rule of the fuel system, and the parts-kit-versus-new-carb math keeps the cost rational. This is the chapter's final fuel-system repair. It completes the fuel ladder. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Small Engines: Troubleshooting: Air

Summary: Air-side failures are a clogged air filter and, more subtly, a blocked or leaking intake that changes the fuel-air ratio; cleaning or replacing the filter is the cheap first move. Source anchors: air filter; intake; ratio.
Analysis: The air-side section parallels the fuel-side ordering (filter first, intake later), and the ratio-change explanation ties air issues to rich-or-lean running symptoms. A $5 filter swap prevents a bogus carburetor teardown. This is the engine's breathing side. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Small Engines: Troubleshooting: Lack Of Ignition (Gas Engine)

Summary: Gas-engine no-ignition causes are a dead spark plug, bad magento coil, a sheared flywheel key, or a loose or grounded kill switch, in that order, checked with a spark tester or by grounding the plug. Source anchors: spark plug; magneto; flywheel key; kill switch.
Analysis: The ordered ignition checklist (plug, coil, key, kill switch) is the diagnostic spine for a spark-less engine, and the flywheel-key shearing is a repair item beginners rarely know. This is the electrical side of the no-start ladder, given equal standing with fuel. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Small Engines: Troubleshooting: Lack Of Ignition (Diesel Engine)

Summary: Diesel no-start diagnosis focuses on fuel delivery and glow plugs rather than spark, since diesels ignite by compression; air in the fuel lines is a classic cause. Source anchors: diesel; glow plugs; fuel delivery; air in lines.
Analysis: The diesel section's reframe (no spark, so check fuel and preheating) keeps the two fuel systems separate, and the air-in-lines cause is the diesel equivalent of a vapor lock. This is the compression-ignition chapter's diagnostic variant. It closes the ignition subsection. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Small Engines: Troubleshooting: Next Steps

Summary: If fuel, air, and ignition all check out but the engine still fails, the next step is compression testing, which points at rings, valves, or a head gasket — repairs that are usually engine replacement territory for small tools. Source anchors: compression test; rings; valves; head gasket.
Analysis: The compression-test endgame sets the honest boundary of small-engine repair, where failed rings or valves on a lawn tool justify replacement. Drawing this line prevents unbounded labor on a cheap block. This is the chapter's cost-limit conclusion. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Hydraulics

Summary: Hydraulics convey force through pumped fluid, powering tractor 3PH lift arms, front-end loaders, skid-steers, log splitters, and firewood processors. Corcoran walks through the core parts — fluid, reservoir, pump, hoses, valves, cylinders, relief valves — plus optional cooling, filtering, monitors, and motors. Source anchors: hydraulics; fluid; pump; valves; cylinders; relief valve.
Analysis: The parts glossary makes every hydraulic machine legible, and the always-engaged-pump logic (relief valve dumps unused flow) explains why these systems are simple and cheap. Understanding the parts list is presented as the key to repair and fabrication. This is the chapter's foundational vocabulary. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Log Splitter Example

Summary: A log splitter unwraps the hydraulic parts list: the steel beam doubles as reservoir and radiator, the engine drives a pump to 2,000–3,000 PSI at 10–20 GPM, and a joystick valve routes fluid to either side of a double-acting cylinder, with the relief valve dumping unused flow and the return detent latching the retract cycle. Source anchors: log splitter; pump; PSI; double acting; relief; return detent.
Analysis: The worked log-splitter example turns the abstract parts list into a functioning system, and the relief-valve-wastes-fuel explanation is the design trade that keeps the system cheap. The return-detent feature makes retraction hands-off while the operator stacks wood. This is the chapter's core worked example. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Pumps

Summary: The homestead typically has two hydraulic pumps (log splitter and tractor), with gear and piston designs; gear pumps are cheaper, constant-displacement, and the homestead default, while piston pumps add the adjustable swash plate. Source anchors: gear pump; piston pump; constant displacement; swash plate.
Analysis: The gear-versus-piston comparison explains why tractors and splitters use simple gear pumps, and the constant-displacement property is why relief valves are needed. The piston pump's swash-plate tunability marks the professional tier. This is the pump selection taxonomy. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Pumps: Gear Vs Piston

Summary: Gear pumps move fluid with meshed gears and flow at a constant rate regardless of back pressure; piston pumps use an adjustable swash plate to vary the stroke. Gear pumps, being cheaper, dominate homesteader equipment. Source anchors: gear; piston; swash plate; constant displacement.
Analysis: The constant-displacement-vs-adjustable distinction is the mechanical difference that determines valve design and cost, and the homestead tilt toward gear pumps is the affordability consequence. This subsection gives the design logic behind the parts list. It is the pump mechanism explained. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Pumps: Open Vs Closed Center

Summary: Open-center systems pump continuously and dump unused flow to the tank, while closed-center pumps only pump on demand via valve feedback; open-center gear systems, being simpler and cheaper, dominate homestead equipment. Source anchors: open center; closed center; tank; feedback.
Analysis: The always-pumping open-center design explains both the cheapness and the relief-valve need, and the closed-center alternative is priced as the sophistication option. Recognizing which system a machine owns prevents incorrect valve matching. This is the pump-circuit taxonomy. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Pumps: One- Vs Two-Stage

Summary: Two-stage pumps pair a high-flow low-pressure pump with a high-pressure lower-flow pump and an unloader valve, switching between them by load, which is why they are called log-splitter pumps — high flow to return the wedge, high pressure to split. Source anchors: two-stage; unloader; log splitter; high flow.
Analysis: The two-stage unloader explanation — Boolean switch between flow modes under load — is the mechanism behind the splitter's dual behavior, and it justifies the log-splitter-pump nickname. This is the chapter's most counterintuitive pump design made clear. It explains why the same pump both rushes and pushes. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Pumps: Connecting Pumps To Engines

Summary: Connecting a pump to an engine needs a mounting bracket matching the two bolt circles (with a giveaway that pumps and engines use standardized patterns) and a shaft coupler with elastomeric inserts to absorb alignment error. Source anchors: mounting bracket; bolt circle; coupler; insert.
Analysis: The bolt-circle concept and the offset-absorbing coupler are the two hardware pieces that make engine-to-pump assembly possible, and the tolerance reasoning explains why rigid couplings fail. Standardized bore patterns make off-the-shelf brackets viable. This is the pump-installation toolkit. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Pumps: PTO

Summary: For building PTO-driven implements, hydraulics give valve control versus the PTO's simple on/off, and installing a PTO-driven pump can deliver more power than the tractor's internal pump (e.g., a 21 GPM, 2000 PSI PTO pump transmits about 24 HP versus the internal 6.4 GPM at 3000 PSI or about 11 HP). Source anchors: PTO; remotes; 6.4 GPM; 21 GPM; horsepower.
Analysis: The horsepower math (GPM x PSI / 1714 = HP) is the chapter's legibility anchor, showing exactly when the PTO route beats the tractor's internal hydraulic system. The remote-versus-PTO choice becomes a numbers decision rather than an intuition. This is the tractor-implement design section. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Valves

Summary: Hydraulic valves switch the flow of pressurized fluid, with lever-actuated spool valves the most common on splitters, tractor loaders, and tow trucks, plus specialty button, electromechanical, and relief valves. Source anchors: valves; spool; lever; relief.
Analysis: The spool valve's linear-piston mechanism is the internal logic of every directional control, and naming the specialty variants completes the vocabulary. This overview sets up the position/port and spool-configuration subsections. It is the control layer of the hydraulic system. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Valves: Positions and Ports

Summary: Valves are named by ports and positions (e.g., 4/3), with spring-center defaults; the tractor FEL curl-dump valve is a 4/3 with forward (extend), open (lock), and reverse (retract) positions. Source anchors: 4/3; spring center; curl; dump; lock.
Analysis: The ports-and-positions convention (P/T/A/B) is the hydraulic valve's own language, and the FEL curl example makes it concrete. Understanding that the default center blocks flow explains why a bucket stays put. This is the valve-reading skill. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Valves: Spool Configurations

Summary: Common spool configurations are forward (P to A, B to T), reverse, open (pump to tank, A/B blocked), and float (A to B), which combined cover every directional valve setting on homestead equipment. Source anchors: forward; reverse; open; float; spool.
Analysis: The four-configuration table (forward, reverse, open, float) is the complete logic of hydraulic direction, and the joystick-with-two-valves fact explains FEL control. The open-versus-float distinction (lock versus free-wheel) is the crucial nuance. This is the valve behavior lexicon. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Valves: Multi-Spool Valves and Blocks

Summary: Multiple cylinders are controlled by block valves bolted to NFPA mounting manifolds (industrial) or by multi-spool valves that gang levers side by side in one housing (e.g., a 4-spool 3-position valve at $300). Source anchors: multi-spool; block; NFPA; $300.
Analysis: The multi-spool-versus-block distinction maps the two control architectures to their cost and industrial context, and the 4-spool 3-position example prices the homestead-relevant option. This is the valve-bank shopping decision. It completes the valve control taxonomy. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Valves: Power Beyond

Summary: Retrofitting extra circuits on an open-center tractor is best done with power beyond (HPCO): rather than a parallel T (which the open-center joystick would short to tank), the new valve goes in series via a plug-reconfigurable spool. Source anchors: power beyond; HPCO; series; T.
Analysis: The power-beyond-vs-parallel-T explanation is the chapter's most important retrofit insight, since a naive T on an open-center system starves the new circuit. The plug-conversion detail makes it an implementable modification. This is the hydraulic upgrade path for adding grapples and remotes. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Valves: Selector

Summary: A selector valve switches fluid to output A or B without a spring return, letting one joystick control either the bucket or a grapple, or steering pressure to a rear remote. Source anchors: selector; A/B; joystick; grapple.
Analysis: The selector valve is the cheaper alternative to power beyond when only one new circuit is wanted, and its no-spring-return stays-where-set behavior is the defining property. The two wiring options (before or after the joystick) show the retrofit flexibility. This is the minimal-cost expansion route. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Valves: Log Splitter Valves

Summary: Log splitter valves carry the return detent feature that latches the retract position so the operator can stack wood hands-free until full retraction pops the lever to neutral. Source anchors: return detent; retract; neutral.
Analysis: The return-detent recap closes the valve section on its most useful homestead feature, and the pop-to-neutral behavior is the safety endpoint. This ties the valve taxonomy back to the log-splitter worked example. It is the feature that makes a splitter a one-operator machine. The sizing, ordering, and flow of this work make it a Logistics and Throughput problem.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Cylinders

Summary: Hydraulic cylinders move the load, specified by closed length, bore, stroke, single- versus double-acting, port type and location, cap and rod ends, and tie-rod versus welded construction; Corcoran recommends buying from Tractor Supply where you can physically match parts. Source anchors: bore; stroke; closed length; double acting; tie rod; Tractor Supply.
Analysis: The full cylinder-spec glossary is what makes buying a replacement match the old part exactly, and the tie-rod-versus-welded repairability distinction is a real ownership trade. The Tractor Supply recommendation (pick up and compare fittings) is the practical retail insight. This is the cylinder-buying skill. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Hoses

Summary: Hydraulic hoses are specified by diameter, length, PSI rating, and end threading, and cannot be crimped at home (a bench-top 5-ton hose crimper costs $2,000, a floor unit $40,000), so off-the-shelf or locally made hoses are the rule. Source anchors: hose; PSI; crimper; $2,000; custom.
Analysis: The crimper-price reveal explains why hoses are bought ready-made, and the custom-shop option covers unusual lengths. The off-the-shelf-default advice matches the book's buy-don't-fabricate habit for safety-critical items. This is the hose-procurement reality. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Quick-Connect Couplers

Summary: Hydraulic quick connects let implements attach and detach from tractor remotes, but come in a bewildering array of series (210, 2RL, 3000, 4000, Enerpac, etc.); the rule is to learn what your tractor uses and spec implements to match. Source anchors: quick connect; series; remotes; match.
Analysis: The incompatible-series warning is the hydraulic analog of the pneumatic quick-connect section, and the match-your-tractor rule is the safety-first resolution. Buying the wrong series means leaking or no connection at crucial moments. This is the connector-compatibility lesson. Workshop: Hydraulics: Maintenance

Summary: Hydraulic fluid and filters degrade over time and should be changed on schedule (100 to several thousand hours) or verified by lab sampling; the change procedure mirrors an oil change: warm, drain, replace filter, refill. Source anchors: filter; fluid; drain; refill; schedule.
Analysis: The maintenance section turns hydraulics from black boxes into serviced systems, and the lab-sampling alternative respects varied use. The oil-change analog makes the procedure instantly familiar. This is the preventive routine that keeps the fleet alive. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Hydraulics: Safety

Summary: Hydraulic fluid is toxic and can inject into skin; a pinhole leak can spray high-pressure fluid that pierces flesh (a hydraulic injection injury that can cause amputation), so suspected leaks are checked with wood or cardboard, not hands, and any injection needs immediate medical care. Source anchors: injection injury; pinhole leak; 2,000 PSI; medical.
Analysis: The hydraulic-injection-injury section is one of the book's most important safety passages, because the invisible-pinhole failure is uniquely dangerous, and the cardboard-check technique is the actionable prevention. The seek-care-immediately rule applies even to a small scrape. This is the chapter's safety climax. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Projects and Plans: Hanging 1-inch Steel Tube Shelves

Summary: The hanging shelf build uses the MIG welder, angle grinder, and drill to make a 2x4-foot two-shelf unit from 1-inch tube and 3/4-inch plywood, with French cleat and spacer for mounting, and Corcoran recommends batching multiple units to amortize setup. Source anchors: MIG; 1 inch tube; plywood; French cleat; batch.
Analysis: The build repeats the pig-roast-grate fabrication pattern (square tube, weld, plywood), showing that one welding vocabulary serves all projects, and the batch-forecaster advice is the throughput lesson. The weld-corner and plywood-screw steps are documented carefully. This is the flagship welding project. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Workshop: Projects and Plans: Storage Crates
