Book Reviews: Workshop and Craftsmanship

Summary: The workshop shelf closes the reviews with Moving Heavy Things and Line: Tying It Up, Tying It Down, the two books that inspired the heavy-moving and rigging chapters. Source anchors: Moving Heavy Things; Line; rigging.
Analysis: Ending the reviews on the two books that inspired the workshop chapters completes the intellectual loop of the manual. The line-and-knot focus makes sailing and rigging techniques legible for farmers. This is the workshop's foundation reading. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Essays

Summary: The essays collect Corcoran's worldview pieces on haggling, prepping and the power law, near-versus-far mode, an electrical-daring story, urban farming, robotics, minimalism, glyphosate, open-source hardware, repairability, and information organization, plus a New Hampshire civic primer. Source anchors: essays; haggling; prepping; near and far; New Hampshire.
Analysis: The Essay section is the book's ideological capstone, translating the manual's techniques into a general philosophy of robustness and self-reliance. Each essay carries a cut-through argument (haggling ethics, power-law prepping, infrastructure pyramids) with the same evidentiary style as the how-to chapters. This is the why behind the how. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: Everything Is Negotiable — A Primer On Haggling

Summary: Corcoran argues that in the country, unlike Western retail, a higher share of purchases is used goods or services with negotiable prices, and recommends Roger Fisher's Getting to Yes as the negotiating read. Source anchors: haggling; retail; Getting to Yes.
Analysis: The city-versus-country haggling contrast is the essay's frame, and recommending a principled-negotiation classic keeps it from being a tactics dump. This overview splits to labor, advertising, BATNA, and material goods. It is the book's negotiation theory. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: Everything Is Negotiable — A Primer On Haggling: Labor

Summary: Corcoran advises against haggling on labor because labor is tied to self-worth; offering less than a quoted wage risks insulting the tradesman, and a yes after an insulting bargain is worse than a no. Instead, add an axis — a slow-season price break or bundled group business — so the counterparty concedes on price while winning on something else. Source anchors: labor; self-worth; slow season; bundle; axis.
Analysis: The add-an-axis negotiation technique (concede price, win on schedule or volume) is the essay's transferable gem, and the emotional-economics of labor haggling is a genuinely humane insight. The shearing-price examples (offer to wait for June, rope in a friend for volume) are concrete scripts. This is negotiation as mutual value creation rather than confrontation. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: Everything Is Negotiable — A Primer On Haggling: Advertising

Summary: When selling, good ad copy raises both interest and price; Corcoran draws on the mail-order A/B-testing tradition, and his takeaways are sell the sizzle (name benefits and problems solved), length sells (conversion rises linearly with length), and give a call to action. Source anchors: ad copy; sell the sizzle; length; call to action.
Analysis: The excavator-ad example (model number versus capabilities versus problems-solved) is the essay's demonstration, and the call-to-action rule fixes the dead-end ad. The length-sells claim stands on decades of mail-order data against web-minimalism. This closes the negotiation essay with a writing skill. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Essays: Everything Is Negotiable — A Primer On Haggling: BATNA

Summary: BATNA (best alternative to a negotiated agreement) is the null hypothesis of any deal; knowing it before negotiating lets you decide rationally, as the pool-table seller whose alternative is disposing of it in 24 hours should take even $5. Source anchors: BATNA; null hypothesis; pool table.
Analysis: The pool-table example makes BATNA instantly graspable, and the insistence that only your alternative, not your original cost, matters is the reframe. This is negotiation framed as decision theory. It connects to the euvoluntary-exchange close of the essay. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Essays: Everything Is Negotiable — A Primer On Haggling: Material Goods

Summary: For goods, haggling is less fraught, and the 80/20 rule is the seven magic words Is that the best you can do? Other tactics: maintain skepticism, never mention how far you drove, bring cash and the ability to take the item now, and anchor the price against a recent comparable. Corcoran closes on ethics — aim for euvoluntary exchange (a deal both parties leave happy, per Michael C. Munger) and never burn a counterparty, because a sheep can be sheared many times but skinned only once. Source anchors: 80/20; seven magic words; cash; framing; euvoluntary.
Analysis: The seven-words tactic and the don't-say-how-far-you-drove warning are immediately usable, and the euvoluntary-exchange principle (a coined term from Michael C. Munger) gives the whole essay its moral center. The sheep-sheared-not-skinned proverb is the book's best summary of why to leave pennies on the table. This fuses practical tactics with an ethics of mutual benefit. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Essays: Prepping and The Power Law

Summary: Corcoran defines the spectrum from fragile (degrading under stress) to robust (slowly degrading) to antifragile (improving under stress), and argues prepping moves individuals from fragile toward robust but rarely antifragile, since shocks rarely raise absolute utility. Source anchors: fragile; robust; antifragile; prepping.
Analysis: The Taleb-derived taxonomy gives the prepping debate a precise vocabulary, and the claim that absolute, not just relative, utility rarely increases under stress is the essay's corrective. This sets up the sociology and probability sections. It is the philosophical foundation of the whole robustness theme. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Essays: Prepping and The Power Law: Sociology

Summary: Many preppers treat it as daydreaming plus consumerism, buying tactical gear for low-probability drama, which the essay likens to 1950s men's adventure magazines. Corcoran is a fan of choosing a robust lifestyle, not of prepping spelled with a trademark, because a robust lifestyle lets you survive shocks with minimal regret and avoids the awful BATNA of the desperate. Source anchors: tactical gear; daydreaming; robust lifestyle; minimal regret.
Analysis: The sociology critique separates the consumer-hobby prepping from the robust-lifestyle goal, and the hungry-women-in-refugee-camps line makes the worst-case BATNA visceral. This is the essay's normative core: prepare to avoid regretful forced choices. It ties back to the infrastructure-pyramid essay's theme. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Essays: Prepping and The Power Law: Severity Vs Likelihood

Summary: Shocks follow a power law, and high-drama unlikely scenarios are poor ROI; Corcoran lists a partial frequency table (recession/1-in-5-year, multi-day outage once per decade, robbery and world war once per 50 years, plague and civil war once per century, little ice ages once per millennium, civilizational collapse once per 1,500 years) and computes lifetime probabilities, so the correct prepping is scenario-ranking by likelihood and ROI. Source anchors: power law; severity; likelihood; probability; ROI.
Analysis: The frequency-and-likelihood table is the essay's signature legibility device, translating the prepping panic into actuarial probability (a layoff at 99.99% lifetime odds versus civilizational collapse at 4.8%). Ranking threats by ROI rather than drama is the actionable doctrine. The author's pride in guessing plague-once-a-century before COVID is the essay's self-aware wink. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Essays: Prepping and The Power Law: I'll Be Gone, You'll Be Gone

Summary: Citing Byrne Hobart's essay, Corcoran argues that authorities have no incentive to plan for scenarios in which they themselves cease to exist, so society underestimates tail risks; every government is permanent until it is not. Source anchors: Byrne Hobart; principal-agent; tail risk; incentive.
Analysis: The principal-agent insight explains why even robust societies mishandle civilization-scale risk, and it grounds the essay's healthy skepticism of official reassurances. This is the institutional argument under the power-law analysis. It justifies individual redundancy against a category authorities ignore. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Essays: Prepping and The Power Law: More Likely Scenarios

Summary: The highest-ROI prepping addresses the likely shocks first: a layoff (pay down debt, build an emergency fund, per Dave Ramsey) and a power outage (a $500 generator plus a $150 transfer switch). Corcoran quotes Ronnie Coleman — everybody wants to be a bodybuilder, nobody wants to lift the weights — against the fun of zombie-prepping. Source anchors: layoff; emergency fund; generator; transfer switch; Ronnie Coleman.
Analysis: Flipping prepping from drama to the drudgery of debt paydown and a generator is the essay's practical punch, and the bodybuilding quote is the motivation for the boring work. This is the chapter's actionable center. It reframes preparedness as financial and infrastructural, not tactical. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Essays: Prepping and The Power Law: Less Likely Scenarios

Summary: Only at the rare-shock end does prepping resemble the hobby, and Corcoran rejects survival-seed cans, bug-out bags, food-forest and Jerusalem-artichoke LARPs for three reasons: a person needs a million calories a year that only years of prepared land can supply; hobby-farming variance is high; and food does not grow itself, since entropy wins and animals, rust, and weeds eat every free calorie. It looks more like living on a homestead than daydreaming. Source anchors: survival seeds; Jerusalem artichokes; million calories; variance; entropy.
Analysis: The three-reason demolition of survival-farming fantasy (calorie math, variance, entropy) is the essay's most rigorous passage, and the million-calories figure is the unforgettable anchor. The conclusion — catastrophe preparedness looks like a homestead, not a can — is the thematic payoff of the whole book. This is the essay's closing argument for the manual's method. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Essays: Near Mode versus Far Mode

Summary: Robin Hanson's near-versus-far distinction holds that we think concretely about things we know intimately (near) and ideally about distant abstractions (far); homesteading and gardening are far-mode dream topics, inflated by an entire media industry. Corcoran warns that encountering the gritty near-mode reality can cause disappointment and self-doubt, and advises embracing near-mode reality — which is more work but more satisfying. Source anchors: near mode; far mode; Hanson; daydreaming; reality.
Analysis: The near-versus-far lens explains both media-generated homestead fantasy and the disappointment on arrival, and the pornography analogy (simplified and shallow) is the essay's memorable insult to glossy farm imagery. The media-kit statistic (magazines full of aspirational readers who never act) grounds the theory. This is the book's most useful psychological warning for aspiring homesteaders. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: The Great 110 Volt To 220 Volt Twist Lock Adapter Saga

Summary: During a first-winter power outage, Corcoran jury-rigged house power by building a suicide cord to feed the workshop's A bus from his generator, then built a second double-male 110V-to-220V twist-lock adapter that energized both bus bars in phase so the wood boiler and circulator pumps (split across legs) both ran. The 2014 ice storm story warns against doing likewise while showing the electrical theory (three-line service, alternating breaker legs). Source anchors: suicide cord; twist lock; bus bars; boiler; ice storm.
Analysis: The saga is the book's most daring electrical confession, and the leg-by-leg explanation (A and B bus bars serving different circuits) teaches the three-wire service concept through a crisis. The explicit I-hope-you-never-need-this disclaimer keeps the dangerous DIY in context. This is the frontier-systems essay with a genuinely useful electrical lesson. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Essays: Vertical Urban Farming is Stupid

Summary: Corcoran concedes urban gardening but attacks urban farming (vertical farms, rooftop farms, container farms) for mispricing resources: transportation is cheap and nearly carbon-free, while urban land is the rarest, dearest resource there is. He computes that a person's trucked food emits about 9 lbs of CO2 a year versus 2,169 lbs for one NY-London flight, and that every acre of Manhattan farmland destroys about $10 million of utility versus rural land at \(10K–\)5M/acre. Source anchors: urban farming; transportation; CO2; Manhattan land.
Analysis: The essay's two computations (food-transport CO2 versus flights, and Manhattan acre cost) are devastatingly legible, and the land-price differential (city versus rural) frames farmland as a catastrophic misallocation. The CO2-accounting of urban professionals is the rhetorical sting. This is the book's economics essay against a fashionable idea. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Essays: Robotic Farming

Summary: Corcoran loves automation (sawmill sensors, self-driving tractors) but argues most robotic-gardening promises are already solved cheaply by dumb tools: walk-behind seeders, weed-barrier fabric, drip tape, and sprinklers. The FarmBot ($4,500 for 200 square feet, about $25/ft2) amortizes to $2.50/ft2/year to save pennies in water and fertilizer, so it is a luxury, not economics. Source anchors: FarmBot; automation; drip tape; seeder; cost per foot.
Analysis: The FarmBot cost math (thousands of dollars to save cents of materials) is the essay's decisive number, and the what-do-you-call-automation-that-works answer (gardening) is its clever thesis. The cheap-dumb-tools-won argument respects modern tools while rejecting gadgetry. This is the automation essay with real economics. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Essays: The Lie of 100 Things and The Truth of The Infrastructure Pyramid

Summary: The Silicon Valley 100-Things minimalism is status signaling that merely outsources ownership; behind it lies the infrastructure pyramid, the network of tools each maintained by the tools below, separating humans from brute nature. A suburbanite owns the top three layers, a homesteader six or seven — the pan, the knife, the smoker, the butcher gear, and down to the grease gun and welder — at the cost of free time but the gain of satisfaction and self-reliance. Source anchors: 100 Things; infrastructure pyramid; outsourced ownership; free time.
Analysis: The infrastructure-pyramid image is the book's most original concept, and the layer-by-layer descent (meal, pan, canner, tractor, welder, grease gun) makes the homestead's capital visible. The minimalism-as-status-countersignaling critique and the Zen-minimalist-has-fractional-claims-on-everything reframe are the essay's sting. This is the ideology section that justifies owning six or seven layers. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Essays: Roundup Is Next To Godliness

Summary: Corcoran addresses the widespread meme in the alt-agriculture world that Roundup is a deadly chemical poisoning the water, ruining the land, poisoning the food supply, and killing humans, and disputes every claim. He defines terms first: Roundup is the brand name of a broad-spectrum glyphosate-based herbicide, and glyphosate (C3H8NO5P) interferes with the enzyme EPSPS, which is present in plants but not in animals. Because animals do not synthesize the three amino acids the EPSPS pathway creates (phenylalanine, tyrosine, and tryptophan), humans have no such enzyme to be disrupted, so glyphosate is not a general broad-spectrum poison that kills humans, rats, and dogs the way it kills plants. The section then reviews the claims one by one: that it is a deadly poison or causes cancer, that it is poisoning the water, that it is ruining the land, and that it is poisoning the food supply. Source anchors: glyphosate; EPSPS; phenylalanine; tyrosine; tryptophan; Roundup.
Analysis: The mechanism-first argument is what gives the essay its rigor: glyphosate inhibits an enzyme humans do not even have, so the poison-and-cancer framing has to appeal to a secondary mechanism the author says the evidence does not support. Listing the four anti-glyphosate claims and reviewing them one by one is the evidentiary structure the three subsections follow. This is the book's most controversial essay, argued on biochemistry and study quality rather than slogans. The limits at work here are a live exercise in Ecological Constraint.

Essays: Roundup Is Next To Godliness: Not Poisonous or Cancer Causing

Summary: Corcoran takes up the claim that glyphosate poisons or causes cancer in humans. Because the mode of action does not affect humans, who lack the EPSPS enzyme, he examines the secondary-harm claims, tangling with an acquaintance who gave him a list of 40 or so papers. Digging into each, he came away radically unimpressed: about half were merely overviews of other studies suggesting further research, which proved nothing; most of the rest did not study humans but used mice or cells in vitro, had small n's (numbers of subjects) in the single digits or teens, and reported p-values far above the standard 5% cutoff (a problem compounded by p-hacking — null results quietly dropped or sliced to create an effect); many were surveys of self-selected populations reporting whether they thought they had been exposed to glyphosate; and the actual lab studies often used doses thousands or tens of thousands of times higher than any person could ever be exposed to, yet most still showed no noticeable impact on the test subjects. Source anchors: 40 papers; overviews; small n; p-values; p-hacking; self-selected surveys; in vitro; absurd doses.
Analysis: The claim-by-claim critique of the strongest anti-glyphosate studies is the essay's evidentiary core — the author applied the same standards of study quality he uses everywhere else in the book and found the case against glyphosate unconvincing. The methods critique (n's, p-values, p-hacking, self-selected surveys, dose magnitudes) is what separates absent evidence from demonstrated harm. This is the risk-analysis discipline that refuses alarm without data. The limits at work here are a live exercise in Ecological Constraint.

Essays: Roundup Is Next To Godliness: Does Not Poison Water

Summary: On water contamination, Corcoran argues that glyphosate binds tightly with both plants and soil, so even the overspray that does not land on the target plant has no chance to enter the water supply. Glyphosate in soil is metabolized by bacteria; the worst-case half-life proposed for the chemical is 174 days (which meets the EPA standard of 180 days), but studies show an average half-life of about 30 days, meaning that a year after application less than 0.02% of the overspray still exists. Source anchors: binds to plants and soil; metabolized by bacteria; 174 day half-life; 180 day EPA standard; 30 day average.
Analysis: The binding-and-metabolism chemistry is the mechanism alleged to keep glyphosate out of groundwater, and the half-life arithmetic gives the claim a measurable time scale instead of a vague fear. Comparing the worst case (174 days) against the EPA's 180-day standard quarters the risk against a regulatory benchmark. This is water-safety analysis by chemistry and numbers rather than imagery. The limits at work here are a live exercise in Ecological Constraint.

Essays: Roundup Is Next To Godliness: Does Not Poison Soil

Summary: On soil, Corcoran notes that glyphosate enters plants only through leaves — not via seeds or roots — so residual glyphosate cannot harm plants. He concludes that because literally dozens of the best studies the anti-glyphosate side could assemble all turned out very weak, he updated his priors and now does not take seriously the idea that glyphosate is dangerous to humans in the doses a homesteader would receive, pointing the reader to a collection of the strongest cherry-picked anti-glyphosate studies at gmofreeusa.org and a Cornell agricultural-school overview for verification. Given no negative effects he has been able to find, how useful glyphosate is at killing weeds and invasive species, and the huge opportunity cost of manual weeding, he finds glyphosate a wonderful tool with a very high ROI and celebrates the entire catalog of glyphosate-based herbicides. Source anchors: leaves only; updated priors; gmofreeusa.org; Cornell; high ROI.
Analysis: The leaf-only absorption claim is the direct answer to the soil-poisoning fear, and the closing verdict explicitly frames the decision as cost-benefit: no demonstrated harm versus the enormous labor cost of the alternatives. Publishing the strongest anti-glyphosate sources for the reader to examine is the same invitation-to-verify honesty the book exercises throughout. This closes the essay on an evidentiary note rather than an ideological one. The limits at work here are a live exercise in Ecological Constraint.

Essays: Critique of The Open Source Ecology Global Village Construction Set

Summary: Corcoran attacks Open Source Ecology's GVCS as a TED-darling set of plans for 50 machines that largely do not exist, are not optimized for cost, maintainability, or labor, and cost as much as working under-warranty commercial tools. The market, via competition and amortized engineering, delivers better value than a handful of build-your-own designs ever will. Source anchors: OSE; GVCS; TED; market; buildability.
Analysis: The essay's economics (the market's incentives versus a hobbyist open-source build) is the same make-versus-buy logic as the rest of the book, and the OLPC parallel (architecture astronauts reinventing wheels) gives the critique historical weight. The buildability-not-optimized claim is the technical core. This is the essay against reinventing well-solved systems. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Essays: Neal Stephenson on Praxis and Repairability

Summary: Corcoran quotes Stephenson's Anathem on the difference between a four-thousand-year-old stove that never breaks and a modern one you cannot fix, and adds his philosophy: no vendor lock-in, anti-fragility, and the power to make my own dog food. He hates a smoker with a microcontroller and Bluetooth pellet supply, preferring tools that do not depend on a supply chain or a smartphone. Source anchors: Anathem; praxis; repairability; vendor lock-in; my own dog food.
Analysis: The Anathem excerpt supplies the essay's ideal (a stove you could always fix), and the three points (lock-in, fragility, self-sufficiency) give the low-tech preference a principled frame. The pellet-smoker rant is the book's most concrete anti-gadget statement, presciently written before supply-chain shocks. This is the philosophical backing for the workshop chapter's MIG-not-TIG and Masterbuilt-not-Bluetooth choices. This is precisely the household-managed infrastructure that Frontier Systems describes.

Essays: Information Organization

Summary: Homestead details are worth remembering a year or two later but not 15 minutes, so Corcoran documents everything, echoing Jefferson's farm book, and uses four tools: three-ring binders for manuals, maintenance logs in spreadsheets, a password-protected mediawiki, and a to-do list (Getting Things Done). Source anchors: Jefferson; binders; maintenance log; wiki; to-do.
Analysis: The information-organization essay is the legibility doctrine applied to memory, and the example (chainsaw chain, pig weights, boiler shutoff date) shows the recurring value of a record. The wiki for every tractor, species, and project plus the reverse-chronological log is the system. This is the book's own note-taking manifesto, and it explains why the manual itself is so documented. This converts a messy domain into the measurable, actionable quantities that define Legibility.

Essays: Information Organization: Three Ring Binders

Summary: Binders organize the paper manuals, as covered in the workshop chapters. Source anchors: binders; manuals.
Analysis: The one-line pointer cross-references the manual system in the workshop chapter, keeping this essay's focus on the tool set. It treats binders as the physical half of the information system. This is the reference infrastructure. This converts a messy domain into the measurable, actionable quantities that define Legibility.

Essays: Information Organization: Maintenance Logs

Summary: Corcoran copies equipment maintenance schedules into spreadsheets (infinite room, cleaner than oil-stained paper, links to parts), with the trade that a printed-out sheet is less convincing at resale than a paper log with stapled receipts. Source anchors: spreadsheet; maintenance log; resale.
Analysis: The spreadsheet-versus-paper trade (searchability versus resale credibility) is an honest cost-benefit, and the parts-links-to-Amazon detail shows the log's utility. This is the machine-history record that makes a workshop run. It is the legibility of equipment maintenance. This converts a messy domain into the measurable, actionable quantities that define Legibility.

Essays: Information Organization: Wiki

Summary: A password-protected mediawiki holds a page per vegetable, species, vehicle, and project, with data-and-links plus a reverse-chronological log; Corcoran back-filled years of data from emails and even Google Earth firewood stacks. Source anchors: wiki; mediawiki; reverse chronological; backfill.
Analysis: The wiki is the essay's centerpiece, and the back-fill-from-Google-Earth detail shows how valuable and recoverable the missing records were. Each page's data-plus-log structure is the template. This is the information-system hub that makes the homestead legible over decades. This converts a messy domain into the measurable, actionable quantities that define Legibility.

Essays: Information Organization: To Do List

Summary: A to-do list is both a priority reminder and an anti-anxiety tool, and Corcoran credits Getting Things Done (David Allen) with changing his life, using a plain text file and the empty-the-inbox discipline of sort into garbage, reference, or action. Source anchors: Getting Things Done; to-do; inbox; anti-anxiety.
Analysis: The GTD methodology is the essay's action system, and the three-bin sort (garbage, reference, action) gives the wiki and to-do list their inputs. The anti-anxiety framing is the human payoff of legibility. This closes the information-organization essay on a practical, calm note. This converts a messy domain into the measurable, actionable quantities that define Legibility.

Essays: New Hampshire

Summary: Corcoran explains why he chose New Hampshire, covering an overview of where to live in the state, the current-use tax, and how New Hampshire towns are governed, framing it as the state where the informal-institutions theme of the book is most visible. Source anchors: New Hampshire; Manchester; current use; town meeting.
Analysis: The New Hampshire essay is the book's civic case study, and its overview, tax, and town-governance subsections give a reader the concrete machinery of local self-rule. This is the state chapter where private frontier capital meets participatory institutions. It grounds the book's New England setting. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: New Hampshire: An Overview Of New Hampshire

Summary: For a young single mover, Corcoran ranks the four southern-region options: Keene (small college town, FSP-heavy), Manchester (second city, FSP-friendly, his nearest city), Nashua (large, strip-mall border town), and the Seacoast (middle-class suburbs), while recommending a farm about 30 minutes west of Manchester, and characterizes the Free State Project crowd as 10% excellent and many tedious. Source anchors: Keene; Manchester; Nashua; Seacoast; Free State Project.
Analysis: The region-by-region ranking is a genuinely useful relocation primer, and the FSP characterization (winnow ruthlessly) is the book's honest community guidance. The self-aware boring-introvert confession closes the overview. This is the move-here guidance with real texture. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: New Hampshire: Current Use Tax Explained

Summary: New Hampshire's RSA 79-A current use law lets owners of 10+ undeveloped acres have land assessed at low agricultural/forestry rates rather than market value, in exchange for a one-time exit payment equal to ten years of taxes at market value if they develop; a 25-acre woodlot at market assessment would cost $3,750/year versus $37.50 under current use. Source anchors: RSA 79-A; current use; 10 acres; exit fee.
Analysis: The current-use essay explains an incentive-design problem (property tax penalizing long-term land stewardship) and its legislative fix, and the \(3,750-versus-\)37.50 example is the legibility anchor. The exit-fee mechanism prevents gaming. This is the tax-policy chapter that makes New Hampshire land ownership rational. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: New Hampshire: How Towns are Run

Summary: New Hampshire towns run on either Town Council/Town Manager or Board of Selectmen/Town Meeting systems, with all policy and spending decided by single-topic warrant articles up for annual vote; the BoS system (common in small towns) reserves budgets and zoning to the citizens. Source anchors: town council; Board of Selectmen; town meeting; warrant articles.
Analysis: The two-governance-structure taxonomy and the warrant-article system are the civic machinery the essay teaches, and the observation that an individual citizen can push or block policy is the empowering point. This is how New Hampshire's informal institutions become formal. It prepares the RSA 675:5 petition subsection. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: New Hampshire: How Towns are Run: Town Council / Town Manager

Summary: Larger towns use a town council as executive and legislative body, with variants (official ballot town council, budgetary town meeting) apportioning powers between the council and voters. Source anchors: town council; official ballot; budget.
Analysis: The council-system variants map how a town divides power, and the example (school budget versus town budget split) makes the taxonomy concrete. This is the city-adjacent governance form. It sets up the BoS contrast. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: New Hampshire: How Towns are Run: Board of Selectmen

Summary: Smaller towns use a Board of Selectmen (five elected members) who implement spending but cannot approve new budgets or zoning, which are reserved to the citizens via one of three mechanisms: official ballot town meeting, official ballot referendum (SB2), or representative town meeting. Source anchors: Board of Selectmen; SB2; representative town meeting.
Analysis: The BoS-limits-itself design is the New Hampshire idea made explicit: executives administer, citizens legislate. The three mechanisms (direct debate-plus-secret ballot, the popular SB2, representative) are the menu of direct democracy. This is the participatory-government core of the essay. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: New Hampshire: How Towns are Run: Board of Selectmen: Official Ballot Meeting

Summary: In an official ballot town meeting, all citizens debate warrant articles publicly once a year, then vote by secret ballot, with amendments allowed. Source anchors: official ballot; debate; secret ballot; amendment.
Analysis: The public-debate-then-secret-ballot structure is the purest New England town-meeting form, and its openness lets any citizen speak. This is direct democracy in action. It frames the SB2 and representative variants. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: New Hampshire: How Towns are Run: Board of Selectmen: Official Ballot Referendum / SB2

Summary: SB2 (official ballot referendum) is the same as the official ballot meeting but separates the debate session from the secret-ballot vote by several weeks; Corcoran lives in an SB2 town. Source anchors: SB2; ballot; debate; weeks.
Analysis: The SB2 time-split trades attendance for deliberation spread, and the author living in one makes it the essay's practical default. This is the town-meeting form modernized. It is the model readers most likely encounter. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: New Hampshire: How Towns are Run: Board of Selectmen: Representative Town Meeting

Summary: Representative town meetings elect citizens to debate and vote on warrant articles on everyone's behalf, trading directness for manageability. Source anchors: representative; elected; warrant articles.
Analysis: The representative variant is the delegation form of the town meeting, and the trade (direct participation versus manageable numbers) is the honest frame. This completes the three BoS mechanisms. It is the least direct-democracy of the three. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: New Hampshire: How Towns are Run: Warrant Articles

Summary: Warrant articles are single-topic proposals on budgets, zoning, and policy generated by boards or by 25 citizens with signatures, voted once a year, amendable at the meeting (except planning-board zoning articles), with a failed budget article defaulting to last year's plus locked-in contracts. Source anchors: warrant article; 25 signatures; amendable; default.
Analysis: The warrant-article mechanics — who can propose (25 signatures), how they are amended, what happens on failure — are the operational rules of New Hampshire democracy, and the budget-default rule is the important consequence. This is the citizen-legislator how-to. It equips a reader to engage town government. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: New Hampshire: How Towns are Run: Board of Selectmen: Amendment of Warrant Articles

Summary: Because warrant articles are amendable, a small group at town meeting can gut or reshape an article (replace $200,000 with $0, or turn with study turning), so showing up with allies matters both offensively and defensively. Source anchors: amendment; gut; $0; allies.
Analysis: The amendment-tactic reveals the real power dynamics of town meeting, where a minority can scuttle an article by amendment, and the both-directions advice (bring allies to protect your article too) is the practical strategy. This is the essay's most participatory-politics insight. It turns citizen involvement into a game with known rules. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Essays: New Hampshire: How Towns are Run: Board of Selectmen: RSA 675-5 Petitions

Summary: An RSA 675:5 zoning protest petition lets a minority raise a zoning warrant article's passage threshold from 50% to 2/3 by signing owners of 20% of affected or abutting acreage. Corcoran recounts mounting a one-man campaign against a corrupt zoning change, using a GIS database and scripting to identify properties and knocking on about 400 doors for 200 signatures. Source anchors: RSA 675:5; 20% acreage; 2/3; petition.
Analysis: The 675:5 petition is the citizen's brake on zoning power, and the acreage-weighted signature requirement (a single 100-acre owner can suffice) is the memorable arithmetic. The author's GIS-and-door-knocking campaign is the book's demonstration that fighting city hall is possible with data and persistence. This closes the New Hampshire essay on an empowering, hard-won note. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Interviews With Homesteaders

Summary: The book closes with nine interviews with other homesteaders — Marcus and Sarah, Brian, Russ Snow, Kate and Alec M, Dan G, Browning Machine, Grasshill Farm, and Jared — each answering a common set of questions about dreams, research, siting, plans, economics, social life, and advice. Source anchors: interviews; Marcus; Sarah; Brian; Russ Snow; Kate and Alec; Dan G; Browning Machine; Grasshill Farm; Jared.
Analysis: The interviews multiply the book's single-case epistemology into a small, honest survey of how homesteaders actually arrive, fail, and persist, and each voice carries a different economics (the farmer's capital-accumulation theory, the survivor's game, the hobbyist's mixed verdict). They function as the field data behind the manual's advice. This is the book's evidence appendix, in the interviewed people's own words. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Interviews With Homesteaders: Marcus

Summary: Marcus, a self-described small-l libertarian tech-project-manager in central New Hampshire, narrates a peripatetic road (couch surfing, Alaska, a Philly rowhome, WWOOFing, deer-driven Y2K paranoia) that ended with a 45-acre farm with a 1780 colonial and a 40x40 shop. His themes: research as years of map-free real-estate fantasy, a data- and intuition-driven siting, the regret of buying too-small equipment and too-late heavy machinery, and his tool philosophy — cheap hammers everywhere, big heavy iron bought used (a $20,000 20-ton excavator over a $90,000 mini-ex) — because you cannot move anything heavier than you are. He frames homesteading as building a transhistorical portfolio whose value spans possible futures, and praises moderate-density capable neighbors over both the city and the remote wilderness. Source anchors: Marcus; New Hampshire; 45 acres; heavy equipment; transhistorical portfolio; excavator.
Analysis: Marcus's interview is the book's richest alternate economics, and the transhistorical-portfolio concept (diversify across possible futures) is a genuinely original frame for homesteading. The buy-used-heavy versus buy-once-cry-once debate with the author is the essay's analytic high point. His insistence that rural safety lies in moderate-density capable communities, not wilderness, directly contradicts the rifle-and-cans prepper fantasy. This interview grounds the informal-institutions theme in a lived example. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Interviews With Homesteaders: Sarah

Summary: Sarah, Marcus's wife, describes discovering the farm life only through her husband, despising Philadelphia, and advocating moving to New Hampshire, buying commercial-zoned land, and making friends with neighbors (for when lambs eat shrubs or chickens visit swing sets). She admits the up-front costs and that raising babies and a farm simultaneously is hard. Source anchors: Sarah; Philadelphia; New Hampshire; commercial zoning; neighbors.
Analysis: Sarah's short interview gives the family and spouse perspective that the economics-first interviews lack, and her three pieces of advice (New Hampshire, commercial zoning, neighbor friendship) are practical and human. The candor about baby-and-farm simultaneity is the household reality the main narrative glosses. This is the book's acknowledgment that homesteading is a family undertaking, not a solo one. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Interviews With Homesteaders: Brian

Summary: Brian, a remote-work systems administrator, moved three years ago to two acres in middle Tennessee, near his kids and grandkids, with a creek and pasture; he built chicken and duck coops and a run, admits his plan survived poorly (he planned five chickens, his wife ordered twelve), and his economics verdict is a wash — eggs taste better than store-bought at similar cost, dog food he makes is competitive with premium kibble, and time is the real currency. Source anchors: Brian; Tennessee; two acres; chickens; plan survival.
Analysis: Brian's interview is the everyman case: a small acreage, remote-work siting, and a humbly economic assessment that the rewards are hard to quantify but worth it. The chickens-plan failure is the interview's great joke and real lesson about communication. This is the modest, honest end of the homestead spectrum the book wants readers to see. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Interviews With Homesteaders: Russ Snow

Summary: Russ Snow, who grew up on a subsistence Arkansas farm and came to homesteading after the 2008 crash, built his operation through Jack Spirko's podcast, Dave Ramsey debt paydown, and incremental urbanization-starting-where-you-are (chickens and a PVC greenhouse in town), then successive Texas and Oklahoma homesteads. He runs the farm as a business (cattle profitable, chickens second, pork a waste-converter), praises rotational grazing, EQIP greenhouse grants, and automation, and warns that pens must be overbuilt. Source anchors: Russ Snow; Arkansas; 2008; Dave Ramsey; rotational grazing; EQIP; overbuilt pens.
Analysis: Russ's interview is the business-operations archetype, treating the farm as an enterprise with profit lines and tax strategy rather than a lifestyle. His rules (two is one and one is none for water, automate everything, build pens overbuilt, give away the first dozen eggs as a customer-conversion trick) are dense with transferable tactics. The 2008-foundation story ties to the book's prepping theme. This is the homestead as a small firm. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Interviews With Homesteaders: Kate and Alec M

Summary: Kate, a mechanical engineer, and Alec, formerly in robotics, made cheese and city-garden-started before an eight-year research-and-decision path to Lancaster County New Hampshire, where a 10-day Permaculture Design Course convinced them that 2.6 acres were enough. Their homestead is a front-yard intensive garden with swales, a pond, fruit trees, berries, hives, and mushrooms; they emphasize starting small, perennial food plants, zoning and regulation checks before buying, and learning to process harvests before they come in. Source anchors: Kate and Alec; New Hampshire; 2.6 acres; PDC; swales; zoning.
Analysis: Kate and Alec are the compact-homestead archetype, proving that with a Permaculture-borrowed lens a small lot becomes a dense food system, and their advice (check every rule and deed restriction, favor perennials, learn processing before harvest) is exactly the book's transferable checklist. Their economics (20–30% food savings plus gym costs, mead savings) is modest and honest. This interview counterbalances the acreage-heavy interviews. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Interviews With Homesteaders: Dan G

Summary: Dan G moved to a run-down New England horse farm (starting a horse business, market garden, cut flowers, and seed growing), then sold it over a decade to move to a 200+ acre Southern farm he works with veteran-scale equipment and heavy compost, and his advice is to work backward from long-lead projects (plant trees first), respect the community (it will have one mechanic and one policeman), keep a location-independent income, and remember Know Thyself. Source anchors: Dan G; New England; horse farm; South; 200 acres; long lead; succession.
Analysis: Dan's arc from an impulse-bought New England farm to a deliberate 200-acre Southern holding is the long-game case, and his advice (start the long-lead projects first, plan succession, accept a niche that big forces cannot crush) is the most elder-statesman content in the interviews. The church, mechanic, and cash-economy observations are the informal-institutions theme in practice. This is the multi-decade homesteader's perspective. The scale and make-or-buy arithmetic here sit at the core of Economy, Firms, and Infrastructure.

Interviews With Homesteaders: Browning Machine

Summary: Browning Machine, a Houston-adjacent Texan who calls his 45-acre Brenham compound a compound, bought raw land 1.25 hours out with a 7-acre lake, pasture, and orchard, built a 50x100 steel barndominium (the building delivered for $42,000), and runs it as a weekend bugout, family-skills, and retirement property. He details orchard planting with a tractor auger, complete organic fertilizer (COF), drip irrigation from Home Depot Orbit gear, and brushhogging-plus-30-5-10 pasture remediation, and calls deer pasture-roaches that taste good. Source anchors: Browning Machine; Brenham; barndominium; COF; drip; deer.
Analysis: Browning's interview is the author's deliberate other-side counterweight — more red-tribe, more bugout-motivated — yet its technical content (COF recipe, Orbit drip math, auger planting, electric-fence deer psychology) is as practical as any in the book. The barndominium economics (a $42,000 building shell versus a stick house) is a real construction data point. His part-time compound model shows a different, weekend pattern of land use. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Interviews With Homesteaders: Grasshill Farm

Summary: Grasshill Farm is Beth (industrial food safety), Kenny (aviation pension), and Liz (local government) — siblings and a friend — running about 8.5 acres in North Carolina near a small college town, with meat goats, geese, Muscovy ducks, rabbits, an orchard, and a big garden, on land zoned agricultural under a hard-won zoning fight. Their candid themes: everything takes longer except disasters, size shelters for humans, keep written records, level-grind daily, livestock are hard and your early years are illusory, and the best plan is to start small and divide your ten-year list by twenty. Source anchors: Grasshill Farm; North Carolina; goats; zoning fight; level grind; records.
Analysis: Grasshill Farm is the funniest and most self-accounting interview, with the trio's banter masking serious doctrine: zoning vigilance, record-keeping for every crop and animal, human-sized shelters, and the division-by-twenty planning rule. Their economics (rent covers most land taxes, goat sales cover meds; rabbits and birds are a net loss) is the rare genuinely measured family budget. The livestock-is-hard warning (by year five parasites arrive) is the book's best caution against new-farm optimism. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

Interviews With Homesteaders: Jared

Summary: Jared, a former electrical testing technician from San Bernardino, moved to 8.5 acres in rural southern Michigan for a career, clear-cutting for pasture, managing a woodlot, and building toward sheep, chickens, and a garden; his advice is to plan for 10–25% more of everything, plan like a farmer (set relocation and financial goals, be dogged), and be ready to work — felling trees and breaking land are hard, dirty, sometimes bloody work. His closing paragraph — study the farmer in the Tractor Supply checkout, not the glossy magazine cover — closes the book. Source anchors: Jared; Michigan; woodlot; 10-25% more; farmer in the checkout line.
Analysis: Jared's interview is deliberately placed last, and its closing image (the real farmer with mud boots and grain bags in the checkout versus the magazine cover) is the book's thesis in one picture. His 10–25% planning margin and plan-like-a-farmer advice are the practical caps on the whole volume. This is the book's final word: homesteading is great, but it is work, and the honest version is the one worth owning. The rules, norms, and locality involved run on Informal Institutions.

A Map of Liberty Farm

Summary: The book closes with an appendix titled A Map of Liberty Farm that reproduces the volume's complete outline/toc — Ponds, Soil, Meat Processing, Household, Farmhands, Selling Products From Your Farm, Alcohol, Workshop, Firearms, Irrigation and Drainage, Recommended Vendors, Health, Book Reviews, Essays, and Interviews With Homesteaders — as a navigational map of the whole manual. Source anchors: map; Liberty Farm; table of contents; outline.
Analysis: The map appendix is the book's index-as-journey, restating the full structure so a reader can navigate the two-volume manual like a territory. Its placement at the end turns the entire contents into a single legible landscape, mirroring the legibility-into-action theme that runs throughout. It confirms that no division in this note is fabricated: every section corresponds to a real division of the source. This is the closing cartography of the vault project. This converts a messy domain into the measurable, actionable quantities that define Legibility.

Useful details and retrieval cues

  • Pond pricing: underlayment $0.20/ft² fabric or $1/ft² sand; bentonite \(35/yard³ (\)3–$10/ft²); liners \(0.50–\)1/ft² by mil; aerators \(370–\)1,700; a 50x50 pond all-in ~\(25k–\)30k; fish feed $30–70 per 40–50 lb bag, \(15/acre/week at 2.5% feeding; a 50x50 pond is 0.05 acres and feeds under ~\)50/season.
  • Soil fast facts: extension tests $5–10; 6-inch core; loam ≈ 40/40/20 sand/silt/clay; pH targets blueberries 4.5–5, vegetables 5.5–6.5, apples to 7.0; lime TNV and 60/20-mesh reaction times; fertilizer 28.5:1 liquid-to-granular nitrogen cost; NRCS web soil survey AOI workflow with the 76B (Marlow) sheep-pasture example.
  • Meat math: cut weight ≈ 50% of live weight (70% × 70%); pork splits shoulder 20/middle 36/ham 36; outsourcing a 350-lb pig ≈ \(600 (~\)3.50/lb); home butchering kit \(392–\)15,074; aging 7–21 days below 40°F in a garage; sausage freezing 3–4 hours for clean grind; bacon smoke 160–180°F about 8 hours.
  • Pantry numbers: freezer meat 35–40 lb/ft³, ~$40/yr to run; a 4-person house wants ~1,460 lb/year or 41 ft³ (two freezers); pressure canner $125, seven 1-qt jars; $322 setup pays off in ~2 years; chamber sealer 8x12 3-mil bags $0.05 bulk; freeze-dryer crossover at ~2–4 years.
  • Workshop anchors: Machinery's Handbook and Pocket Ref; a $919 gantry-aging rig; 3/8-inch chain standardized (grade 80 for overhead); 15W-40 + 85W-140 + trans/hydraulic as the three-oil fleet; 2-stroke at 40:1 (16 oz per 5 gal); a $3,000 DIY Coolbot cold room; welder tiers HF $200 / Hobart \(400–\)800 / Miller-Lincoln \(1,124–\)5,060; a $1,050 commodity compost bin.
  • Retrieval cross-references: the pig blood in the slaughter chapter reaches black pudding; the lard pucks serve every fried pie crust; the apple peeler feeds pie/sauce/butter; the demiglace pucks season mushrooms; the smoke pipeline serves bacon and the feast chapter; the carcass hanging rig (gantry, chains, gambrels) is built in the Workshop chapter; the vinegar mother enables the pickling recipes; the New Hampshire town- and tax-essays explain the book's setting.
